[A front cover of a book bound in deep red (maroon) leather with a pronounced pebbled grain, likely goatskin <note>Traditionally known as "morocco" leather.</note>. Centered on the cover is a blind-stamped <note>A technique where a design is impressed into the leather without the use of gold or ink.</note> printer's mark. The device depicts a dolphin coiled around the shaft of an anchor. The letters "AL" appear to the left of the anchor, and "DUS" appear to the right. This is the celebrated trademark of the Aldine Press, founded by Aldus Manutius. The left edge shows the spine with five raised bands creating six compartments.]

->ALDUS<- <note>original: "AL DVS". The dolphin and anchor symbolize the motto "Hasten slowly" <note>original: "Festina lente"</note>, representing the ideal balance between speed (the dolphin) and stability (the anchor) in the printing process.</note>

<note>Technical Terms: 
**Aldine Press**: The Venetian printing house famous for its high-quality editions and invention of italic type.
**Aldus Manutius**: The scholar-printer who revolutionized book design.
**Printer's mark**: A logo used by early printers to identify their work.
**Morocco leather**: High-quality goatskin leather known for its durability and beautiful grain.</note>

The emblem contains the English words "**HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY**" arranged in a ring. In the center is a traditional iconographic depiction of a "**pelican in its piety**"—a pelican pecking its breast to feed its young with its own blood. This is a symbol of self-sacrifice and the "Great Work" in <term>alchemy|The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the soul</term> and <term>Rosicrucianism|A secret spiritual and cultural movement that emerged in Europe in the early 17th century</term>. The bird sits on a crescent-shaped nest atop a square pedestal. The pedestal is divided into four sections, each containing a red rose <note>The rose often symbolizes the soul or the unfolding of divine consciousness.</note>. From the top of the emblem, golden rays of light shine down upon the scene, emanating from a source that contains a small Hebrew character **Yod** <note>The letter י (Yod) is the first letter of the divine name in Hebrew and represents the spark of life or the presence of the Creator.</note>.
</image-desc>

<note>Technical Terms: 
**Marbled paper**: Paper decorated with a pattern resembling polished marble, often used for book endpapers.
**Pelican in its Piety**: In alchemy, this symbolizes the "reddening" stage (rubedo), where the philosopher's stone "feeds" the lesser metals to transform them into gold.</note>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening to the first book of iambic poetry by Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli. It establishes the central theme of the poet as a divinely inspired figure, or "Vates," who transcends the material world through virtue and the influence of the Muses.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK OF IAMBICS BY GIOVANNI AURELIO AUGURELLI.

## THAT POETS ARE INSPIRED BY DIVINE FURY AND ARE NEAR TO BEING TRULY BLESSED.

### POEM I.

*A heavenly vigor stirs the Poets from within:* <note>original: "Vates." A Latin term for a poet-prophet, suggesting their words come from a divine source rather than mere craft.</note>
*And it ever drives them further still:*
*So that it might place them there, from whence it moved them;*
<margin>c</margin> *And claim them as citizens for Heaven.*
*And so long as they dwell in the seats of mortals,*
*Forgotten in this darkness:* <note>original: "caligine." This refers to the spiritual or intellectual fog of the material world that obscures higher truths.</note>
<margin>V</margin>*irtue alone delivers them from the shadows,*
*Purer than the burning sun:*
<margin>S</margin>*plendor in which they may walk more safely:*
*And bring light to others.*
<margin>H</margin>*ence, he does not willingly apply the devouring torches*
*of honors or of wealth to his heart,*
<margin>A</margin>*s he who has once set foot with a certain rite*
*To the sacred places dedicated to the Muses.* <note>The Muses are the nine Greek goddesses who preside over the arts and sciences, providing inspiration to creators.</note>
<margin>V</margin>*itates—rather, he avoids—the sickly common crowd, and hates*
*the foolish cares of an anxious social rank.* <note>The poet argues that the pursuit of social standing and political power is a source of sickness and anxiety compared to the life of the mind.</note>
*Equally devoted to grief and to joy,*
*Held by neither, he remains unhurt.*
*He wishes often, yet does not crave; he is careful, yet*
*He does not tremble, terrified by fear.*

<summary>This opening poem introduces the Neo-Platonic concept of "divine fury," arguing that the true poet is a sacred figure whose virtue allows them to escape worldly anxieties and find heavenly light. It portrays the poet as a figure of Stoic detachment, indifferent to the common pursuit of wealth and status.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, Divine Fury, Poetry, Virtue, Muses, Humanism, Stoicism, Vates</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the first poem of Augurelli’s Iambics. Following the previous page's introduction of the "Vates" or divinely inspired poet-prophet, the text now describes the poet's spiritual and intellectual journey, contrasting the tranquility of the creative life with the hollow pursuits of the material world.</meta>

# I A M B I C S .

*Therefore he either wanders through the open regions of the World:* <note>original: "Mundi plagas." This refers to the vast reaches of the universe or the "zones" of the heavens that the poet’s mind is free to explore.</note>
  *And gathers the very flower of things:*
*While meanwhile Nature, in her many forms,*
  *is painted with learned colors.*
*Or, with tireless labor, he seeks*
  *that which is fittingly desired by all.*
*And bringing upright morals to cultivated minds,*
  *he passes by all that is unrefined.*
*Or, often admitted among the joyful choirs*
  *of maidens, he plays in secret:* <note>These "maidens" (uirginum) are a poetic reference to the Muses or the personified Virtues, who represent the highest forms of inspiration and purity.</note>
  *To whom none are permitted to approach openly:*
  *but to go secretly is granted to very few.*
*There, in the recesses of a pleasant valley,*
  *beneath a tall, leafy poplar,*
*He avoids the heat under the shade, while the breeze*
  *gently murmurs back through the leaves.*
*There, he wanders harmlessly*
  *through the flowery soil of a green field.*
*Or, at the sounding source of glassy water,* <note>original: "uitreæ lymphæ." A classical poetic description of a spring or stream so clear it resembles glass.</note>
  *he stretches out, freed from cares.*
*Or he sits in a pleasing cave, now full of God:* <note>original: "Deo plenus." This is the state of divine inspiration, where the poet is no longer acting on his own will but is filled with a higher power—the "divine breath."</note>
  *And awakens the Muse with his lyre:*
*In a cave which sacred ivy clothes all around,*
  *and which the mystical laurel overshadows:*
*From there he then sings of heroes, and of men with praises,*
  *and of the gods with worthy songs.*
*What could be more blessed than this? Who would compare*
  *all wealth to this divine madness?* <note>original: "furori." This refers to the *furor poeticus*—the "poetic frenzy" described by Plato and Renaissance Neoplatonists as a sacred state of mind that allows humans to access truths beyond ordinary reason.</note>

<meta>catchword: TO</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the nature of the poet, illustrating the "Vates" as a figure who retreats from the common world into a sacred landscape of the mind to commune with the Muses. It characterizes poetic inspiration as a "divine madness" far more valuable than any material riches.</summary>

<keywords>poetic madness, furor poeticus, Muses, divine inspiration, pastoral landscape, virtue, Vates, Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of Iambics by Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli. While the previous page focused on the spiritual retreat of the poet-vates, this page shifts to a panegyric (a poem of praise) dedicated to a member of the powerful Loredan family of Venice, using the symbol of the Phoenix to celebrate their lineage.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

# TO LORENZO LOREDAN,
## <term>Patrician of Venice</term>. The Phoenix, by which the House
### of Loredan is signified. <note>The poet uses a wordplay on the name Loredan (Latin: *Lauretanus*) and the laurel tree (*laurus*), suggesting the family is as immortal and distinguished as the sacred laurel.</note>

### POEM II.

->For a long time now, the wondrous bird has made its nest<-
<margin>m</margin> ->Beneath this ancient trunk:<-
->And from as far as the Indies and the Red Sea,<-
->it gathers miraculous scents:<- <note>In classical mythology, the Phoenix was said to build its funeral pyre with rare spices and incense from the East before being reborn from the ashes.</note>
<margin>Q</margin> ->by which it consumes itself; and, renewing itself beneath a unique<-
->and eternal Laurel, it remains:<-
<margin>I</margin> ->Just as when it adorns its feathers with fresh purple,<-
->and illuminates its crest with Gold.<-
<margin>E</margin> ->And so, against fierce birds, with Jove<-
->willing it, the bird prepares its strength.<- <note>original: "Ioue uolente." This invokes the "will of Jove" (Jupiter), the king of the gods, suggesting divine favor for the bird/family.</note>
<margin>A</margin> ->It beats its wings, and cherishing the gentle in its breast,<-
->it rouses the sluggish with its cry.<-
<margin>v</margin> ->For your House flourishes beneath the tall laurel,<-
->as if it were another perennial Phoenix.<-
<margin>Q</margin> ->Which always brings forth just men; always, for the Republic,<-
->it gives birth to those who will be the highest:<- <note>The "Republic" refers to the Republic of Venice, where the Loredan family held immense influence.</note>
<margin>H</margin> ->And it always keeps them: it always brings forth good leaders<-
->both at home and abroad.<-
<margin>C</margin> ->Just like him to whom now, with the heavens favoring,<-
->Virtue has entrusted the heavy reins of affairs:<-
<margin>S</margin> ->Under whom the wicked fear the laws of the fatherland,<-
->while the best of men rejoice in them.<-
<margin>A</margin> ->And when the fury of Mars with his unspeakable step...<- <note>The "fury of Mars" is a classical metaphor for the onset of war.</note>

<sig>a iiii</sig>
<meta>catchword: a</meta>

<summary>In this poem, Augurelli uses the myth of the Phoenix nesting in a laurel tree as an allegory for the Loredan family's longevity, rebirth, and service to the Venetian Republic. He praises the family's ability to produce virtuous leaders who uphold the law and defend the state.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo Loredan, Phoenix, House of Loredan, Venice, Republic, laurel, political virtue, Mars, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous poem praising the Loredan family and the peace brought by the Venetian Republic. It then begins Poem III, in which the author, Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, reflects on his life spent in various cities under Venetian rule—specifically Padua and Treviso—and expresses his deep loyalty and joy regarding his homeland's status within the Republic.</meta>

<header>IAMBIC VERSE.</header>

[May she] wander through the Italian cities:
From these shores gentle Peace, enriched by her daughters,
  keeps wars far away.
Truly, the just gods watch over the pious
  people under the nurturing Prince; <note>The "nurturing Prince" likely refers to the Doge of Venice, the figurehead of the Republic.</note>

## HE CONGRATULATES HIS HOMELAND, BECAUSE
## *it has come under Venetian rule.*

### POEM III.

I have spent the greatest part of my life passed 
<margin>a</margin> under golden laws.
Until, having been gifted with the City of Antenor, <note>original: "Vrbe... Antenorea." This refers to Padua, which according to legend was founded by the Trojan prince Antenor.</note>
I devoted my leisure there to letters:
Soon after in Treviso: <note>original: "Taruisina." Augurelli spent a significant portion of his later life in Treviso.</note> here too I was enriched 
  by the gift of citizenship:
Or while it delighted me at times to be an inhabitant 
  of the prince of cities. <note>The "prince of cities" is a common Renaissance epithet for Venice.</note>
Therefore, no one indeed could now know the customs 
  of this Republic, however excellent they were, 
as well as I do, nor 
  be more devoted to it,
Than my own mind always desires to obey 
  this unique state to the best of my ability.
Would anyone wonder if I now long 
  to appear more joyful than usual?
That which breathes within me longs [for it], and glowing for me...

<summary>This page features the conclusion of a poem on peace and the beginning of a poem celebrating the poet's life under Venetian governance in Padua and Treviso. Augurelli expresses his personal devotion to the Venetian Republic's laws and customs.</summary>
<keywords>Venice, Padua, Treviso, Antenor, citizenship, Republic, Peace, Golden Laws, Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli</keywords>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

*...it overflows with full joy.*
<margin>V</margin> *Shall I see you, oh sweet homeland?*
    *Shall I see you under the most holy* <note>The "most holy Senate" refers to the Venetian Senate, which the author portrays as a stabilizing and sacred governing body.</note>
<margin>S</margin> *Senate, seeing you at last enjoying*
    *a steady peace, my fatherland?*
<margin>Q</margin> *The same peace by which other cities lead their lives*
    *with eternal and blessed customs.*
<margin>R</margin> *Therefore, you shall return, noble colony*
    *of powerful Roman citizens,* <note>original: "Quiritium." This term specifically refers to the citizens of ancient Rome in their civil capacity, highlighting Rimini's heritage as a Roman colony.</note>
<margin>N</margin> *now to your former splendor; now you shall remove*
    *the filth from your honorable face.*
<margin>V</margin> *Already we seem to see the harbor properly*
    *built up with high piers.*
<margin>P</margin> *The ancient walls, collapsed on every side,*
    *restored with new stone.*
<margin>A</margin> *And the temples being raised at wondrous expense,*
    *to be dedicated to the worship of the gods.*
<margin>P</margin> *And even the private roofs of the citizens—*

<margin>C</margin> *Your fertile hills, standing out on every side,*
    *are tilled and burdened with crops.*
<margin>D</margin> *You shall be called, as you once were, the most beautiful*
    *of all the cities of Emilia, O Rimini!* <note>original: "Ariminum." Rimini is located at the terminus of the Via Aemilia, the Roman road running through the region of Emilia-Romagna.</note>

## THE BANQUET OF THE GRACES.

### POEM IV.

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic sequence of Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli. While the previous page focused on the political restoration of Rimini, this page shifts to a mythological and allegorical "Iambic" poem, continuing the acrostic pattern started previously.</meta>

# IAMBIC VERSE.

->Comus, Jest, and Cupid.<-

**D**ining together are Bacchus <note>original: "Liber." An ancient Italian deity of fertility and wine, frequently identified with the Greek Dionysus.</note> and Love; Jest
    and Revelry <note>original: "Comus." The god of festivity, riots, and nighttime dalliances.</note> sit very close by them.
**I**t is to these that the lovely Graces <note>The Charites (Aglaia, Euphrosyne, and Thalia), goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity.</note> serve 
    cups of pleasing flavor.
**S**weetly they mix them, and temper the "poisons" <note>A poetic reference to the intoxicating power of wine.</note>
    of many a vine-shoot in turn.
**I**n unusual Nectar <note>The divine drink of the gods, said to bestow immortality.</note> do these 
    drunken companions fully bathe themselves.
**A**nd by chance, being captured by an enticing sweetness,
    they gorge themselves a little more than is proper.
**H**ere, among the gods now feeling the effects of wine, there begins 
    at first a laughable little quarrel of words.
**G**rave deceptions does Bacchus charge against Love,
    which the latter frequently employs.
**I**n turn, Cupid reproaches him for his vain, playful joys
    and his slurred, stuttering speech.
**C**omus throws light laughter at Jest; and Jest, as a companion, 
    throws clumsy wit back at him.
**M**oonwards, or rather soon, the little ones fly at each other’s hair and eyes,
    and weave their hands together in a scuffle.
**A**t once the three sisters <note>The Graces, acting here as peacemakers.</note> are present for those lost in the fray,
    and these best of women urge peace.
**T**hen, at last, Bacchus secretly pounces 
    upon the arrows and the quiver of Cupid.
**A**nd with force, Jest tears the flowery crown
    from the shining hair of Comus.
**S**o then, with torn hair, Love withdrawing...

<summary>This page features a lively allegorical poem depicting a banquet where Bacchus, Cupid, Jest, and Revelry become intoxicated and engage in a playful, mythological brawl. It uses personification to explore the chaotic relationship between wine, love, and humor.</summary>

<keywords>Bacchus, Cupid, Comus, Graces, mythology, allegory, banquet, wine, iambic poetry, Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the mythological narrative poem from Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli’s *Chrysopoeia* (The Art of Making Gold). The previous page described a celestial dinner party where Bacchus (Wine), Cupid (Love), Comus (Revelry), and Jest fell into a drunken brawl. It ended with Bacchus attempting to seize Cupid’s weapons. This page resolves that scene with an encounter between Cupid and his mother, Venus, before transitioning to a new poem addressed to the Venetian humanist Vincenzo Quirini.</meta>

# BOOK I

He encounters his mother while drunk.

<margin>C</margin> **C**omus <note>The god of festivity, nighttime revels, and excess.</note> rushes off to join witty men
Through a winding alleyway.
<margin>B</margin> **B**acchus <note>original: "Liber." An ancient Italian deity of fertility and wine, frequently identified with the Greek Dionysus.</note> and Jest strike a pact—a *brief* one,
Soon to end in time.
<margin>A</margin> **A**s Venus <note>The goddess of love and beauty.</note> approaches, seeing her son
Filthy and soaked with unmixed wine:
<margin>I</margin> **I**ndignant with herself, she grabs his hand
And drags the clinging boy to a clear fountain.
<margin>H</margin> **H**ere, she immediately washes the loose hair
Of his sacred head with pure water.
<margin>P</margin> **P**recisely she combs and with effort untangles it,
Arranging hanging locks around his shining neck.
<margin>S</margin> **S**uddenly, as her hand wanders, she touches
His dewy skin with the sharp comb.
<margin>T</margin> **T**hen, feeling the sting, he thrusts a lit torch
Into his mother’s devoted breast.
<margin>A</margin> **A**flame, she leaps into the waves just as she was,
Dragging Cupid <note>The god of desire, often depicted as a winged boy.</note> along with her.
<margin>F</margin> **F**inally, a single fountain frees them both,
Soothed by the cooling of their different heats. <note>A poetic paradox where the water extinguishes both the literal fire of Cupid's torch and the metaphorical "heat" of his drunkenness and her anger.</note>

---

## TO VINCENZO QUIRINI <note>Vincenzo Quirini (c. 1479–1514) was a prominent Venetian nobleman, diplomat, and humanist who eventually joined the Camaldolese order as a hermit.</note>
## THE GOLDEN FLEECE <note>original: "Vellus Aureum." While referencing the Greek myth of Jason, in this context it serves as an alchemical allegory for the process of extracting or creating gold.</note>

### SONG V

<margin>๏</margin> The Shepherd, leading him out after leaving the flock... <note>The "Shepherd" here likely begins an allegorical journey or represents a teacher/guide in the alchemical or spiritual sense.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a mythological acrostic poem about a drunken Cupid being bathed by Venus and introduces a new poem, "The Golden Fleece," dedicated to the humanist Vincenzo Quirini. The imagery shifts from a playful divine comedy to the start of a more serious, perhaps alchemical, pastoral allegory.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical allegory from Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli’s *Chrysopoeia*. The previous page concluded a mythological scene involving Venus and Cupid, which served as a transition into this section. This page introduces "Alcon," a shepherd figure who represents the alchemist, and describes the symbolic transformation of a sheep's fleece through various colors—a direct reference to the stages of the alchemical "Great Work" (the creation of the Philosopher's Stone).</meta>

# IAMBIC METER <note>original: "IAMBICVS." This refers to the poetic meter used in this section, often associated with directness or satire, though here used for narrative allegory.</note>

-> Alcon <note>A name from classical pastoral poetry, used here to represent the alchemist.</note> places [the beast] within a cave. <-
**I**t is there, diligent, that he feeds her alone with cut
-> Herbs, and from drawn springs. <-
**N**owhere does he allow her to wander under the open sky,
-> But keeps her confined in a narrow enclosure, <note>This "narrow enclosure" represents the alchemical vessel or furnace where the substance is contained.</note> <-
**C**ontinuing until she who was shining with a white fleece,
-> Is covered over with a dark color. <note>The *nigredo* or "blackening" stage, the first step in the alchemical process representing decay and the stripping of old forms.</note> <-
**D**irectly after, she is more often sprinkled with green,
-> Or blue, and even with redness. <note>The *viriditas* (greenness) and *rubedo* (reddening) stages, indicating chemical reactions and the approach of the final perfection.</note> <-
**T**hen, at last, restored to her former covering,
-> She begins to grow white once again. <note>The *albedo* or "whitening" stage, representing purification.</note> <-
**S**lowly she comes forth so much that, to those watching,
-> She takes away the sharpness of their sight. <note>Suggesting a brilliant, blinding light or purity.</note> <-
**T**hen the Shepherd supplies more bountifully to her
-> Fodder sought from everywhere. <-
**A**nd she herself—which soon would be a wonder to all,
-> Whoever lives near <-
**S**ile’s <note>The Sile is a river in the Veneto region of Italy, near where the author lived.</note> flowing streams—immediately filled with the rich
-> Fattening of joyful grass, <-
**I**nstantaneously changes her remarkable hide, and for her, everywhere,
-> Her fleece shines with gold. <note>The final stage of the work: the successful transmutation into gold.</note> <-
**A**nd with gold her teeth, and her tiny horns,
-> And her small hooves gleam. <-
**S**natched away at last by the fate of a noble beast,
-> While breathing, she even stores away gold. <note>The death of the "beast" signifies the final fixing of the alchemical stone.</note> <-
**A**nd happy Alcon strives to carry the weight of his wealthy flock
-> Back to his home. <-
**F**inally, the Shepherd-Goldsmith <note>original: "Pastor Aurifex." This title explicitly links the pastoral imagery of the shepherd (Alcon) with the technical reality of the alchemist (the goldsmith).</note> celebrates a feast day.

<summary>This page uses the pastoral allegory of a shepherd named Alcon tending a sheep to describe the alchemical process of transmutation. The sheep's fleece changes from black to green, red, white, and finally gold, mirroring the chemical stages required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Alcon, alchemy, Great Work, transmutation, golden fleece, Sile river, Pastor Aurifex, nigredo, albedo, rubedo</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific alchemical allegory of Alcon the shepherd (described on the previous page) to a more philosophical and personal address by the author, Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, to his friend Giovanni Quirini. The author discusses the nature of poetic inspiration and the tendency of readers to find hidden meanings—specifically alchemical ones—within "slight" or "pure" fables.</meta>

# BOOK I

And he gives thanks to the gods.
We have composed a pure fable, <note>original: "fabulam." Here, a poetic story or allegory.</note> Quirinus, <note>Giovanni Quirini, a Venetian nobleman and poet to whom Augurelli dedicated this work.</note>
This one which we now send to you.
There are some who think, however, that something lies beneath it,
Which perhaps I myself have not noticed.
From this, those foolish fictions often arise,
And long commentaries.
Do you yourself desist: nor, as you are a most serious
Examiner <note>original: "Scrutator." A scholar or investigator who looks for deep truths.</note> of all things,
Should you wish anything to be unearthed from these slight things,
Which does not actually lie hidden beneath.
They say that some things have slipped from the ancient poets <note>original: "vatibus." Specifically "vates," which means both "poet" and "prophet," suggesting divinely inspired creators.</note>
When they were stirred by divine frenzy:
And that those few things were truly known by them:
For they remain hidden/abstruse,
Unless someone leads them out from the inner sanctuaries <note>original: "penetralibus." The most sacred, private rooms of a temple; here used to mean the deepest secrets of the heart or mind.</note>
And makes them clear for posterity.
Which I would hardly approve: Antiquity <note>original: "Antiquitas." The authority of the ancient world.</note> has
Only as much power as the faith we give to its words.
But I would say that the poets nonetheless are stirred,
And carried by the impulse of the mind:
Whether that comes forth from an internally moving cause,
Or by the favor of heaven;

<summary>Augurelli addresses his friend Quirinus, cautioning him not to over-analyze the "slight" poem for hidden meanings, while acknowledging the ancient belief that poets (vates) are divinely inspired to speak truths they may not fully realize.</summary>
<keywords>Quirinus, fable, poetic inspiration, divine frenzy, antiquity, commentary, hidden meaning, vates</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of poetry by Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli. While the previous page focused on philosophical and alchemical allegories addressed to Giovanni Quirini, this page shifts to a more courtly and aesthetic subject: the praise of a fine piece of needlework. The author moves from the "slight fables" of alchemy to the literal "slight threads" of embroidery, yet maintains the same theme of hidden skill and divine inspiration.</meta>

IAMBIC VERSE.

# COMMENDATION TO PEROLLA
## Of a Linen Cloth most elegantly embroidered with a needle.

->POEM VI.<-

It was my fortune to see a wonderful work
Produced now by the art of a new Pallas: <note>original: "Palladis." Pallas Athena was the Greek goddess of wisdom and handicrafts, especially weaving and embroidery.</note>
Such as Nature herself or a powerful Painter might provide:
Which represented a joyful and fertile little garden,
Emulating the colors of flowery spring.
Neither the panels of Apelles <note>Apelles of Kos (4th century BCE) was considered the greatest painter of antiquity.</note> had so moved
The minds of those watching: nor Myron’s bronze <note>Myron of Eleutherae was a famous Greek sculptor.</note>
Heifer, <note>original: "Bucula." Myron’s bronze heifer was so lifelike that ancient poets claimed it deceived real bulls and herdsmen.</note> which, as if she were alive and hidden among the bulls,
Had thus deceived the flock, and the leader of the flock:
Nor had Praxitelean <note>Praxiteles was a renowned sculptor known for the grace and lifelike quality of his marble statues.</note> marble, polished into the sacred
Faces of the Gods, turned those looking upon it
Toward itself with such power: as Perolla’s silk
Gift, sent here to her Iphis, <note>Iphis and Perolla appear to be the names of the lovers or friends involved in this gift exchange; Iphis is a name drawn from classical mythology.</note> moved me:
So much does excellence prevail in all things,
And win favor for itself, and drawing with it
The minds of those admiring, it binds them powerfully.
Now indeed, what an elegant hand can do with a needle
Seems rendered in the narrow fold of the fabric,
Where on the grassy soil of the green earth
Shrubs sprout with flowering branches.
One may also see small animals
Stealthily entering hidden recesses here and there,
And coming out again from their thick hiding-places,

<summary>This poem praises a highly detailed piece of silk embroidery (needlework) gifted by Perolla to Iphis, comparing its realism to the legendary works of ancient Greek masters like Apelles and Myron. The author marvels at the "new Pallas" whose needle creates a lifelike garden filled with flowering shrubs and tiny animals.</summary>
<keywords>embroidery, needlework, art criticism, Pallas Athena, Apelles, Myron, Praxiteles, Perolla, Iphis, nature in art</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Perolla’s embroidery from the previous page, concluding Poem VI with a vivid description of the natural scenes depicted in the needlework. It then begins Poem VII, which shifts the dedication to Francesco Cornelio (Francis Cornaro), a member of a noble Venetian family, using a humble "modesty topically" where the poet claims his small verses cannot contain the greatness of his subject.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

*And meadows are traversed with mutual course,* <note>This likely refers to the "courses" or paths of the threads in the embroidery crossing one another.</note>
*As a stream falling from a high rock flees*
*Among little stones and the grass springing up,*
*And the green banks of the clear fountain [shimmer] with moss:*
*And how songbirds perch in the brambles:*
*And how glassy fish swim under the waves.*
*Even the insects, about to perform the parts*
*Of their duty, as they cling, run about, creep, and fly.*
*And everything you see depicted here laughs.* <note>original: "ridet." In Latin poetry, nature "laughing" implies it is blooming, bright, or thriving.</note>
*But now a limit must be set for the wandering Muse,*
*Lest I seem imprudent to strive with you in any way*
*In vain with these small verses,*
*Perolla, the sweet glory of our age;*

---

## TO FRANCESCO CORNELIO,
## VENETIAN PATRICIAN.

### POEM VII.

*You who lead your lineage from the Cornelian clan,* <note>The Cornelii were one of the most distinguished families of ancient Rome; the Venetian Cornaro family claimed descent from them.</note>
<margin>c</margin> *Famous, ancient, and wealthy,*
*Francesco, I would wish for you also to hold a place*
*Even the first place in our iambics:* <note>Iambics are a specific meter of poetry, often used for personal addresses or conversational styles.</note>
<margin>S</margin> *But no one has ever tried to enclose a deep spring*
*Within a small vessel.*
<margin>N</margin> *Nor would I myself hope to be able [to capture]*
*The divine things pertaining to you in a tiny poem.*
<margin>V</margin> *See what the error of a diligent poet can do:*

<summary>This page concludes a poetic tribute to the needlework of Perolla, describing its lifelike depictions of nature, and begins a new dedication to the Venetian nobleman Francesco Cornelio, utilizing a classical metaphor about the difficulty of capturing greatness in small verse.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page concludes Poem VII, dedicated to the Venetian nobleman Francesco Cornelio (Francis Cornaro), and begins Poem VIII, addressed to Andrio Artusio of Ravenna. It continues the poet's use of iambic meter—a rhythm traditionally used for more personal or direct communication.</meta>

<header>BOOK I. — IAMBIC.</header>
<page-num>II</page-num>

So that it <note>The poet’s "vessel" or "song" mentioned on the previous page.</note> might stretch toward that place where it cannot reach.
Should I bring forth with a **thin lyre** <note>original: "tenui... Lyra." A common poetic trope where the author humbly describes his instrument as "thin" or "weak" compared to the greatness of his subject.</note>
The riches, the men, and the proclamations of an ancient house?
These many things must be spoken with a greater power
Of speech, or of a lofty song.
It is enough for a friend as small as I
To report only this, which he can:
Namely, that the fountain of honorable offspring always
Gushes forth, most fertile with men:
Among whom you have grown up, flourishing in an
Ample succession of honor and affairs.
And just as these men honor you, you yourself shine back
As a splendid glory to them in return;

# TO ANDRIO ARTUSIO
# OF RAVENNA.

## A POEM COMPOSED OF IAMBICS ALONE.
### POEM VIII.

Go, my **iambic** <note>The poet personifies the meter of the poem itself, sending it as a messenger.</note>, as quickly as you can:
<margin>i</margin> And climb the high mouth of the **king of rivers**: <note>original: "regis... fluminum." This refers to the Po River, which ancient poets like Virgil called the king of rivers; it flows near the city of Ravenna.</note>
Where an ancient city will meet you,
The pleasant, sweet, and blessed fatherland
Of my best companion, Andrio.
There, first seek him with many greetings,
And then say that we are well.
He will perhaps ask what I have been doing

<meta>This page continues Poem VIII addressed to Andrio Artusio. It completes the sentence started on the previous page regarding the poet's current activities and shifts to inquiries about Artusio’s own life, family, and public service.</meta>

<header>LIB. I.</header>

For a long time? Or what work I do often? <note>This completes the question from the previous page: "He will perhaps ask what I have been doing for a long time..."</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But report that I myself live with a lot [of fortune],
Which the kind **stars** <term>sydera: often used in Latin to denote destiny or divine influence through astrology</term> gave to me,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Or which a balanced Mind prepared, so that I desire
Nothing in excess, and a brief abundance from the
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Gushing mouth of a spring** <note>original: "scatente fontis ore." An image of poetic inspiration or modest satisfaction.</note> provides enough for my use.
And how the **Muse** <term>Camœna: a Roman deity of song and poetry, often identified with the Greek Muses</term> sings melodies for me:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In which she seems to want to bring playful things
To the serious ones, and no less willingly
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To temper **bitter things** <note>original: "amarulenta."</note> with sweet ones.
And my life, as it usually is, remains
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Always modest, yet filled with
All kinds of jokes, except that we are
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Far from **desire** <note>original: "cupidine." Here, likely referring to romantic or erotic passion.</note>, which for a long time
Used to strike my sick heart with a light,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Frequent, but not heavy, torch.
But having said these things first, ask afterwards
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What he is gaining through his **affairs** <note>original: "negociis." Referring to his professional or public duties.</note>:
Many of which are honorable and just, as becomes a man,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He performs them frequently, and thereupon brings forth
A splendid glory for himself and for the **Republic**. <note>original: "Reiq; Publicæ." In this period, this refers to the state or the common good, likely the Venetian state or civil life.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Likewise, how he fares as a Father with his offspring:
And how the whole house is. In the meantime, [see] that
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He does not allow his letters to perish in squalid, rotten neglect.
But he, nonetheless, with a mind stirred by God,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Often gave many honorable things to his **Graces** <term>Gratiis: the Charites, three goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity</term>
With **Apollo** <note>The god of the sun, music, and poetry.</note> favoring the Graces;

<summary>The poet describes his modest but contented life of poetry and "balanced mind," free from the fires of passion. He then directs his iambic verse to inquire about Andrio Artusio's professional success, his family's health, and his dedication to literature.</summary>
<keywords>Andrio Artusio, contentment, Muse (Camena), Apollo, Graces, Republic, fatherhood, poetic inspiration, Ravenna</keywords>

->IAMBICS<-

## TO C., RECENTLY INITIATED.
<note>original: "AD . C . NVPER INITIATAM ." This refers to a young woman who has recently "taken the veil" or entered a convent to become a nun.</note>

### POEM IX.

Where do you go, O virgin, most beautiful of all?
<margin>q</margin> Where do you withdraw yourself, poor little girl?
<margin>V</margin> Where do you hide such great beauty so suddenly,
As if you had been **driven mad** <term>vecors: from 've-' (without) and 'cors' (mind/heart), implying she has lost her reason by choosing the cloister</term>?
<margin>H</margin> Alas, where do you flee so suddenly? Where do you steal yourself
From your own self? You are unaware of the evils
<margin>Q</margin> Which will seek you out, which will tear you apart, and which will
Rush upon you from all sides like an organized army.
<margin>V</margin> Where do you go, impiously scorning your parents
Whom you leave behind, exhausted by heavy grief?
<margin>Q</margin> To them, you alone were their heart, their one spirit,
And you were absolutely everything to them.
<margin>H</margin> Could a wicked, **hostile hand** <note>The poet uses the language of warfare and kidnapping ("hostilis manus") to describe her voluntary entry into the religious life.</note>
Ever perpetrate anything more cruel?
<margin>Q</margin> Than if it snatched a daughter from the fostering breast
Of her mother, or from the embrace of her father?
<margin>O</margin> Foolish girl, you despise such great wealth of a rich house,
And such great expenses.
<margin>O</margin> The finery that suited your tender years, your noble birth,
And your own self, is now scorned.
<margin>Q</margin> Alas, how sad all the youth are left behind,
But more sad than all the rest,
<margin>A</margin> If there is any boy in the flower of his age
Who had ardently desired **marriage** <term>nuptias: a legal wedding; the poet frames her religious devotion as a rejection of her social and domestic duties</term> with you:

<meta>catchword: Qui</meta>

<summary>This poem is a lament and a rebuke addressed to a beautiful young woman who has chosen to enter a convent, portraying her religious devotion as an act of madness that abandons her grieving parents and potential suitors.</summary>
<keywords>nun, convent, initiation, parents, grief, beauty, marriage, iambic poetry</keywords>

# A RESPONSE ON BEHALF OF C.

## POEM X.

<margin>E</margin>Why should it please the <term>poet: the Latin "vates" implies both a poet and a prophet or diviner</term> to harass a pious girl in so many impious ways?
<margin>V</margin>She who immediately and constantly committed herself to the one greatest 
<margin>Q</margin>Prince of the world <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, often styled as the Prince of Heaven in religious poetry.</note>: 
<margin>C</margin>From whom she had learned that all these "blessed" things you boast of 
<margin>E</margin>would soon be **fleeting** <term>caduca: literally "falling" or "perishable," used here to describe worldly goods</term>:
<margin>B</margin>But on the contrary, everything she follows is firm,
<margin>A</margin>And unharmed by any passing time.
<margin>▴</margin>Those things you gather as evidence are indeed "good" things,
<margin>S</margin>And absolutely convenient for this life:
But they are entangled everywhere with many evils;
They have more **gall** and less **honey** <note>original: "Plus fellis et mellis minus." This is a common Latin idiom meaning more bitterness than sweetness.</note>:
<margin>S</margin>Nor do they persist in a single state;
<margin>H</margin>Having barely begun, they cease to be.
<margin>N</margin>Nor does she, by thus deserting her dear parents,
Wander as an impious girl, as you believe.
<margin>H</margin>For she embraces them with as much **love** <term>caritate: while "caritas" is the root of "charity," it here refers to the deep, selfless love between a child and parent</term>
As is fitting for a daughter.
<margin>N</margin>But she places before them the name of the best
Parent of all things <note>God, the "optimus rerum parens."</note>.
<margin>F</margin>And she values Him alone as the greatest, and before the only...

# RESPONSE ON BEHALF OF C.

## POEM X.

*What joy did it bring the poet to harass* <note>original: "entare," likely a variant of "tentare," meaning to test or agitate</note> *a pious*
*girl in so many impious ways?*
*She who immediately and constantly vowed herself*
*to the one greatest Prince of the world* <note>A reference to God or Christ</note>:
*From which she knew that all these things you boast of as being blessed*
*would, in a short time, fall away* <term>caduca: literally "falling" or "perishable," referring to the transitory nature of worldly goods</term>:
*But on the contrary, all the things she pursues are firm:*
*And harmed by no length of time.*
*Those things which you gather are indeed good things:*
*And entirely the comforts of life:*
*but being entangled everywhere with many evils*
*They have more gall* <note>original: "fellis," symbolizing bitterness</note> *and less honey:*
*Nor do they persist in a single state:*
*But having barely begun, they cease.*
*Nor does she, in thus deserting her dear parents*
*as you think, wander as one impious.*
*For she embraces them with as much affection*
*as is fitting for a daughter's greatness.*
*But she places before them the name of the best*
*Parent of all things* <note>God, as the ultimate creator</note>.
*And she considers that alone of greatest value, and above the only—*

IAMBIC POETRY

She considers everything else as nothing.
<margin>E</margin>Therefore, let her parents consider that she, who was taken from them,
Has been returned to her true Parent <note>Referring to God as the ultimate father of the soul</note>.
<margin>P</margin>Indeed, let the stricken youth finally set a limit
To their sad tears.
<margin>Q</margin>For what envy should be felt for mortals who are cast aside,
Now that the maiden has wed a god? <note>original: "nupsisse diuo uirginem"; this refers to the theological concept of a nun as a "Bride of Christ."</note>

# TO BERTUTIUS LAMBERTUS:

## POEM XI.

<margin>S</margin>If ever you lighten the heavy cares of your sacred breast
With honorable play:
Lambert, do not altogether despise these jests <term>iocos: a standard term in Latin poetry for light-hearted, lyrical, or erotic verses</term>
Which we now send to you.
<margin>F</margin>You will see Faunus following the marriageable Lyce
Through all the pathless places:
Wherever she flings herself headlong,
And puts off the sweet marriage.
<margin>L</margin>You will read how Orus, as a fervent lover,
Desires another Lyce for himself.
<margin>V</margin>How Flora and Alcon love each other mutually,
And bless themselves with equal affection.
<margin>H</margin>You seemed to advise me of these things long ago—

BOOK I.

*Jesting, so that I might write to you—*
*You who, while mixing serious matters with jests,*
*Season the bitter with the sweet;* <note>original: "Condis amara dulcibus"; a common poetic trope suggesting that difficult truths are easier to hear when wrapped in pleasant poetry.</note>

## POEM XII.

Faunus, captured by a love for the marriageable Lyce, <note>Faunus was a rustic Italian god of the forest and fields. Lyce is a name used by the poet Horace for an elusive or cold mistress.</note>

### HORUS TO LYCE.

## POEM XIII.

<margin>t</margin>
I shall hold you now and enjoy my desire,
O Lyce, so long longed-for by me:
Present, I will gaze at you, and I will endure
the striking of those eyes that rival the stars, at close quarters.
I will cover your tender mouth with kisses,
pressed softly by my honey-flowing lips.
Behold, you will now be permitted to drink in
the pleasing voices of your lover with eager ears.

But I, happy and blessed, shall hear
at close quarters the sweet murmur of a sounding kiss.
You will tell me how you value me alone most highly,
and how all things now seem worthless to you compared to me;
how you tend and adorn yourself entirely for me alone,
and always desire to be able to please me.
But I, in turn, say that I would consider myself wretched
unless I am always with you in person.
And so that I may be near, and carried nowhere else,
I would desire to face and endure every horror,
and strive to live with you in every way,
and be with you at least in mind, if it is not granted in reality.
O how blessed am I, and you, Lyce, how satisfied,
because we have thus come together so fortunately as one;

## POEM XV.

This small panel <note>original: "Abella", likely a printer's error for "Tabella" (a small panel or painting), which matches the "tabulae" mentioned in the heading.</note> dedicated to divine Pallas <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena, patron of wisdom and the arts.</note>

coalesce fittingly and bring forth light together.
A certain praise is owed to these men, and perhaps
both will one day live through our song.

BOOK I.

But you, Avogadro <note>The Avogadro family were prominent patrons of the arts and humanists in Treviso.</note>, who strive here to contend among the greatest in turn, and to undergo the keen judgment of observers with your own distinct virtue, you shall not be cheated of your own due praise. To excel in such wondrous arts is no small thing: just as this man is capable in the painter’s art, and that one in the woodworker’s. But to favor these men, and to nourish them, is a splendid and magnificent office. Indeed, it is so much the greater, as these things are seen by so few to be worthy of any favor at all.

## POEM XVI.

Silenus, for when sweet Euhyus <note>A cult name for Bacchus, the god of wine, referring to the "Euhoe" cry of his followers.</note> has begun to swell your veins, you perceive all hidden secrets in a clear light, and of your own accord you foretell the things you have seen. Tell me, I pray, what cause so stirs the empty minds of mortals, that no one is willing to endure his own lot, but each utterly disapproves of it? <note>This is a classic Horace-inspired theme regarding human discontent with one's own station in life (mercurial nature).</note> These things I asked; when the God, having moved the drinking cup <note>original: "cantharo", a large, deep drinking vessel with handles, typical of Dionysian imagery.</note> from his lips, replied to me in turn...

**D**o not seek what mortal power can never
      Attain by any search.
**S**o it is; so it once seemed best to supreme Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note>,
      That he had turned Gold into bronze,
**A**nd then defiled the ages with iron,
      Which were once blessed and golden. <note>This refers to the "Ages of Man," a common mythological theme where humanity declines from a virtuous Golden Age to a corrupt Iron Age.</note>
**H**aving spoken these things, he <note>Silenus.</note> returns to his task,

**B**ut you, Paolo <note>Paolo Canale.</note>, do not think however through all things
      That this extends everywhere,
**S**o that no one, by chance content with his own lot,
      Could ever live a blessed life.
**R**ather, consider those whom you see gaping
      Always for these honors,
**W**hich the breeze of the common crowd <note>original: "Aura vulgi"; a metaphor for the fickle and fleeting nature of political popularity.</note> fans, or for those things
      Which Fortune alone grants,
**T**hat they live neither content by any chance,
      Nor live in any way blessed.
**B**ut rather, that there are many things which can spread
      A grim poison through their limbs.
**B**ut he who, having left anxious cares far behind,
      Prolongs his life in quiet,
**O**r he who, pleasing in the bosom of his girl,
      Is scorched by the fire of love,
**O**r he who is led by the frenzy of Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of music and prophecy, who inspires "divine madness" in poets.</note>
      Amidst the sacred dances of the nine sisters <note>The Muses.</note>,
**U**pon all these the pure light of the mind shines back,
      And the cloudy air gives way.

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem addressed to Angelo Gabriele, which began on the previous page with an allusion to Catullus’s famous "Vivamus mea Lesbia." The poet now transitions from an invitation to love into a philosophical defense of Love (Cupid) as a cosmic force essential for life itself.</meta>

# IAMBIC POEM

When Catullus, the best of poets, spoke, <note>A reference to Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BC), whose poetry often focused on the intensity of personal emotion and the necessity of living for the moment.</note>
He did not speak to one person alone, but, as I believe, to everyone.
Life is to be preferred over every individual thing,
And yet, we always lose it willingly:
And we pierce our wretched life with a thousand darts.
It is fitting for everyone to love and to be loved,
For although all things beneath the course of the stars
Persist through love, and slip away through discord:
We nevertheless provoke Love with bitter voices.
And we call desire <note>original: "cupidinem"</note> now impudent, now bold, now heavy,
And often we call it vain:
As if this desire were a burden to mortals.
But if that desire did not exist, what good would remain,
To further lead back scattered things into a single whole?
To what origin would Nature grant existence?
How could opposites cling to one another in turn,
And living things emerge from those bonds,
Unless the force of hidden Love provided it? <note>The poet is drawing on the ancient philosophical idea that Love (Eros) is the "glue" of the universe that holds disparate elements together.</note>
So it is: we ourselves are the cause of our own misfortunes;
We fill our lives with ruined passions,
Either those which a foolish ambition for honor brings,
Or those which the greedy hope for petty profit <term>Lucelli</term> <note>A diminutive form of "lucrum" (profit), implying a tawdry or insignificant financial gain.</note> procures.
But how we drive away the rebellious and "wicked"
Affection of Cupid the Liberator, <note>original: "Liberi Cupidinis"</note>
Which alone, perhaps, comes to us from heaven.
Hence it is, Gabriele, that you frequently and no less
Wisely marvel that there are so very few,
Who wish to enjoy life well and blessedly.

<summary>The poet argues that while humans often blame "Love" or "Desire" for their woes, it is actually the vital force that binds the universe together. He concludes that true misery comes from greed and ambition, not from the divine gift of love.</summary>
<keywords>Catullus, Cupid, Angelo Gabriele, Cosmic Love, Philosophy of Nature, Ambition, Human Nature, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>catchword: j</meta>

And this seems to look especially toward this point:
That just as you speak and act skillfully in other matters,
Proving it charmingly among your companions
And as if encouraging them, so you do quite often
And elegantly with the mouth of the pure poet:
"Let us live," you say, "and let us love, my Lesbia;" <note>original: "Vivamus, inquis, ac amemus Lesbia." A direct quotation of the opening line of Catullus 5, one of the most famous poems of Roman antiquity, urging lovers to ignore the gossip of the world and cherish their time together.</note>

### TO SEBASTIAN MEDULO
### of Treviso, that there is neither
### greater loyalty, nor love 
### firmer, than that of a wife.

## POEM XVIII.

Do not think, Medulo, that anything sweeter
Or dearer could happen to you,
Than if a faithful maiden should respond
With mutual love—she who has captured you
With the bonds of her charming character and rare beauty.
But no greater loyalty exists or is evident,
Nor is any love firmer, than that of a wife,
Who, scarcely grown, leaves her father’s house,
And lives happily in the dear embrace of her husband.
For Venus herself fans this torch; <note>In Roman mythology, Venus is the goddess of love; the "torch" refers to the wedding torch (*taeda*), symbolizing the heat and light of a legitimate marriage.</note>
She always nourishes it so that it may live and grow.
Oh, what could anyone ever choose that is more blessed?
Than to successfully possess the thing he loves
In such a way that he may gaze upon her from nearby, and with a kiss—

<summary>This page concludes a poem referencing Catullus's famous call to love and begins a new ode celebrating the superior stability and devotion found in marriage compared to other forms of affection.</summary>
<keywords>Catullus, Lesbia, Sebastian Medulo, Treviso, marriage, Venus, fidelity, Roman poetry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poem to Sebastian Medulo, transitioning from the initial attraction to the joys of marriage and family life. It then introduces a new poem addressed to the humanist Girolamo Bologni (Hieronymus Bononius) regarding a lighthearted journey.</meta>

IAMBIC METERS.

May she freely allow him to offer an embrace,
And may it be right for her to recline in a single soft bed.
What if, witty, she delights you with sweet jests,
And lifts you when you are down, and lightens you when you are sad?
And soon makes you a father with noble offspring,
While your children play about with lisping voices? <note>The Latin *blaesa voce* refers to the charming, "lisping" or "stuttering" speech of young children learning to talk.</note>
Nor would she harm your literary studies at all,
In any way—despite what some might think. <note>A common trope in Renaissance humanist literature was the debate over whether a wife hindered or helped a scholar's work; the poet here argues she is an asset.</note>
But she alone, by taking away domestic cares,
Will lead you through those studies with more fire.
She will immediately consider you her second self,
Just as you will consider her to be your other half:
Indeed, she and you, though two, now live as one,
Living under the favor of a single spirit;

### TO HIERONYMUS BONONIUS 
### OF TREVISO, <note>original: "TAR·" for *Tarvisinus*, referring to the city of Treviso.</note> A DESCRIPTION
### OF A PLAYFUL JOURNEY.

#### POEM XIX.

How much that joyful day delighted me, Bononius,
As well as our excellent companions—a day which shone
Brightly for me and my friends recently.
If you have any leisure for these playful verses,
Which were produced almost out of the effort of laughter,
You will know: and perhaps you will rejoice just as much
As if you had joined us on this journey as a companion.

<meta>This page continues the humorous poem to Hieronymus Bononius (Girolamo Bologni), following the introductory lines where the poet describes his delight in a recent journey with friends.</meta>

# BOOK I.

*While we were returning from the country estate of Marcellus,*
*where we had spent two days in the most lavish style,*
*the four of us, full of wit, climbed into the carriage.*
*For Marcellus—though his body was a bit frail,*
*his mind was perhaps too sharp—*
*and as one who often provoked me with his jokes,*
*had seated himself in his place first.*
*I sat down second, modestly taking the spot to his right,*
*so that he would have a better chance*
*to needle me from such close proximity.*
*But on the other side of the carriage, Advogarius*
*and Pancratius—both highly learned lawyers <note>original: *Iureconsultissimi*, a title for those most expert in the law.</note>—*
*pressed side-by-side; neither one*
*would give way to the other, behaving just as if*
*they were diligently representing their clients in court.*
*They began to argue intensely between themselves*
*about the "laws" of our seating: what share of the space each held,*
*or the division of offspring, and even what the term "Eraum" <note>This likely refers to *Erctum*, from the legal phrase *erctum non cito*, involving the division of an inheritance among co-heirs. The lawyers are jokingly applying complex property law to their cramped seating in the carriage.</note>*
*signified, as they made a show of speaking with legal elegance.*
*We all burst out laughing. Meanwhile, the clever*
*Marcellus, acting as a judge who settles a funny dispute*
*with a fresh joke, began with a grin:*
*"You see how clear and calm everything is right now;"*
*"yet that noble prophet <note>original: *Vates*, a term for a poet-prophet; here likely referring to a contemporary weather forecaster or an almanac.</note> had predicted*
*that heavy, unending rains were coming."*

# IAMBIC 1. BOOK 1

...from where he would be thrown headlong into the waves from such heights, the ropes having been suddenly cut. Laughter poured out even more freely following this; for so often had these men facetiously mocked the predictions of the seers <note>original: *vatum*, referring to poet-prophets or astrologers who interpreted the stars.</note> who observe the stars. 

But I, in response, contended that the storm would arrive the day after tomorrow, having prophesied it with true omens. And so that I might return like for like, I said: "Hey you, why is your health treated with such negligence? Why sleep with the windows wide open now, and pass your time in a bedroom stuffed with sour apples <note>original: *malis acidis*. Sour apples were sometimes used in Renaissance medicine for their scent or as a dietary aid to balance "humors," though here it may be part of the poet's friendly ribbing about Marcellus's health.</note>, even while the stars threaten imminent and accursed storms of rain?" 

What more can I say? You would have said we were dying of laughter; so nearly did it seem that everything we spoke of was nothing but trifles. Then, each of us happy together, we began to sing the highest praises of the greatest Jupiter <note>The king of the gods, here used in a classical poetic style to give thanks for a safe and pleasant journey.</note> and the other inhabitants of heaven who must not be kept silent. We gave them manifold thanks that we were permitted to return home safe and sound, filled now with country humor. 

From here, it is a pleasure to recount what we did during our stay in the country, and likewise what we said while reading—things which, perhaps, ought not to be entirely neglected. For instance, how Alleotus <note>A companion of the poet, likely a member of the same humanist circle in Treviso or Venice.</note>, the best of companions, came to our aid with more than a hundred humorous jests, and afterwards gladly led us on...

->visum<-

### BOOK 1

...the citron grove <note>original: *Citriaria*. This refers to a garden or orchard of citron or lemon trees, which were exotic and highly prized in Renaissance Italy for their scent and "golden" fruit.</note> seen with its green foliage,
Which the wonderful cultivator himself compelled
To exist under a sky that is certainly not suitable
For growing them: but it is said that "labor conquers all" <note>original: *uictor labos*. This is a nod to the Roman poet Virgil’s famous phrase *labor omnia vincit* (hard work overcomes all obstacles).</note>
And a victorious care is powerful through all things.
How it pleased us here to see the golden apples <note>original: *poma aurea*. In a classical context, "golden apples" often refers to citrons or lemons, evoking the mythical Garden of the Hesperides.</note>
Hanging in abundance from the drooping little branches:
To see everything turning green wherever one looks,
And the ground strewn everywhere with fallen blossoms,
All things smelling so sweetly fragrant.
These things, just as they were done by us before,
Are sought out and recounted in that same order.
The sunny hill of Pansacus <note>A specific location, likely the village of Panzacco in the Veneto region, where the host’s estate was located.</note> among these
Is not passed over; rather, it is celebrated with the praise
Of almost everyone—more praise than I can express,
For it is not to be proclaimed in such humble verses.
For who, within the small scope of a poem,
Could encompass the praises of such a fertile estate?
Surpassing all else by the cultivator’s art, not its own strength,
And those things which flourish in the fertile soil
Under the protection of the "deity of the countryside" <note>original: *rusticantis numinis*. A playful, complimentary reference to the owner of the estate, suggesting he governs his land like a local god.</note>,
And those things which follow the ancient order
With a prosperous succession of cultivators.
While we, performing these things, catch from everywhere
Whatever might provide us with laughter, meanwhile,
While each of us has indeed forgotten himself <note>Meaning they have lost themselves in the joy and relaxation of the moment.</note>,
Still singing, though a bit tired, we enter
Almost into the city, and soon in the house of our host

Little book, dearer to me

# TO H. BONONIUS <note>Hieronymus Bononius (Girolamo Bologni, 1454–1517) was an Italian humanist, lawyer, and poet from Treviso, known for his extensive literary circles.</note> ON THE DEATH OF HIS DEAREST LITTLE SON.

## POEM 21.

Octavius, little soul <note>original: *animula*. A tender diminutive of *anima* (soul), famously used by the Emperor Hadrian in his own deathbed poem to suggest the fragility of life.</note>, the life of your Mother and Father,
Where are you withdrawing? Where do you flee? Return: stay.
I shall lead you to your parents, who are anxious for you.
Stay, my little dove; stay, sweet boy.
Woe is me, why is it that I cannot hold you?
But like a shadow, you pull yourself away from me.
And this face is surely yours, and your gait;
You are certainly Octavius, the son of the poet <term>Bononius</term> <note>A reference to the father, Girolamo Bologni, characterizing him as a *vates*—a divinely inspired poet or prophet.</note>:
Whom he calls always by night, and always by day,
Wretched man, lamenting in vain with a sad <term>muse</term> <note>original: *camœna*. The Camenae were Roman goddesses of song and springs, often used as a synonym for the Muses who inspire poetry.</note>.
From where comes this adornment of yours? And the beauty of your form?
More majestic than usual, and breathing of a God?
Oh, if I, Love, could lead you back to your parents:
How dear you would be; what pleasing gifts they would give you.
Draw near, if you please; come here, Boy, I pray:
Come here for a kiss, here, honey-sweet one; here, little heart <note>original: *corculum*. A Roman term of endearment, literally "little heart," used to express deep affection for a child or loved one.</note>, come here.
You seem for some time now to pay no heed to these words.
But who is this light assembly of infants?
Look, who stands above you, and suddenly flies around you?
Where do they carry you through the cloven clouds,
Through the pure air to the golden seats of the <term>Heavens</term> <note>original: *Poli*. Literally "of the Pole," referring to the celestial pole or the highest reaches of the heavens.</note>?
What sweet song resounds in my ears?

<vocab>Octavius, Bononius, Poem, Death, Little Son</vocab>

<summary>This poem is a moving funeral elegy for Octavius, the young son of the poet Girolamo Bologni. It transitions from a desperate plea for the child to return to his grieving parents to a visionary description of the boy's soul being carried to heaven by a choir of infants.</summary>

<keywords>Girolamo Bologni, Octavius, grief, elegy, childhood death, soul's ascent, Renaissance humanism, Latin poetry</keywords>

O blessed are you, who, quickly leaving this decaying weight <note>original: *putre pondus*. A common Latin poetic trope describing the mortal body as a heavy, earthly burden that the soul sheds to return to the heavens.</note> upon the earth, seek a star equal to yourself. ### TO MARINUS LAMBERTUS <note>Likely a humanist colleague of the author. The name "Amorislaus" (Glory of Love) appears to be a pseudonym or a poetic persona used in this dialogue.</note>: AMORISLAUS.

## POEM IN PURE IAMBICS <term>Iambics</term> <note>A meter in classical poetry consisting of iambic feet (short-long). "Pure" iambics mean the poet strictly adheres to the rhythm without substituting other types of poetic feet.</note>.
### 22.

What is this, Marinus, and from what source should I suspect it is given?
<margin>q</margin> Is it the nature of faithful lovers, that one should always live within the other, and the heart of the one who loves should beat in turn within the breast of the one beloved? What is that other thing too: that he should desire nothing more and wish for only that for which he burns—and consider no honor more worthy than the beloved? Why is it that he cannot be captured by the worth of anyone else, by the love of anyone else, or the modesty of anyone else, but one single beauty always comes forward before him, and she alone is loved and cherished above all? But if she herself should happen to perish by chance...

Yet this [love] leaves behind a longing for itself. Let this other thing recede far away; it does this very thing also. One alone remains stored in the mind of the Lover: nor does it allow another to enter, but let whatever foolish thing was here before depart. Truly, nothing is more charming than a lover: for he shines glowing with Love, just as a happy house gleams with a splendid fire. All that you see consists of Love; Love alone is the Father of every fitting good;

<margin>m</margin> It used to seem a wonder to Vadius <note>Likely Joachim Vadianus (1484–1551), a prominent Swiss humanist and scholar.</note>, as he once said: that, although Petrarch was otherwise a sharp and skillful poet <note>original: *vates*. A term for a divinely inspired poet-prophet.</note> who frequently wrote many verses in Latin, and though he was experienced, he saw too little of what was fitting [in that tongue]; yet in the language which we now use <note>Refers to the Italian vernacular.</note>, he so filled his charming poems with all the Graces <note>original: *Veneres*. Literally "Venuses," a classical term for the aesthetic charms, beauties, or "loveliness" of a literary work.</note> that he can be compared to all the best poets of the Greek and Latin Muse.

<meta>This page continues Poem 23, addressed to Cornelius Castalius Fertinus. # IAMBICS <note>original: *IAMB.* Refers to the iambic meter used in this poem.</note>

Thus he spoke: he was at once an honest witness,
at once learned in matters, and at once prudent,
a man skilled in both languages <note>original: *utraque lingua*. In a Renaissance context, this refers to being fluent in both Latin and Greek.</note>, and an old man
endowed with the great virtue of a wondrous life.
On the other hand, as often as I examine your elegant poem
sent to me and to Bologna <note>original: *Bononium*.</note> now:
and whatever similar poems you frequently send out:
I immediately follow you with so much just praise:
Because, having emulated the ancients by the ancient law,
you express not only their sentiments, but also their words,
and you seek a glory equal to theirs.
But when I read through your *Amores* <note>original: *Amores*. Likely a collection of love poetry written in the Italian vernacular.</note> which are now
being circulated, I wonder that you feel differently
than before; you, who were always accustomed to follow
the renowned Lydian poets <note>original: *Poëtas lydios*. Humanists often referred to Tuscans as "Lydians" due to the historical theory that Etruscans originated in Lydia. This refers to the great Tuscan poets Dante and Petrarch.</note>, such as Dante
or even more Petrarch, and once composed serious verses,
and at the same time verses set to the sweet Lyre.
But now, having wrongly followed certain new,
minor poets <note>original: *vates minutos*. Literally "tiny" or "petty" poets; a dismissive term for contemporary writers who lack the depth of the classical tradition.</note>, you indeed surpass them by as much
as you previously drew full draughts of water
from the ancient springs: but you do not equal
those men whom you used to follow in your former race.
Nor would I think this should be attributed to a fault of your mind,
unless it is because you wish it so: for you are free,
as you please, to strip off base garments first:
and soon to enjoy the golden clothing which becomes you.
For the ornament of the new language <note>The Italian vernacular.</note> is its purity,
its splendor, and the labor added by the sharp file <note>original: *limæ*. A classical metaphor for the meticulous polishing and editing of a literary work.</note>:

<meta>catchword: Lest [Ne]</meta>

<summary>The author praises Cornelius for his skill in classical imitation but expresses surprise that he has recently turned toward "minor" contemporary poets. He encourages Cornelius to return to the high standards of Dante and Petrarch, noting that true beauty in the vernacular comes from rigorous refinement.</summary>

Will nothing at all be of benefit now? Not wealth,

->POEM XXV.<-

Aldus, these works which our little book contains
Were begun when six *lustra* <note>A *lustrum* is a five-year period; six *lustra* indicates the author was thirty years old.</note> of my life had passed,
But they were finished a little after ten *lustra* <note>Fifty years of age.</note>.
This is why poets perhaps suppress for nine years—as they say—
Whatever they wish to publish <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace’s advice in *Ars Poetica* that a writer should wait nine years before publishing a work to ensure its quality.</note>
Unpolluted by any stain; even if they cannot
Fill it with every grace, or if the bow
Does not always strike that which it threatens from afar,
Since human strength alone brings forth nothing perfect.

<header>BOOK I.</header>

Therefore, to these works, whatever care could be bestowed,
We gave our utmost effort and diligence,
So that they might one day reach the hands of posterity
Shining and thoroughly polished.
Nor should they be an immediate object of contempt for the living,
If they were first weighed in a just scale.
Thus I was thinking to give a long life
To my little books, as if they were children to me,
Sufficiently and properly raised according to custom.
For these books, I believe, there shall be a second life,
If you will deign to make them more abundant through the gift
Of your adoption <note>The author uses the metaphor of "adoption" to describe Aldus Manutius taking the manuscript under his care to edit and print it.</note>, as they deserve.
That is, if you look keenly at anything here
Which could be said more elegantly in the proper way,
And if you change it according to your great knowledge.
And soon, if you lead them forth from their father’s house
Clothed in that shining garment <note>The "shining garment" refers to the beautiful typography and high-quality paper of an Aldine press edition.</note>,
Which you grant to poets with your great praise;

# BOOK I. IAMBICS.

->*It had made a shelter for the singer.* <note>original: "Tegmen canenti fecerat."</note><-

**E**ver since then, the poets cherish such a great tree:
*And diligently, they almost worship it.*
**B**ut among them all, it delighted the Lydian Poet <note>The "Lydian Poet" likely refers to the Roman poet Horace, who was often associated with Lydian ancestry and was a master of the lyric forms being emulated here.</note>
*To play under this tree at the same time,*
**M**any have traveled the same path, as if it were granted,
*To seek its foliage.*
**B**ut often they do so in vain, and it happened that one could scarcely see
*The leaves even when looking from afar.*
**S**o difficult is the approach, and the sacred tree
*Stands back along a long, winding path.*
**Y**ou perhaps, O Momus <note>A nickname for the addressee, Girolamo Accio Avogadro. In Greek mythology, Momus was the god of satire and mockery; here it suggests the addressee is a keen critic or witty poet.</note>, by your good fortune,
*Skillfully approach it closely.*
**A**nd now, under the high peak of its foliage,
*You catch the shifting, green breeze:*
**Y**ou now break off a crown from the living trunk
*For your honorable head.*
**Y**ou handle it in person, and sometimes carry
*The chosen little branches in your light hand.*
**A**nd rejoicing in the most beautiful shade of this one tree,
*You despise all others everywhere.*
**O**nce adorning the greatest Triumphs <note>The Roman "Triumph" was a civil ceremony and religious rite held to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander; the commander wore a laurel wreath.</note>,
*And carried on the chariot of princes,*
**I**t showed that it was not the lowest of stocks,
*Among all that the earth brings forth.*
**T**his, the hand of thundering Jove <note>Jupiter (Jove) was the king of the gods and god of the sky and thunder.</note> does not deny:
*For this is the one tree he does not strike;* <note>The author refers to the ancient Greco-Roman belief, documented by Pliny the Elder, that the laurel tree was the only vegetation never struck by lightning.</note>

<summary>The poet praises the laurel tree as a sacred symbol of poetic achievement, noting its exclusivity and its immunity to the lightning of Jupiter. He celebrates his friend Girolamo (Momus) for successfully attaining the "leaves" of poetic glory that many others seek in vain.</summary>
<keywords>laurel tree, Apollo, Horace, Momus, Girolamo Accio Avogadro, poetic fame, Jupiter, Roman Triumphs, lightning protection</keywords>

# PEROLLA TO IPHIS:
# THAT HE SHOULD NOT HAVE SUSPICIONS

## POEM 27.

<margin>P</margin>
*Since you alone hold power*
*Over my mind and my body,* <note>original: "Ex mentis, atq; corporis / Mei potens"</note>
*Always take care that you love us.*

<margin>S</margin>
*Keep the faith you gave to me,*
*And remain faithful to your promises,*
*Just as you have been doing to the utmost.*

<margin>E</margin>
*And although you do this diligently—*
*For I know you desire me alone,*
*And you seek nothing besides this—*

<margin>A</margin>
*Love nevertheless so stirs*
*My raging spirit within me,* <note>The speaker describes her "furentem spiritum" (raging or burning spirit), a common way for Renaissance poets to describe the intense, almost maddening agitation of romantic passion.</note>
*That I am compelled to speak with you in these words.*

<margin>N</margin>
*Not because your visible fire*
*Appears to be extinguished anywhere,*
*Or because the old heat has grown less;*

<margin>Q</margin>
*But rather, I always marvel*
*That you are so agitated; and I feel*
*That you are struck by a heavy fear:* <note>The speaker is noticing her lover's anxiety and jealousy, rather than a lack of affection.</note>

<margin>N</margin>
*Lest perhaps another lover*
*Might cling more closely to me,*
*And I might desert you, poor wretch.* <note>original: "te misellum." The diminutive "misellum" (little wretch) is a term of endearment frequently used in the love poetry of Catullus to express pity or tender concern.</note>

<margin>S</margin>
*But as long as the mountains remain in their own place* <note>The text breaks off here, but this is the start of a "formula of impossibility" (an adynaton), a poetic device where a speaker claims they will remain faithful as long as the laws of nature—like mountains staying still—remain unchanged.</note>

<summary>This page contains a poem written from a female perspective (Perolla) to her lover (Iphis), reassuring him of her fidelity and addressing his deep-seated fears of being replaced by a rival. It utilizes the classical language of Roman elegy to explore themes of jealousy and devotion.</summary>

# BOOK I.

...than any mortal could.

**N**ow, let your life not be heavy 
To you: please, my dear, take care of this:
Do not be suspicious any longer. <note>original: "amabò." A colloquial Latin expression of entreaty, similar to "I pray you" or "please," often used in affectionate conversation to bridge a gap of trust.</note>

**F**ashion it so that a soft bed <note>original: "mollis Thorus." In Roman poetry, the "soft bed" is a symbol of peace, security, and the comforts of mutual love, contrasting with the "hard" life of a soldier or a rejected lover.</note>
Always provides you with dreams
Filled with sweet rest.

**H**eavens, your slight faith toward me
Moves me not a little,
Nor is it—as it should be—trusting.

**V**ery much do I wish you to be certain:
That you bring me the greatest pain
Because you hesitate so. <note>The letter "V" here functions as the vowel "U" to continue the poetic structure; the speaker is expressing frustration that her lover still doubts her constancy.</note>

**D**issolve this false feeling
From your weary heart: and
Remove the cause of your grief.

**A**nd if you wish me well:
And do not desire what brings punishment:
You will do this, as we ask.

**L**ong will we both, happy,
Be far away, O my light, <note>original: "mea Lux." A common term of endearment in Latin love poetry, comparing the beloved to the very light of one's eyes or the sun.</note>
From dark thoughts.

**E**ven after a thousand years there will be
One who, perhaps for the sake of Love, <note>original: "Amoris gratia." This refers to the personified god of Love (Cupid), suggesting their romance will become a legendary example for future generations.</note>
Will be stunned with envy at such a great love;
