# TO BENEDETTO BERTOLDO
# OF PADUA. A COM-
# MENDATION OF ELEGANT
# LOVE.

## POEM XXVIII.

*When you reveal your fires to me,*
*The fires of your heart for your mistress:*
*I feel the idle strengths of a languishing*
*blaze resume within me.*
*Indeed, I feel the sharp arrows <note>original: "spicula." These are the small, sharp darts or arrows associated with Cupid, the god of Love.</note> being readied,*
*And the bow drawn tight against the string,*
*With which Love used to wound me—lost as I was—*
*With frequent injury.*
*For who is scorched by a more beautiful flame?*
*Who by a slower-burning fuel? <note>original: "lentiore fomite." In the language of Renaissance romance, a "slow fuel" refers to a love that is steady, deep, and enduring, rather than a brief flash of lust.</note>*
*Blessed, you burn for the most beautiful of all the maidens*
*As many as frequent the bank*
*Of the living spring—the one that is visited*
*By all the girls most closely.*
*And you have a means by which your heart might*
*Seethe more gently under this heat,*
*Because you constantly soothe your scorched wounds*
*With the pure water <note>original: "Lympha." While meaning "water," this term often carries a poetic or religious connotation, referring to a water-nymph or the cleansing power of a sacred stream.</note> of the sacred stream.*
*And the moisture is enough to relieve these wounds:*
*And the heat strives against it.*
*For indeed, they gush forth from the same source with such unequal force*

<summary>This poem is a personal address to Benedetto Bertoldo of Padua, where the poet describes how hearing about Benedetto’s refined and steady passion for a beautiful woman has reignited his own dormant feelings of love.</summary>

What can we not bear by loving? What can we not endure?

->A DREAM: OR THE IMAGE<-
->OF A VISION NOT FALSE<-

### POEM XXIX.

Sleep, that gift of the Gods, is gone:
The sweet rest of mortals.
Depart, you wicked cares, into
The deepest oblivion.
Depart! Why do you linger?
Or shall one care always follow another
In succession? And shall they gush forth
In the manner of a continuous, wandering river?
Certainly not: for I shall either wash you away,
You sad things, with plenty of pure wine <note>original: "mero." Unmixed or "neat" wine, often invoked in classical poetry as a remedy for sorrow or insomnia.</note>:
Or with the sweet little words <note>original: "uerbulis." A diminutive form of "words," implying playful, affectionate, or flirtatious chatter.</note> of a girl

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic sequence of Janus Secundus, specifically continuing the theme of the "Dream" (Somnium) initiated on the previous page. While the previous page dealt with the struggle against insomnia and "cares," this page transitions into the surrender to sleep and the arrival of a divine, poetic vision.</meta>

# IAMBIC 118

At last I shall surrender to the charming rest:
Here, a soft bed will be spread;
Here, the fragrant Rose will breathe its scent.
An elegant boy will strike the lyre,
And a singing maiden will accompany him.
A gentle sleep will slowly steal
Over my eyes for my weary self,
And desired dreams will present themselves
To my happy mind.
Look! I seem to see the light
Dances of the Nymphs <note>Divine nature spirits often associated with springs and groves.</note> playing even now
By the clear stream of water
Dedicated to the Muses <note>The nine goddesses of the arts and sciences.</note>.
Is this sleep? Or rest?
Or a vision? Or indeed a dream?
To see what we seem to see, and
To enjoy such sweet repose.
Who is she? The one who adorns a garland
With so many little flowers; or she who,
Gathering roses from the blushing stem,
Offers those fleeting blossoms to her?
O, for whose head are these prepared?
For whose learned brow are these rewards,
For whom the Myrtle of Cypris <note>original: "Cypridis." An epithet for Venus (Aphrodite), who was born from the sea foam near Cyprus; the myrtle tree was sacred to her as a symbol of love.</note>
And the Laurel of Phoebus <note>original: "Phœbi." Apollo, the god of light, music, and poetry; the laurel crown was the traditional prize for poetic excellence.</note> are woven together?
Unless perhaps you weave a crown
For a poet just now being born:
O sweet and, O, guardians
Of the eternally flowing wave!

<meta>Catchword: IOANNIS (John/Janus)</meta>

<summary>In this poem, the speaker finally succumbs to a pleasant sleep where he envisions a chorus of nymphs by a sacred stream. He witnesses them weaving a crown of myrtle and laurel, symbols of love and poetic mastery, intended for a "newborn poet."</summary>
<keywords>Janus Secundus, Dream, Nymphs, Muses, Poetry, Venus, Apollo, Laurel Crown, Myrtle, Sleep</keywords>

# A POEM COMPOSED ENTIRELY OF IAMBS.
<note>original: "ex solis iambis." An iamb is a metrical "foot" consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one. Augurello is highlighting his technical skill in maintaining this specific rhythm throughout the poem.</note>

#### I.

*Be present, divine Muse of Pieria* <note>original: "Pieris." A name for the Muses, the Greek goddesses of inspiration, named after their birthplace in Pieria.</note> *favorably*
->*to me,*<-
❦ *Protect this gift for a long time,*
->*which I bring*<-
*Into your fitting temple now,*
->*to the protector.*<- <note>original: "praesidi." This refers to a patron or a guardian deity who watches over the arts.</note>
*For you yourself will better approve of this work*
*Because of this: that he who worships the Goddesses will wish*
*To dedicate it eternally to devout poets?*
*If a painted tablet* <note>original: "tabella." Here referring to a painting or a votive offering.</note> *is to be dedicated to the gods,*
*Coated with the colors of a powerful Art,*
*Another painter, a friend, is usually accustomed*
*To see it immediately, before it is brought forth*
*To be instantly approved or rejected by the ignorant.*
*Therefore, when a skilled craftsman approaches,*
*(What the author himself perhaps had not been able* <note>The sentence continues onto the next page, suggesting the craftsman sees flaws or beauties the author missed.</note>

<summary>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello opens his second book of poems with a dedication to fellow poet Girolamo Bologni, arguing that poetry should be judged by peers rather than the unlearned public. He uses the metaphor of a painter seeking a friend's expert eye before displaying a masterpiece in a temple.</summary>

It then transitions into a new poem addressed to Antonio Vonica, another scholar from Treviso, advising him to balance his heavy philosophical studies with the lighter pursuit of poetry.</meta>

<header>IAMBIC VERSE.</header>

*...meanwhile, captured by a love for the work, he sees* <note>Continuing from the previous page: the "he" refers to a fellow poet acting as a critic.</note>
*where it may fail, or what it can show*
*when filled with elegant graces.*
*That same poet, taking the role of a judge,*
*will act as a companion to the recent poem.*
*This little book of mine, Bologni, will submit*
*itself to you—though it was written under your own auspices—*
*because a dear little friend* <note>original: "Amiculo." This likely refers to a mutual acquaintance or is an affectionate diminutive for a specific person in their circle.</note> *requested it of me.*
*You will polish it fairly, and most skillfully wash away*
*the stains of whatever might be within or without;*
*nor will its glory be tainted by any deceit*
*when something in it seems to shine to you.*
*A poet cannot place a poem more foolishly*
*than one who dedicates it solely to the powerful,*
*without first seeking out an educated ear;*

### TO ANTONIO VONICA
### OF TREVISO: *The studies*

->*of philosophy should sometimes be interrupted,*<-
->*and time must be made for the gentler*<-
->*Muses.*<- <note>The "Gentler Muses" refers to poetry and literature, which were considered "lighter" or more restorative than the "heavy" labor of technical philosophy.</note>

### POEM II.

*Any strength of mortals that has not become accustomed*
*to softening labor with alternating rest,*
*will grow weary, Vonica, conquered by the constant burden,*
*and will collapse of its own accord.* <note>Augurello is using a common classical trope: that the mind, like a bow, will break if it is always kept under tension. He encourages his friend to find "rest" in writing poetry.</note>

<header>BOOK II. IAMBICS.</header>
<sig>d ii</sig>

*Thus we, who have already spent a full year*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*devoted to Socratic discourses,* <note>original: "Socraticis... sermonibus." This refers to the study of moral philosophy and dialectic associated with the philosopher Socrates.</note>
*unless we soften harsh, serious matters with the gentle Muses,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*or relax our intense focus,*
*might perhaps be forced, weary on the journey itself,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*to set down the burden from our weighed-down shoulders.*
*Nature herself observes equal turns, and she alone*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*alternates by just laws...*
*And the hands of the craftsman vary; the left hand,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*in turn, steps in for the weary right hand.*
*And sometimes the wise poet inserts empty trifles*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*into a serious poem,* <note>original: "nugas." This term was famously used by the Roman poet Catullus to describe light, playful, or "frivolous" poetry that nonetheless required great skill.</note>
*so that he may relieve those who are too focused on weightier things,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*and render them more eager,*
*and make them more vigorous for the distinctions of things,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*if ever a "knot" should occur,* <note>A "knot" (nodus) refers to a difficult or complex philosophical problem that is hard to untangle.</note>
*which, being worthy of solution, might test the sharpness of mind*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*of those looking deeply within.*
*Thus, with lighter things placed between for a short while,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*you yourself repay the turn with leisure:*
*And continue with the poem for which you began with such praise,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*and advance your step further;*
*While I, having set aside all else, strive to fit sharp iambics*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*to the sweet lyre;* <term>Barbiton: A multi-stringed instrument of the lyre family, used in ancient Greece and revived as a poetic symbol in the Renaissance for lyric and iambic verse.</term>

<summary>Augurello uses the metaphors of a craftsman switching hands and the alternation of nature to justify taking breaks from philosophy. He encourages Vonica to continue his own poetry while the author focuses on composing iambic verse.</summary>

<header>IAMBICS.</header>

Meanwhile, captivated by love for the work, the poet sees
its flaws, or what it can offer when filled with elegant charms. <note>original: "gratiis." The "Graces" in Greek and Roman mythology were the goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity.</note>
The same poet, acting as a judge,
will play the role of a companion in this new poem.
This little book of mine, O Bononius, written under your 
favorable omen, will grant you to take up the same burden 
at my request, dear little friend.
You will polish it justly, and most skillfully wash away 
whatever blemishes exist within or without; 
its glory will not be stained by any deceit 
where something appears to you to shine. 
A poet can place a poem in no more foolish a way
than by dedicating it only to the powerful,
without first seeking out an educated ear;

# BOOK II.

Thus we, who have already spent a full year dedicated to Socratic discourses, <note>original: "Socraticis sermonibus." This refers to the study of moral philosophy through the dialectical method of Socrates.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;unless we soften these harsh, serious matters with the gentle Muses,
or relax our strained minds,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;we might perhaps be forced, weary, to set down the burden
from our pressed shoulders right in the middle of our journey.
Nature herself observes equal cycles, and alternates 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;by her own fair laws.
Even the hands of a craftsman alternate, and the left hand 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;takes over for the tired right hand in turn.
Sometimes a wise poet <note>original: "vates." A term implying a divinely inspired poet or prophet.</note> skillfully inserts empty trifles into a serious poem,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;so that he may relieve those readers who are too focused on heavier things,
and so that he may make them more eager,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;restoring their vigor for discerning the nature of things,
if ever a difficult knot should arise
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;that is worthy of being untied, and which tests the mental sharpness
of those looking deep within.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So, by interspersing these lighter moments, you yourself 
should grant a turn to holidays:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And proceed with the poem you began so praiseworthy,
and advance your progress further:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;While, having set aside all else, I shall attempt to fit sharp iambics <note>A rhythmic pattern in poetry (short-long) often used for lively or biting verse.</note>
to the sweet lyre; <note>original: "Barbito." A barbitos was a multi-stringed ancient instrument, similar to a lyre, associated with lyric poetry.</note>

<sig>d ii</sig>

<vocab>Socratic, muses, nature, poet, poem, iambics, lyre</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that even the most dedicated scholars must balance intense philosophical study with the "trifles" of poetry to avoid mental exhaustion. It compares this intellectual rest to the natural cycles of the world and the way a craftsman switches hands to finish a task.</summary>
<keywords>Socratic philosophy, Muses, intellectual rest, poetic meter, Antonio Vonica, nature's laws, craftsmanship</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general defense of lighthearted poetry in the previous section to a specific example of iambic verse. It begins a new poem addressed to Ludovico Marcello, celebrating his ability to find happiness in both urban and rural life.</meta>

# IAMBIC VERSE.

## TO LUDOVICO MARCELLO

Knight of the Order of Jerusalem: <note>original: "HIER. Equitem." Short for "Hierosolymitano Equitem," referring to the Knights of Saint John of Jerusalem, also known as the Knights of Malta.</note> stating that he seems blessed, because to the two charms <note>original: "gratiis." The poet plays on the dual meaning of "graces" as both mythological entities and social or natural pleasures.</note> which he himself enjoys—the urban and the rural—he adds a third: that he lives content with these.

### POEM III.

<margin>m</margin>Marcello, no other man is more blessed than you,
if you weigh within yourself what true happiness is:
For you, whether you wish to be in the city or the countryside,
the elegant Graces <note>In Greek and Roman mythology, the three Graces represent charm, beauty, and human creativity.</note> frequently play on every side.
The finest citizen visits your home,
as does the craftsman skilled in every possible art,
and every friend of the Muses, and every devout poet. <note>original: "vates pius." A "vates" is more than a poet; they were considered divinely inspired prophets.</note>
Your home, matching your honorable comforts,
shines everywhere with refined elegance.
This is the urban charm that presents itself to you.
The rural charm is just as ready, and does not delay:
Whenever you seek your estate on the green hill—
however lovely it may be—whether it pleases you to watch the Nymphs
in the cave of a rugged cliff, or by the water of a clear spring,
or the gods wandering here and there through the sacred grove,
while gentle Pomona <note>The Roman goddess of fruit trees, gardens, and orchards.</note> fills your lap with the purest fruits;
while Flora <note>The Roman goddess of flowers and the season of spring.</note> circles her head with flowery wreaths,
and joyful Abundance steps forward with her overflowing horn. <note>original: "cornu... copia." This is the "cornucopia" or horn of plenty, a symbol of harvest and prosperity.</note>

<summary>This page contains the opening of Poem III, a tribute to the Knight Ludovico Marcello, praising his balanced life of city sophistication and country leisure. The poet argues that Marcello’s greatest blessing is his contentment with the various "Graces" or pleasures available to him.</summary>

<keywords>Ludovico Marcello, Iambic poetry, urban life, rural life, Graces, Pomona, Flora, Cornucopia, contentment, Knights of Jerusalem</keywords>

BOOK II.

Therefore, you seem blessed by these two graces
to many. But to me, you are wealthy, powerful,
and kind: we acknowledge each of these qualities.
Yet we deny that you have made yourself blessed
by these things alone: rather, it is by a greater benefit,
that you live content with these, far from all worldly ambition.
Being free, you seek nothing more, living for yourself.
And thus a third sister <note>The poet refers to the three Graces. Marcello enjoys the first two (the city and the country), but the third (true contentment) is what completes his happiness.</note> joins the other two—
that which is believed by the learned to be true happiness.
This one grace alone makes you truly blessed.

## TO FLAMINIUS. <note>Likely Marcus Antonius Flaminius (Marcantonio Flaminio, 1498–1550), a famous humanist poet known for his lyrical Latin verse.</note>

### POEM V.

**W**hy, Flaminius, do you reproach me with my hastening years,
**o**r that burden which nature brings forth?
Do I seem to knock at the doorposts of trembling old age,
Or to be far from those doors?
**S**ince you often say, while beholding this same face,
that the passing days ought to have changed it.
**H**ow much better perhaps it would be, if you were to think
that what you see is just as it appears.
**F**or you do not see empty things; this very complexion
shines with its own youthful strength.
**F**or although I have now passed three times three <term>lustra</term> <note>A "lustrum" is a ritual purification performed every five years; here it denotes a five-year period. "Three times three" indicates the poet is forty-five years old.</note> by the perennial
<meta>catchword: slipping [away]</meta>

<summary>The author concludes a poem celebrating his Muse's return and begins a new poem to his friend Flaminius, arguing that despite being forty-five years old, he retains his youthful vigor and poetic passion.</summary>
<keywords>Clio, Apollo, Flaminius, aging, youthful vigor, lustra, iambic meter, Latin lyric poetry</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the poet's response to his friend Flaminius. On the previous page, the author began defending his youthful appearance and spirit despite his advancing age (noting he has passed "three times ten" or perhaps more years). This page concludes that defense by arguing for the moral and practical value of poetry.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>
<sig>d iiii</sig>

Along the path of life’s deceptive course,
I was once able to hold the reins of my fleeting youth
with a firm right hand,
so that the same vigor that first drove my flourishing years
might also be present here, held fast and fierce;
and just as it used to grant my slender limbs
a welcome and steady strength,
so that same power might continue to keep me strong
with a constant vitality, <note>The author uses "uirtute" (virtue/strength) to mean both moral character and physical health.</note>
so that I may still delight in gentle pastimes
freed from every worry.
Amidst these delights—nature's earthly gifts—
it pleases me to leaf through the works of the holy poets,
whom you, for some reason I cannot fathom
or for some slight cause, seem to despise.
And yet, despite your disdain, you appear
to walk as a noble bard among the poets yourself—
for by the very poem in which you mock the Muses,
you crown your own head with laurel. <note>The laurel wreath was the traditional crown for poets and victors in antiquity; the author is teasing Flaminius for being a great poet even while he pretends to dislike poetry.</note>
For indeed, you can find no class of men
more useful than the poets;
they do not bring you trifles in silly verse
or empty fables, as you claim,
but instead, once they have lightened the mind
from its heavy sorrows with the sweet medicine of song,
they bring to eager ears everything
that serves to guide a man’s life.
I do not write here to heap further praise
upon those who serve the Muses... <note>original: "Pieridum." The Pierides are the Muses, named after their birthplace in Pieria.</note>

<vocab>Pieridum (the Muses), poëtas (poets), Musas (Muses), iuuentæ (of youth), uirtute (with strength/virtue)</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes his defense of his youthful spirit, arguing that poetry is not merely idle "fable" but a "sweet medicine" that provides moral guidance and relief from care. He playfully points out that even his critic, Flaminius, is a talented poet who earns the laurel wreath he claims to disparage.</summary>

I maintain that they have their own virtues.

  since I read almost all writers—
whether those who wander through the causes of things with panting breath,

Or to produce anything at all?
This friend himself, who can exchange ten poems 
for a single verse. <note>The author suggests his friend is so talented that one of his lines is worth ten of anyone else's.</note>
Away with this venerable bashfulness of mine,
or fear, or my own sluggishness.
Let us strive with all our strength—for so he commands—
to describe the beauty of a maiden’s form.
It is fitting for you, O Muse, since you are the glory of maidens,
to favor these beginnings.
Be present and kind, and uphold your role here
by watching over us more closely.
First, she is not beautiful to me whom Nature 
has not bountifully aided: 
but who instead seeks from art the help she desires,
and walks about painted, just like 
a bird sprinkled with colors not its own. <note>A reference to the Aesopic fable of the jackdaw who dressed in the feathers of other birds to appear more beautiful.</note>
Nor, on the other hand, does she please much,
to whom many natural gifts have fallen, 
if she herself, being idle, does not cultivate them.
But she who, having been granted both, 
preserves with greater care that which she received—
she alone is worthy to please 
those who look upon her with love.
She whose hair, not overly labored, 
shines upon her head like gold:
but hanging loose, it seems to carry 
a negligent diligence. <note>original: "negligentem... diligentiam". This is a classic rhetorical oxymoron describing the "art of artlessness"—making a carefully arranged appearance look effortless.</note>
She who is skilled in all things, and [bestows] grace upon

<header>IAMBIC VERSE.</header>

->He compares her to each man's own ideal.<-

Her little eyes <note>original: *ocelli*, a diminutive of "eyes" often used as a term of endearment.</note> sparkle on her brow, but she hides 
or reveals them in a becoming manner.
She keeps silent in her turn, nor does she chatter 
with talkative little words <note>original: *uerbulis*, suggesting trivial or idle gossip.</note> among those who speak.
She walks with decorum and sits softly, 
lovely in her fitting manners.
Pleasing in any complexion, provided a healthy vitality <note>original: *succus*, literally "juice" or "sap," referring here to a healthy, lively glow or "bloom" of the skin.</note> lies beneath; 
she is more pleasing than any woman of pale white skin.
Tall, straight, and slender, yet in such a way 
that she does not have limbs like reeds <note>original: *iunceos*. The poet praises a lithe figure but warns against being "skinny as a reed."</note>.
She carries the sweet form of a noble boy, 
like that of a laughing Cupid <note>original: *cupidinis*. In Renaissance poetry, a "boyish" grace in a woman was often considered a mark of refined beauty and youth.</note>.
Yet tempering her laughter with modesty, 
she ennobles her sweet jests with a balanced grace.
Content with the one man she has chosen for her husband, 
she wisely savors what she enjoys in her own heart.

But if perhaps you desire, my friend, 
for these things to be told more minutely in detail,
Let her—adorned in her hair, well-fitted, sweet, charming, 
and modest—surpass all others:
She who might walk among the girls as one 
worthy of a poet, worthy of the choirs of the Muses.

<meta>This page continues the narrative sequence introduced at the end of the previous page, moving into the specific pastoral scene of "The Love of Alcimus and Alcon." The meter is likely elegiac couplets, common for such descriptive Roman-style poetry.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

The playful girls Cloris and Flora sport
Upon the green ground near a bubbling stream.
The handsome youths Alcimus and Alcon watch 
Secretly from a secluded mountain peak.
The maidens often bathe in the icy waves,
Their smooth legs bare, their breasts bare too.
But the boys' feet often stumble, and their gasping chests
Burn with the fire of love that strikes them.
From one side, Alcon's love is suddenly for you, fair Cloris;
From the other, Alcimus pines ardently for Flora—
Flora, who though she has a darker complexion <note>original: *fusco... colore*, referring to a tanned or swarthy skin tone, which the poet notes still "matches the others" in beauty despite the period's preference for pale skin.</note>,
Yet matches the others in her graceful ways.
Now each prefers his own and covers her in praise,
Enjoying a sweet murmur between themselves.
"Nowhere," says Alcon, "is any girl more beautiful than this one,
Wherever the nourishing light of day travels the earth.
See how her breasts are set wide apart,
And how they swell outward in a firm circle.
How softly the belly slopes down from the full chest,
Supported on every side by well-shaped legs.
How the thigh of the golden girl <note>original: *Aureolæ*. This may be a proper name, Aureola, or a descriptive term for a "golden" or radiant woman.</note> sprouts 
With the first soft down <note>original: *lanuginis*, referring to the fine, soft hair of youth.</note> of blooming hair.
How a snowy whiteness shines through her whole body,
Veiled by the gold of her spreading hair.
How she is perfectly made for soft and close embraces!
O how blessed we are, who are permitted to see such things!"
So spoke Alcon; and Alcimus answered him with a charming expression—
For Alcimus was ever ready with a witty remark.

<summary>This page contains a pastoral poem depicting two youths, Alcimus and Alcon, secretly observing two bathing maidens and debating their respective beauties. It utilizes classical "blazon" imagery, detailing physical perfections from a voyeuristic, idealized perspective.</summary>

<keywords>pastoral poetry, Alcimus, Alcon, feminine beauty, Cloris, Flora, classical tropes, bathing scene</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the pastoral dialogue between two youths watching young women from a distance, concluding the section on Alcimus (here called Alanus) and Alcon before transitioning to a new poem in the voice of an aging man.</meta>

<header>IAMBIC II.</header>

*“O how much more blessed you would be, if you could but touch these charms!*
*For as for me, it is enough to have seen Flora—*
*She, who now with but a nod of her laughing eye,*
*Has snatched me from myself, a sweet and wanton plunder,*
*And has bound my captured limbs in heavy knots*
*From which no passing day shall ever set me free;*
*Nor will any day stop me from keeping this vision in my heart,*
*Ever more tightly bound by these clinging chains.”*
*So spoke Alanus.* <note>The previous page identified this speaker as Alcimus; "Alanus" may be a variant name for the same character or a transcription choice in the original manuscript.</note> But the restless <margin>Alcon:</margin> Alcon does not offer a reply;
Instead, he rages, powerless and impatient of any delay,
And often shifts and stirs within his hidden coverts,
Frequently rustling the leaves this way and that.
Until at last the girls rush away in fearful flight,
Feeling themselves watched from the high cliffs;
But the youths hang there motionless, following with their gaze,
Wherever the weary girls hide themselves in secret.

# THE PERSONIFICATION <note>original: *Prosopopeia*, a rhetorical device where a speaker adopts the character or voice of another person, often for dramatic effect.</note> OF AN OLD MAN RAGING
## AT FIRST, AND THEN GROWING CALM
### N. F. to Bertutius Lambertus,
#### *Chief of Treviso.* <note>original: *prim. Tar.*, likely referring to the *primarius* (first citizen or chief magistrate) of Tarvisium, the Latin name for the city of Treviso.</note>

### POEM VIII.

*“Shall I never then be able, in a mild spirit, to endure*
<margin>i</margin> *The sharp attacks of a spiteful mind?*
*Shall I remain unavenged forever, while that most wicked*
*Head carries off the reward of its own pride?”*

<summary>This page concludes a pastoral scene where the youths Alcon and Alanus/Alcimus startle a group of bathing maidens, followed by the opening of a "Prosopopeia" or dramatic monologue of an old man addressing a civic leader in Treviso.</summary>
<keywords>Alcon, Alanus, Alcimus, Flora, pastoral poetry, Prosopopeia, Treviso, Bertutius Lambertus, revenge, pride</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Poem VIII, "The Personification of an Old Man Raging," which began on the previous page. The speaker, an elderly man, is expressing his indignation at being slandered by malicious critics before shifting to a more philosophical, calm reflection on the nature of public opinion.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

*Which, just as a swelling snake rears up, rages*
*Fiercely, stirred by malice, and moves its wicked*
*Mouth, lashing out everywhere; nor is it moved*
*By the sake of the matter itself, but rather attacks*
*The upright and the undeserving, just like a low-bred*
*Dog, which, when it cannot bite with its teeth,*
*Soon fills all places with fearsome barking.*
*Not so: for I will no longer bear such bites nor empty*
*Threats, nor will I suffer any longer that*
*The innocent be harassed by the guilty with impunity.*
*Justice persuades, and a more powerful force*
*Implanted in nature by right <note>original: *ius*, referring to natural law or inherent right.</note> commands—nature which*
*Even to mild beasts has provided strong weapons*
*To drive back the attack of the arrogant.*
*When the old man <note>original: *senex*.</note> had spoken these things, and revealed*
*The strong opinion of his hidden heart,*
*Having paused a little, he brought forth gentler words.*
*No one has ever sufficiently guarded against*
*What each person among the common folk might say,*
*Nor has any mortal ever guarded against it.*
*For there is a vast sort of them everywhere,*
*Who wish to be certain they know what they do not know:*
*And pretend to know everything, though they speak falsely.*
*They are people upon whom the labor of wisdom <note>original: *prudentia*.</note>*
*Never imposes any laws, nor provides a limit to keep,*
*To stop them from suddenly blurting out whatever they feel.*
*No one who is wise should think the uproar of these people*
*Is worth even a single penny <note>original: *assis*, the *as* was a small Roman copper coin of very little value.</note> at all:*
*But should deem them as fleeting as they are sudden.*

<summary>The poem concludes the old man's angry outburst against his detractors, comparing their malice to snakes and barking dogs, before he adopts a calmer tone to reflect on the worthlessness of gossip and the ignorance of the masses.</summary>
<keywords>malice, slander, nature, justice, wisdom, common folk, old man, stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page concludes the iambic poem (Poem VIII) regarding the "Old Man" and his transition from anger to a philosophical calm. It then introduces a new poem dedicated to Andrea Trevisan, the Bishop of Feltre, focusing on the theme of inner peace as the foundation of a happy life.</meta>

<header>IAMBIC VERSE</header>
<sig>f</sig>

You see, my friend, how different the mind of that same old man is when it is released from troubling cares. By those cares, he—who was once light-hearted—was long held back and raged, half-consumed by the fires of anger. Following his example, you should despise whatever the envious and base crowd whispers from every corner. Being forewarned, do not fear the bite of the "black tooth" <note>original: *atri dentis*. In Roman poetry, the "black tooth" is a common metaphor for envy or malicious slander that "bites" a person's reputation.</note>, which often rages fiercely against those who are unprepared. For you will have done enough to counter false rumors if you are shielded by the broad mantle of virtue. Often do more than you wish to be said you have done; do not seek to please yourself, nor boast that you are worthy of praise, unless you truly act in a way that deserves it. Let those who are less ashamed of sinning than of being the subject of idle gossip—flying through the mouths of the public in empty tales—be the ones to fear words and chattering stories.

### TO ANDREA TREVISAN,
### BISHOP OF FELTRE:

> A great part of a happy life is peace,
> which comes from a calm mind.

### POEM IX

It is said that a great part of a blessed life is peace; which neither the power of high honors <note>original: *honorum*, referring to public offices or titles of rank</note> nor wealth provides, but rather a mind capable of setting aside turbulent cares.

<term>Andrea Trevisan</term> (Latin: *Andreas Trivisanus*) was a Venetian nobleman who served as the Bishop of Feltre from 1499 to 1504. <term>Feltre</term> is a town in the Veneto region of northern Italy. The poet here uses the term <term>Carmen</term> to denote a formal song or poem, and <term>beata uita</term> (the happy or blessed life) to reference a central theme of ancient philosophy: how to achieve true fulfillment.

<summary>This page concludes a poem on resisting slander through virtue and begins a new work dedicated to Bishop Andrea Trevisan regarding the philosophical necessity of a calm mind.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Trevisan, Bishop of Feltre, virtue, slander, peace of mind, happy life, iambic poetry</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues Poem IX, addressed to Andrea Trevisan, Bishop of Feltre. The previous page established that true happiness comes from a calm mind rather than external honors or wealth. This page concludes that advice and introduces Poem X, addressed to the Venetian humanist Tryphon Gabriel.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

You may be able to obtain those external things in abundance:
But unless your mind becomes quiet and far removed from cares,
even those successes will not truly benefit you much.
Having experienced this, you know it better than anyone else:
you, who are accustomed to mock with me in serious speech
the empty and light cares of mortals,
so that it should be enough for me now
only to remind you of how you might wish to speak with yourself:
Lest, as the common proverb says,
I should now foolishly "bring owls to Athens," <note>original: *Athenas noctuas*. A classic proverb for performing a redundant or useless action, as Athens was already full of owls—the symbol of its patron goddess, Athena.</note>
Or "a pig teaching Minerva," <note>original: *sus Mineruam*. A proverb describing a fool attempting to teach a wise person or an expert.</note> or "carrying small waters to the sea."
Should I tell you how much cares press upon us "little men"? <note>original: *homunculos*. A diminutive form of "men," used here to emphasize human frailty and insignificance in the face of fate or emotion.</note>
Whether chance provides them, or necessity
drives them, or the mind itself leads them: Should I tell these things
to one most learned and endowed with the highest wisdom?
Or should I say how much force a heavy emotion,
once received into the soul, gradually generates?
And if one does not immediately thrust those feelings out the door,
do they not attack more powerfully by the hour?
You do not need these reminders; you need only to open
the sacred storehouse of your mind, and there will be present a ready,
effective remedy to help the sorrowful wound.

## TO TRYPHON GABRIEL, <term>Tryphon Gabriel</term> (Latin: *Tryphon Chabrielus*) was a celebrated Venetian humanist and poet (1474–1553), often called the "Socrates of Venice" for his wisdom and preference for oral teaching over publishing.
## A VENETIAN PATRICIAN:
### *on the changing of his own life,*
### *and the hatred of that change.*

### POEM X.

<vocab>Athenas noctuas, sus Mineruam, homunculos, penum mentis, Tryphon Chabrielus</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a philosophical exhortation to Bishop Andrea Trevisan on finding inner peace and introduces a new poem to the Venetian humanist Tryphon Gabriel regarding the stability of one's life choices.</summary>
<keywords>inner peace, stoicism, proverbs, Tryphon Gabriel, Andrea Trevisan, wisdom, human frailty</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophical advice of Poem IX into the nostalgic and personal reflections of Poem X (Iambic II). While the previous page addressed the Bishop of Feltre on finding internal peace, this page addresses Tryphon Gabriel, a central figure in the Venetian humanist circle, recalling their shared youth spent in pleasure, poetry, and the countryside.</meta>

### IAMBIC II

Tryphon, in our early youth, there was once a life
<margin>a</margin> Gushing forth abundantly with many good things. <note>original: *Scatens*. The poet uses the imagery of a bubbling spring to describe the vitality of their youth.</note>
When no thing was pleasing to my happy self
If it were devoid of love, sweet jests, or the Muses—
Muses full of the light playfulness of lovers.
When it was permitted for us to laugh from the rising
Of the sun right through to the dawn of the next little day:
Which it was necessary to make brief by sleeping,
So that it might repair the turn of the night just spent.
While on one side a friend would play verses on the lyre,
And on the other, a girl would tell little stories.
When Apollo <note>original: *Phoebus*. The god of the sun and poetry.</note> and Venus, competing in turn,
Would tire out Bacchus <note>original: *Lyæon*. An epithet for Bacchus/Dionysus, meaning "the deliverer" from care.</note> by night and by day.
When, weary of the city and seeking the countryside, I used to leave
Whatever troubles there were at the city gate,
Bringing a whole flock of laughter with me.
Where, watching the rivulets of gushing ripples,
I would relieve my heat by the mere sight of them.
Where I was allowed to enter the grove of green foliage
While the Nymphs <note>Nature spirits inhabiting the woods and waters.</note> looked on,
Just as I had seen the light-footed Fauns <note>Deities of the woods and fields, often depicted with goat-like features.</note> on high mountains
Catch the wild beasts exhausted by the sharp chase.
Where, bringing home from the country the sweet honors
Of Ceres and Pomona, and of Pales and Bacchus, <note>These four represent the bounty of the earth: Ceres (grain/agriculture), Pomona (fruit), Pales (livestock/shepherds), and Bacchus (wine).</note>
I feasted on banquets more delightful than any others.
When, in the meantime, I would often visit the sacred choirs of Apollo
As a devoted poet in both country and city,
If, however, I am worthy of so great a name. <note>original: *vates*. A term for a poet that implies a semi-divine or prophetic status; the author here adds a touch of humble self-deprecation.</note>

<summary>In this poem, the author reminisces to his friend Tryphon Gabriel about their idyllic youth, characterized by literary leisure, romantic play, and escapes to the countryside. It paints a vivid picture of the Renaissance humanist ideal of "otium" (productive leisure) through classical mythological imagery.</summary>

<keywords>Tryphon Gabriel, youth, pastoral life, Apollo, Bacchus, Muses, Ceres, Pomona, Pales, poetry, nostalgia</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Poem X (Iambic II) addressed to Tryphon Gabriel. While the previous page evoked a golden age of youthful leisure and rural escape, this page shifts to a lament over the poet's current entrapment in the "turbid cares" of public life and social obligation.</meta>

# BOOK II.

<meta>catchword: e</meta>

Now I wander far from the whole path, nor am I ever
Released onto the right one, driven along by turbulent cares. <note>original: *turbidis curis*. This refers to the anxieties and distractions of public or professional life.</note>
I, who now ficklely handle honors and now wealth:
Though I would willingly be far away from both:
Yet toward these things my friends drag me, the common crowd drags me,
One by speaking against me, another by urging me on, they demand:
That I knowingly pursue the very things I should flee,
And that not a single day is free from business, <term>negociis: from the Latin 'negotium', meaning the busy-work of public affairs, the opposite of 'otium' or scholarly leisure.</term>
Which distracts and drives me at every hour,
Allowing no space for any rest,
Indeed, they deny me rest all the more,
The more I claim it as my great reward.
Is this, then, to be called life—or was that?
Or is this a living Death, and that other the life of the Gods?
When perfect peace and perfect rest walked beside me
As companions with equal step, and no lawsuit
Clung to me, nor contention, nor the stubborn insolence
Of a spiteful quarrel ever disturbed my well-being.
When every horrific crime was utterly absent,
Along with all violence, all error, and all grasping ambition. <term>ambitus: the unethical or illegal pursuit of political office.</term>
I was a good friend to all good men:
A joyful companion and an upright comrade to everyone,
Among whom there was a mutual and faithful love, always
To be cherished above all else.
The day that would restore me to that former life
Would likewise make me the equal of the highest kings;

<vocab>anxieties, honors, wealth, friends, the masses, Death, of the Gods, peace, crime, kings</vocab>

<summary>The poet laments his current life in the city, describing it as a "living death" characterized by the hollow pursuit of wealth and honors, and contrasts it with the peaceful, virtuous companionship of his youth.</summary>
<keywords>ambition, city vs. country, public life, friendship, peace, negotium, social obligation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative from Poem XII, where the narrator (Aurelius) describes meeting the scholar Trifone Gabriele in the countryside. <header>BOOK II.</header>

...when the tree was green with leaves in early spring.
Arriving there by chance, I saw him <note>Referring to Trifone Gabriele, the "Venetian Socrates" mentioned on the previous page.</note> eagerly,
And he, in turn, received me with a joyful expression.
And having spoken much back and forth, 
As is the custom among friends when they have been long apart,
At last we both came to one point of conversation:
That we should speak of the learned men with whom
He himself had been in close contact in the meantime,
While he was wandering abroad through honorable cities.
Here, then, reviewing those whom he had visited everywhere—
Men who were indeed famous and of great name—
And placing you <note>Pamphilus, the author of this collection.</note> especially among them,
"Hey," he said, "Aurelius, I want you to know that you owe
More to Pamphilus alone than to all the others;
For he thinks you worthy of much praise,
And not only in speech, as he frequently does,
But he even celebrates you with the art of sweet song,
And including you in his learned <term>libellis: "little books," a modest term used by Roman poets like Catullus to describe their collections of polished, personal verse.</term>, he sings of you."
At this I said, "What kind of gentle kindness is this?
That one whom he has never spoken to, nor
Seen, he should consider worthy of honor as a Friend."
And returning thanks to you and to the messenger,
Finally I departed from him, wishing that I might soon
Gain possession of your <term>lucubrationibus: "night-labors," or literary works composed with great effort by lamplight.</term>.
And after several days had passed since then,
Behold, your long-awaited book is brought to me.
Taking it up immediately with an eager hand to be read,
This varied and learned poem, O Pamphilus...

<meta>This page continues the exchange from the previous section. While the prior page featured Aurelius recounting Trifone Gabriele's praise for Pamphilus, this page presents Pamphilus’s direct poetic response, followed by a new poem (Poem XIII) addressed to the scholar Hieronymus Bononius.</meta>

# IAMBICS <note>original: "IAMB." referring to Iambic trimeter, a metrical form often used in classical poetry for personal or satirical expression.</note>

My great honor of our age, immediately
I read with amazement how many things you have written of me:
And I give you many thanks everywhere.
And since it is only fair that I repay the favor in turn,
Although my talent may not be entirely equal to yours,
I have just sent these trimeters <note>original: "trimetros"; a line consisting of three pairs of iambic feet.</note> to you
As a pledge of my love toward you, to reveal
That I have always, before now, most highly cherished
Your mind, filled as it is with such wondrous gifts.
But now, I desire both to cultivate that mind and to pursue you
With as much love as befits a pure poet,
Whom a kind and friendly spirit has thus united.

### POEM XIII.

Even now we are leaving snowy Feltre <note>original: "Feltria"; a town in the Dolomites.</note>.
<margin>i</margin> Behold, Bononius, we are complying with your commands.
I shall bring my body back to you, dear pledges of a friend,
For my mind, having been left behind,
Has long been present with you, nor has it passed
A single day unmindful of any one of you.
Therefore, I shall return again with my other part,
Though it be the smaller <note>The "smaller part" refers to his physical body, contrasted with his "greater" mind/soul which never left his friends.</note>, and I shall remain whole

For a year, with three months added on
(for we had retired here for that very purpose),
we give you thanks; and for you, we desire and pray
to the heavenly gods for the greatest things we can.
That is: that you always stand open to the fair and the good,
but stay closed to the greedy and the wicked; and that you might
never wish to receive anyone more willingly or kindly
than one to whose heart the sacred choir
of the Muses <note>The nine Greek goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences.</note> is dear, whenever it happens that they come here
released from turbulent cares, so that in quiet
leisure <note>original: "ocio ignobili"; literally "ignoble leisure." For a Renaissance scholar, this meant a productive retreat from the "noble" but exhausting demands of public life and politics.</note> and among pleasant things, they may healthfully

## XV.

<margin>❧</margin>
From the mountains, the dire snow and rain
press down swiftly from afar—a turn of events
the dark sky, covered everywhere with turbulent
clouds, had long been warning.

### BOOK II.

...the wave flows back, unable to bear the force of the whitening foam,
just as he <note>Likely referring to the friend Bononius mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> fits the strings to sweet melodies—
with Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of music, poetry, and light, often invoked by Renaissance humanists as a patron of their work.</note> approving both the strings and the measures.
Friends from here and there call to me in great numbers,
nor do they approve of my delay; but as one who has
withdrawn for long enough and more, they pull,
carry, and drive me by their own right.
Why then, Castalius, should I linger any longer,
where I have no measure of time nor of leisure <term>leisure</term> <note>original: "ocii." To a Renaissance scholar, *otium* (leisure) was the productive time dedicated to study and writing, as opposed to *negotium*, the "non-leisure" of public business and chores.</note>,
since the powerful and impartial force of both
companionship and the place takes both away?
One can see the tiny birds immediately
retreat together and change their soil
as winter presses on. But we trifling little men <note>original: "homunculi." A diminutive term used to highlight human frailty or mortality in comparison to the instincts of nature.</note>
cannot be taught that which is innate in lesser creatures,
and so fail to follow the order of necessity
granted to things that bear their lot.
Do you perhaps rouse yourself for this reason? So that from here
you might devote yourself to the leisure of a healthy
and learned city, where you can thrive, and at the same time
be free for the arts, cultivating the sharpness of your
mind with good pursuits, as is your custom; by which
you bring great honor to yourself, your kin, and your country.
But I shall seek the streams of the clear Sile <note>The Sile is the river that flows through the city of Treviso.</note>,
where the breeze is warm and it is almost a continuous spring,
if you compare it to these frozen, snow-covered heights.
There, the city is filled with becoming graces,
and with men and manners of the most fitting kind...

*I have not sent you letters for a long time myself,*

# TO PIETRO BEMBO <note>Pietro Bembo (1470–1547) was a towering figure of the Italian Renaissance, a scholar, poet, and later a Cardinal.</note>, CONGRATU-
# LATIONS ON THE GOVERNORSHIP <note>original: "Praeturae." In the Venetian Republic, the Praetor (or Podestà) was the high-ranking official sent to govern subject cities like Verona.</note> OF VERONA
# ENTRUSTED TO HIS MOST ILLUSTRIOUS FATHER <note>Bernardo Bembo, Pietro’s father, was appointed as the Venetian governor of Verona in 1502.</note>.

## POEM XVI.

I myself have not sent letters to you for a long time,
<margin>4</margin> O Bembo, ever longed-for and always
clinging to the deep roots of my heart.
Perhaps you have not heard news of me from elsewhere either—
I who, hiding among the rugged Alps of Feltre,
have completed a year with three additional months added,
from where hardly anyone could tell you
what I was doing then, because I would give myself 
to very few indeed, always being at leisure for the noble arts alone;
seeking whether any hidden place might be found,
far removed from the frequent annoyance of citizens,
where, living an obscure life, I might behold
the sacred Choirs of the Muses <note>original: "Aonidum Choros." The Aonides are the Muses, named after Aonia, a district in Boeotia containing the Heliconian spring.</note> more closely—
those who loathe the ignorant common crowd, and are not visible
even to those poets whom the popular breeze carries.
But never to any retreats [did I offer]...

<summary>Girolamo Fracastoro begins a poem to his friend Pietro Bembo, apologizing for a long silence caused by his fifteen-month retreat in the mountains of Feltre, where he sought poetic inspiration away from the distractions of city life.</summary>
<keywords>Pietro Bembo, Feltre, Muses, poetic isolation, Verona, Bernardo Bembo, governor, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem addressed to Pietro Bembo from the previous page. The poet is finishing his reflections on his time in the Alps and expressing his joy at the news of Pietro's father, Bernardo, being appointed to a high political office in Verona.</meta>

BOOK II.

...though I was in such remote places, your celebrated name
still reached my ears, and I knew what studies
you were pursuing, and what honors of the fatherland
your father was continually performing.
Then, having congratulated you, as was fitting,
I prayed that future rewards might always follow,
which would be worthy of your lineage and of you all.
Now I hear of the honor recently given to your father,
how he has become the illustrious Praetor <note>The *Praetor* (also called the *Podestà*) was the high-ranking Venetian official sent to govern subject cities like Verona.</note> in an illustrious city.
I hope you are all well: your aged
grandmother, your excellent parents, your honest brother,
and you—whether it pleases you to travel there or to stay here;
but I pray that you all return to this place together
in good health: bringing back wealth, glory,
and honor, and that you and your father may undertake
the other greatest honors of the fatherland,
so that she [the country] may be worthy of you, and you of your Country.

## TO MARCO MAROCENO <note>Marco Marcoceno (likely a member of the noble Morosini family) was a Venetian patrician.</note>,

### A POEM IN PURE IAMBICS.
#### XVII.

How most wretchedly of all he wastes away, who loves—
if indeed you are held by a heavy love.

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem addressed to Marco Marcoceno, a Venetian patrician. The previous page introduced the theme that those who love suffer the most wretched of fates. This page elaborates on the volatile nature of Love (Amor) and the necessity of resisting its grip while one is still young and impressionable.</meta>

IAMBICS

This man will likewise seem blessed to you,
If the breeze of Cupid <term>Cupid: the Roman god of desire and erotic love, often depicted as a fickle force.</term> favors you.
In these matters, there is nothing to doubt: Love <term>Amor: the personification of passion.</term> either crushes
Or uplifts; it always prefers one extreme or the other,
Unable to maintain a middle ground.
You will therefore either grieve excessively, or be carried away
By too slight a joy; but fear also
And a sickly Desire will force you more sharply
To endure all that is bitter and cursed.
Realizing this yourself, beware of the danger while it is still brief,
Young man <note>original: *puer*; literally "boy," but here used as a term of address for a younger man, likely Marco Marcoceno.</note>, and do not allow the festering ulcer
To drive deeper or to settle for any longer.
If you guide something while it is still tender,
It will follow the hand readily. But if it soon hardens,
As is the custom, it will remain stuck and quite unmanageable.
A young mule <term>Hinnulus: literally a young mule or fawn; here used metaphorically for a stubborn, untrained creature.</term> will long retain
What it has once swallowed; nor will it, fearing lashes or threats,
Heed the jagged bits <term>Lupata: a "wolf-toothed" bit, an instrument with sharp points used for taming unruly horses.</term> as an obstinate beast of burden.

## FOR THE BOY

### FROM THE PURE IAMBICS.
### XVIII.

If any young man should burn with a heavy passion <note>original: *more*, likely a variant or error for *amore* (love), given the context of "burning" (ardeat).</note>,
<margin>a</margin>
He will have a means by which he can bear
The fiery burden, and a way to set a limit to the evil,
While Youth <term>Iuuenta: the personification of the prime of life.</term> drives him and this time of life allows it.

<summary>The poet warns Marco Marcoceno that love is a volatile force that destroys emotional stability, urging him to break the habit of passion before it "hardens" like an untrained animal. The page then begins a new section of iambic verse addressed to a "boy" on how to manage the burdens of youth.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Amor, passion, youth, iambic poetry, temperance, Marco Marcoceno</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page concludes poem XVIII, which contrasts how Love affects the young and the old, and begins poem XIX, focusing specifically on the plight of the elderly lover. It continues the advice to the Venetian patrician Marco Marcoceno.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

An old man cannot endure the power of fire itself,
But is like dry wood; he resists the initial flame
Less, and is more immediately consumed by it.
Nor, in his weakness, can he lift the heavy burden
Which fierce Love, piling it up continuously for a long time,
Forces him to undergo with trembling limbs.
A tender young man <note>original: *puer*; literally "boy," but referring to a youth or young man of noble standing.</note>, therefore, will love more safely;
He will set aside heavy desire whenever he wishes.
To him, a new Love will not be useless,
But the honorable power of his hidden nature will be awakened
And will joyfully lift its head,
As this new warmth burns the green youth more intensely.
And so, graceful habits will refine him,
Such that you would think he had come forth
With becoming graces <note>A reference to the Three Graces of mythology—Charm, Beauty, and Creativity.</note>. Or he will put on his armor with such elegance
That you would believe the God who rages against horses and men <note>Mars, the Roman god of war.</note>
Had instantly granted him Martian glory.
And then, rendered pleasing by many skills,
He will act and speak in such a way that you feel
Pallas <term>Pallas: another name for Athena, the goddess of wisdom, craft, and strategic warfare.</term> is teaching him
How to manage or relate his affairs.
A young man sprouts with as many blessings from love
As an old man is consumed by evils from love.

<sig>d</sig>

He who knows how to hold a measure in his own affairs:
For he is carried along with less force or impulse;
He grows less hot, having become more moderate.
Therefore, wounded by a light care of Love,
He loves, and he desires; and what he loves and desires, he burns for.
But a fire exists within this man, so that he may always burn:
Yet it does not scorch too much, and nevertheless it lasts long enough.
He himself knows what this is sooner than he did as a boy <note>original: *puer*; referring to the transition from early adolescence to a more mature youth.</note>.
And being prudent, he takes care that if he does anything improper,
He may hide it: which that other [the old man] cannot do, stirred
By the sharp heat of a youth that burns within him <note>The poet suggests that when an old man falls in love, he acts with the reckless intensity of a teenager, but without the physical stamina or social grace to carry it off.</note>,
Feeling more and more the heavy blows of the lashes.
Because of this, if any girl were not acting foolishly,
Avoiding the side of a vigorous <note>original: *virentis*; literally "green" or "blooming," a common metaphor for youth.</note> but treacherous young man,
She would prefer for herself a faithful, even if withered <note>original: *aridum*; literally "dry." In ancient medicine, youth was "moist" and old age was "dry."</note>, old man.

### CARMEN XX.

These things, Bononius, which you read, perhaps to you...

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical discussion from the previous page, concluding the sequence regarding the Old Man and the Boy. It transitions from a debate about love and age into a technical explanation of the iambic meters used, before introducing Poem XXI addressed to Chrysilla.</meta>

<header>BOOK II</header>

To Pancratius <note>A learned friend or associate of the author.</note>, a man of great learning,
They also seem to clash at first glance,
Because what the Old Man <note>original: *Senex*</note> says,
The Boy <note>original: *Puer*</note> not only denies, but perhaps
Boldly and even too impudently tries
To speak against; he mockingly ignores
The voice of his advisor, proud and trusting
More in himself. On the other hand,
The Old Man may seem so foolish that,
In the matters of Love, he prefers himself—
Fickle as he is—to the boy, as if it were 
Altogether fitting for one so withered to match one so blooming.
But you will not deny that many things 
Are accustomed to stand together in this way;
Things which, though at first they were far apart, 
Grow together in turn and produce a single whole.
So the great world itself, as much as it can, 
Appears as one, though born from many contraries.
This is what my trimeters <note>Poems written in iambic trimeter, a meter consisting of three pairs of iambic feet.</note> now demand for themselves:
Though they have put forward different opinions,
Let one thing stand out, and let it sound in harmony,
If any wish to look into the hidden meaning.
And if they achieve this less in their subject matter, 
At least they excel by the virtue of their poetic Foot;
For a single iamb <note>A metrical foot consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one.</note> runs swiftly through the whole 
Poem as it should—unless, tired at the very end,
The "Scazon" <note>original: *Scazon*; also known as the "limping iambic," a variant of the meter where the very last foot is reversed, creating a stumbling or "limping" rhythm.</note> should limp.

# TO CHRYSILLA, THAT SHE IS
## *an example to other maidens, which*
## *they themselves should follow.*

# POEM XXI.

<summary>This page concludes a philosophical and metrical discussion on the harmony of opposites, using the debate between an old man and a youth as a metaphor, before introducing a new poem dedicated to Chrysilla as a model of virtue.</summary>
<keywords>Pancratius, iambic trimeter, Scazon, harmony of opposites, Chrysilla, poetic meter, youth and old age</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page concludes Poem XXI, a tribute to a woman named Chrysilla, and begins Poem XXII, addressed to Daniel Rhenerius. The previous page discussed the technical aspects of iambic meter and the philosophical nature of "contraries" in poetry and the world, leading into the praise of Chrysilla's beauty and virtue.</meta>

<header>IAMBICS, BOOK II</header>

Just as the stars are not seen to shine,
Nor the sister of Phoebus <note>The Moon (Diana), sister of the sun god Apollo.</note>,
Perhaps even Phoebus <note>The Sun (Apollo).</note> himself, through whom all things gleam,
Would not shine for that man
To whom you—adorned with charming character 
Above all other women—do not appear beautiful.
Such a man knows nothing of what moderation is, or what modesty is,
Nor what true beauty is, O Chrysilla.
Do not delay in being an example to all,
Nor let the other women hold themselves back
From following you, a towering gift of the heavens
Bestowed upon mortals.
As one goddess walks in heaven, she who surpassed
The other two in the judgment of Paris <note>Refers to the Myth of the Judgment of Paris, where Paris chose Venus (Aphrodite) as the most beautiful over Juno and Pallas Athena.</note>;
And though the other goddesses keep their distance,
With Juno and Pallas Athena so consenting;
So may a great troop of virgins, always following you,
Be gathered around you,
And let them draw from you such a blessed glory,
As the stars draw their light from Venus <note>original: *Venere*; referring both to the goddess of beauty and the planet/morning star.</note>;

# TO DANIEL RHENERIUS <note>A member of the Rhenier (Renier) family, a prominent Venetian noble house.</note>
# A VENETIAN NOBLEMAN.

## POEM XXII.

My *Nelphus* <note>Likely the title of a specific literary work or dialogue authored by the writer.</note> has been completed for me, most learned Daniel Rhenerius, in the very manner you had commanded.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic praise of Chrysilla, comparing her virtue and beauty to the goddess Venus, and begins a new poem dedicated to the Venetian noble Daniel Rhenerius regarding a completed work titled Nelphus.</summary>
<keywords>Chrysilla, Phoebus, Venus, Paris, Daniel Rhenerius, Venice, virtue, beauty, Nelphus, iambic poetry</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Poem XXII, addressed to the Venetian nobleman Daniel Rhenerius, following the completion of a literary work titled "Nelphus." It then transitions into Poem XXIII, a dramatic dialogue between the mythological ferryman Charon and the character Nelphus.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

Whether I have achieved what you desired,
you must see for yourself, you who are most skilled in all things.
You are forever turning over Greek models,
nor do you ever set aside Latin works with your diligent hand:
so great is your reading by night and by day. <note>This is an allusion to Horace’s *Ars Poetica*: "Turn over the Greek models by night, turn them over by day."</note>
I found it sufficient in this matter to follow your custom.
But it is not for me to pass judgment 
on whether this text has been properly woven 
with pleasing charms, or whether some poorly inserted
end of the sacred thread <note>original: *staminis sacri*; a metaphor comparing the writing of poetry to weaving a fabric.</note> strays within, 
since it was not guided by the same right hand.
You must judge whether anything should be moved,
added, or corrected; or whether instead
whatever I have written should be given to the sponge— 
as the fierce Ajax has already fallen upon the sponge. <note>A literary joke referencing the Emperor Augustus, who, when asked how his tragedy *Ajax* was coming along, replied that his Ajax "had fallen upon a sponge"—meaning he had rubbed out/deleted the entire play.</note>

## POEM XXIII.

### CHARON AND NELPHUS.

<margin>Charon</margin> Bah! Who is this? Who weighs down the boat so heavily,
<margin>a</margin> and occupies all the benches alone, lying there 
covered in armor, and holding a spear in his hand?
Ha! He snores—good heavens, what is this? Nor has he touched Lethe, 
the eternal fountain of sleep and 
underworld forgetfulness. <note>In Greek mythology, Lethe is the river of oblivion; souls drink from it to forget their earthly lives.</note> Ah, I recognize him: he sleeps, and has not yet
completed the path of life granted by the fates.
But he must be awakened by me from his sleep immediately.

<summary>The author concludes a dedicatory poem to Daniel Rhenerius by deferring to his editorial judgment, then begins a mythological dialogue where Charon, the ferryman of the dead, discovers a living, armed man (Nelphus) asleep on his boat.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dramatic dialogue of Poem XXIII between Charon and the armored newcomer, Nelphus. On the previous page, Charon was surprised to find a snoring, heavily armed man in his boat who had not yet drunk from the waters of forgetfulness.</meta>

# I A M B. <note>original: *IAMB.*; referring to iambic meter, the rhythm often used in classical drama and biting satire.</note>

Lest the boat toss—but hey, what is this? Hey you!
What? He cannot be awakened; he continues to snore.
Greater force must be used here, for this was achieved 
by a trick. And first, I must lift his head 
from this helmet. Good heavens, the triple-jawed gape of a dog! <note>original: *triplex canis rictus*; likely a description of a crest or decoration on the helmet resembling Cerberus, the three-headed hound of Hades.</note>
How foul the odor! And how effective is the power within!
For it nearly caught me off guard, and a wicked sleep seized me.
The smooth spear must also be taken from his hand, lest
he does something violent, being impiously ignorant.
The skiff <note>original: *faselus*; a light, fast boat.</note> must also be steered carefully to the shore.
This shrub, which is called *Marathron* by the Greeks, <note>original: *Marathron*; Fennel. In ancient herbalism, fennel was used to clear the senses and counter poisons or magical stupors.</note>
will be plucked by me to counter this black venom.
But oh, we have arrived now, and we have done all
that is necessary: for he, having woken up, rises,
and threateningly he boldly provokes his fellow.
But he must be questioned, so that he might reveal the cause
why he comes here armed and in such a stupor.
Hey you! I say to you again: who are you? And from where did you come?
<margin>Nel.</margin> Do I fight in earnest? My name is Nelphus.
I have just laid low a most brave and savage man.
Now I shall send another, who stands exposed, to his ruin.
<margin>Ch.</margin> Whom will you ruin? <margin>Nel.</margin> The one who stands over there opposite—
<margin>Ch.</margin> There is nothing there; you are being foolish, you simpleton. <margin>Nel.</margin> Nothing? You're babbling.
Another waits, whom I shall immediately, with the first
blow, lay out for you in the middle of the arena. But he approaches!
And I apply the spur! <note>Nelphus believes he is still on horseback in the middle of a battle.</note> But alas, wretched me, where is the helmet?
Where is it? Where is my horse? Where is the spear? Where am I being swept headlong?
Where am I, truly? None of my men are here; all—

<summary>Charon manages to wake the mysterious soldier Nelphus by using fennel to counter a magical slumber, only for Nelphus to wake in a delusional state, believing he is still in the heat of battle. The scene highlights the confusion of a soul arriving in the underworld while still clinging to his earthly violence.</summary>

<header>BOOK II. MA I</header>

It is clear I am a miserable man, and I do not know where I am.
<margin>Charon</margin> Recover your senses. And first, tell me what you have done. I myself will ensure you know the rest later.
<margin>Nelphus</margin> At the worthy seat of ancient Antenor <note>original: *uetusti Antenoris... sedes*; refers to the city of Padua, which legend says was founded by the Trojan prince Antenor.</note> an equestrian game <note>original: *equestris... ludus*; a tournament or jousting match.</note> was being held. From there, I departed as the victor, not knowing how I just came to this place—unless perhaps I am now being tested by a deceitful sleep, similar to what is known to have happened to me at Verona. For there, while I sat upon my horse, resting a beam-like spear <note>original: *hastam trabalem*; a lance as thick as a wooden beam.</note> in my arm, all my strength suddenly left me, so that I stood motionless in the middle of the forum. They said I had drunk a cup of dire stupor, powerful with a heavy juice. Such is the power of a wicked mind’s malice.
<margin>Charon</margin> Wait a little, while I bring forth secret words and write these signs for the God of the Styx <note>original: *stygis Deo*; referring to Pluto or Hades, the ruler of the underworld.</note> in the dust: soon you shall hear what misfortunes have befallen you.
<margin>Nelphus</margin> I wait, and I gladly lend you my ear.
<margin>Charon</margin> O pitiable, and no less honored Old Man: for you tell the truth, and indeed you would have been the victor, were it not for the envious fraud hindering you. That same evil trickery now rages against you as it did once before; although that first time it was accomplished by a drink, this time it was done by a scent. For inside your helmet, a sponge is soaked all around, infected with the foul juice of Hemlock <note>original: *Cicutae*; a poisonous plant used as a sedative or toxin.</note> or poppy, and other things in which sleep resides. And I fear lest that evil now creeps further; for from here, a fatal path offers itself to you.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page. Although the previous page identified the knight as Nelphus, the vocabulary list on this page identifies the speaker "Nel." as Neleus. <header>BOOK II.</header>

They will immediately compel the new guest to be afraid:
Until they have put you to the test,
To see whether you come worthy to be admitted among them.
You, go forward against them, and do not fear the light shadows:
For indeed they cannot touch you, nor can they be touched.
**Neleus** Do empty images <note>original: *imagines*; the ghosts or shades of the departed.</note> thus play then?
And does the flash of armor shine?
And do horses wander everywhere through the meadows and fields,
Set loose in the joyful grass?
O how blessed I would be, if a place were given to me,
Even if it were the very last among them.
For it is better to be the lowest of the highest,
Than the head of the lowest kind. <note>A common philosophical sentiment: Neleus would rather be the least among heroes than the greatest among common men.</note>
**Charon** It is so, for you are wise; and as for me, though I am numbered among the Gods,
I would rather drive this humble boat <note>original: *Ratem*.</note> with my pole,
Than rule as a King over fleeting mortals.
**Neleus** But although I myself rejoice to be added to these men,
Who once by their deeds brought forth a splendid glory
For themselves and for their country,
I would nevertheless now return to the world above,
And there I would wish to endure whatever fate,
And leave this kingdom to you,
And whatever it is for which you exalt yourselves
So excessively and vainly.
**Charon** Still stained by the filth you left behind,
You think and speak of human things; and not yet purified,
Wretched man, you have not yet put on another way of life.
But when you are able to grasp divine things in your soul,

<summary>Charon explains that the heroic shades in the underworld will test Neleus's courage, while Neleus marvels at the sight of ghostly horses and armor. <meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 112 between the ferryman Charon and the knight Neleus as they cross the river to the underworld. Charon is explaining the metaphysical nature of the soul and why the afterlife is more "real" than the world of the living.</meta>

### IAMBIC VERSE

If you compare them with human things, immediately the distance <note>original: *intercæpedo*; the gap or interval</note> between them will appear to you so prominent:
That between those things which are nothing,
And those which endure forever, there is the greatest difference.
For what I had said before—that they are not harmed,
Nor are the **Inhabitants of the Underworld** <note>original: *Inferos*</note> themselves hurt—understand it this way:
They are subject to no dangers,
And those who are good are subject to no labors.
But they truly *exist*, since they can neither suffer,
Nor perish, nor waver in any way,
Since they enjoy an eternal age, and the heavens,
With the stars always shining fortunately upon them.
And know that you <insert>mortals</insert> do not truly "be," but only "seem" to be.
For how can you truly exist? You who always pass away
In a perpetual flux? For indeed, only that which remains
Can truly be said to **exist**.
Those things that are eternal, are the things that *are*. Compared to these,
You would truly say that everything else is absent, and does not exist.
Therefore, when you arrive here, you first leave behind
That which was decaying; then your merits are diligently
Weighed in an equal balance, and to everyone are given
The rewards of happiness, or of misery.
**Neleus** You tell of wondrous things; and I give you thanks:
Because you have lifted me from my error;
Me, whom you have willingly persuaded with holy speech
So that I might approach these blessed seats.
But while you continue speaking these excellent things,
Behold, we have now nearly reached the **Shore**.

<meta>catchword: And,</meta>

<summary>Charon explains the philosophical distinction between the "flux" of mortal life and the eternal reality of the afterlife, concluding as the boat reaches the shore. He argues that only eternal things truly exist, while human life is merely a passing shadow.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, metaphysics, Charon, Neleus, judgment, eternal life, philosophical dialogue</keywords>

BOOK II.

*And, as the ancient proverb goes,*
*A **traveler** <note>original: *Vector*; literally one who is carried, here referring to a passenger</note> who is a witty companion is as good as a carriage,*
*We have beguiled the long path of the swamp*
*With these conversations.*
**Charon** *Descend, where the honor of a noble fate*
*Leads you, proud among the best of **Heroes**.*
*I myself shall return immediately to the remaining shades,*
*Who wait for me to cross over: farewell.*
**Neleus** *I will do so as you command, gladly. And you also,*
*Remembering us, farewell, Charon;*

### TO LORD RHENERIUS, CONCERNING THE WOLF:
### THAT IS, CONCERNING THE VIOLENCE OF FATE.

## POEM XXIII.

<margin>r</margin>
*Rhenerius, a massive **Wolf** <note>Used here as an allegory for death or the cruelty of Fate</note> wandering these fields,*
*Frequently steals prey secretly from the shepherds:*
*Often he also snatches it by force,*
*Placing it upon his back, even while they watch.*
*Thus he devours the wretched flock everywhere.*
*He is always thirsty for blood, and perishes from hunger.*
*Recently, by the waters of the clear **Sile** <note>original: *Silis*; a river in the Veneto region of Northern Italy</note>,*
*Ligdus was raising a lamb intended for me,*
*Which was to be the hope of the future flock.*
*Busy with this, the wolf first prepared to take her secretly.*
*But after he saw that he played this game in vain,*
*Attacking with all his might, he snatched the little one:*
*While the leader of the betrayed flock cried out fruitlessly.*

<header>IAMBIC METERS.</header>

*All the country youth then shuddered,*
*Pitying Ligdus, and me, who had fallen so quickly*
*From such great hope. But I, struck by this heavy*
*Misfortune, and immediately mindful of my own small flock,*
*Lest by neglect I should give that too to those ravenous mouths:*
*Suddenly climbing the familiar seats of the steep ridge,*
*I seek the cave of my former peace.*
*There, by the bubbling stream of **water** <note>original: *Lymphae*; used poetically here for pure spring water</note> sacred*
*To the **Nymphs** <note>Nature spirits associated with mountains and waters</note> who love hidden retreats,*
*Safely pasturing my flock in the high mountains,*
*I console my vain hope with the sharp sound of the **flute** <note>original: *tibia*; a common reed instrument for shepherds</note>:*
*Content with a fair lot, which my own mind—*
*A scorner of luxury—has prepared for me in a sober fashion:*
*Since I lack nothing, yet nothing is left over for me,*
*For **Abundance** <note>original: *Copia*; personified here as a goddess of plenty who provides exactly what is needed</note> thus provides both states frugally.*

Near to that spirit of a great soul <note>Referring to her husband, Ferdinand I</note>,
You will nourish your mind with a thousand blessed joys.
But he, gazing upon your golden character,
And how much **Venus** <note>The goddess of beauty, representing the Queen's physical grace</note> shines in your body,
