You will watch how he now makes diverse nations equal
Under his laws, and how he now enjoys golden
Peace, and now rages in fierce war,
And you will be carried away ten thousand times by joys.
He will overthrow the treacherous **Turks** <note>original: *turcas*; the Ottoman Empire, the primary rival of the Habsburg and Hungarian crowns</note>, and as victor
He will drive them far from the homeland, the faith, and our goods;
You will be a partner both in his counsel and his **Trophy** <note>original: *Trophæi*; a monument to or the glory of military victory</note>:
And a hundred thousand joys will seize you both.
You will bring forth offspring laden with the highest goods:
You will see Kings of royal blood,
Sweet little boys; soon, as youths, gifted with
Virtue, such as uniquely befits those born
From the ancient lineage of Kings; and then, more often,
There will be a thousand thousand joys for you.
These things the **Praetor** <note>The chief administrative officer or governor</note> of the city, **Salomon** <note>Niccolò Salomon, the Venetian official governing Treviso at the time</note>, and the
Whole city, eager for your arrival, pray to the heavens,
And the city desires its prayers to reach the Gods in this way,
As it pours them out for you from a dedicated heart;

And endowed with great skill in various matters,
Do not wonder if, by chance, these verses cohere aptly
With the former ones, and harmonize with the **Graces** <note>The Charites, goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity</note>,

The whole City had wished for it; all good men
Had desired that this opportunity be granted to me:
One that would provide a constant and decent addition
Of benefits to my modest affairs.
Perhaps I would have entered upon a more elegant life,
If I had achieved that. But nevertheless,
Though this did not turn out successfully for me, a heavy
Fate has taken nothing from my former peace;

<header>IAMBICS</header>

Will you move against me? And will you, unjust and unmindful of law, seek this liver <note>In Renaissance medical and poetic theory, the liver was considered the seat of passion and desire, often the target of Cupid’s arrows.</note> from my throat? Law commands that no one bring weapons against a captive. Alas, defeated, I have often surrendered my hand to you. And I myself have revealed the ulcer a thousand times, rendered rotten everywhere by the passing days. No medicine <note>original: "Medela"</note> soothes it, and the seething heat <note>original: "Ardor"</note> within feels no relief. Instead, I am burned continually; nor did it seem enough to you to strike an honest breast with impious iron: but you burn me, applying the torches of a dreadful fire. Yet I, wretched, bear all these things willingly; and desiring them—who would believe this? And cherishing the fire, I turn within the fire, just as they say a certain beast <note>Referring to the mythical salamander, which was famously believed to live in and thrive upon fire.</note> often dwells in a fiery furnace and does not suffer from the heat, but rather goes through the midst of the flames, so that it might thrive the more.

## TO HIERONYMUS BONONIUS <note>Girolamo Bologni (1454–1517) was a noted humanist poet and lawyer from Treviso, a friend to many scholars of this period.</note>

### POEM XXIX.

Go now, Bononius, I pray, laugh if you are wise:
<margin>r</margin> For I myself laugh—I, for whom it would be a sin to laugh, if I wished to feel those things the common crowd usually feels. Nor should you wonder if he breaks faith with me—he who cannot stand by his promises, nor can long remain consistent with his own words. Know that this is the custom of that rank <note>original: "ordinis"; the author likely refers to a specific social or professional class, perhaps courtiers or fickle patrons.</note> of men.

So that they may carry one thing in their mouth and another in their breast:
Nor do they care that they speak the same as they feel.
Instead, it is always fitting for them to speak and act 
Treacherously in all things, for such is that wicked race of men:
Whom Lycaon <note>In Greek mythology, Lycaon was a king of Arcadia who was transformed into a wolf by Zeus as punishment for his cruelty and impiety; he serves here as a symbol of human savagery and deceit.</note> has now left behind, as he departed

Laugh, therefore, and let all our friends laugh equally:
But let this laughter target the misfortunes of mortals,
And let it stir up empty cares: and mocking 
Whatever this is, which we in our madness so approve,
Consider all of it with me as something entirely unhealthy;

### POEM XXX.

Returning from there, I often think to myself,
<margin>i</margin> How kindly each of you received me,
My friend Porcius, and you, honest Angaranus:
How gladly I, in turn, visited you:
And the many joys that were ours.
How we spent every day and almost every night
In most pleasant conversation with one another.

->I A M B I C S<-

*You have read many of my writings which, with the Muses <note>In Greek mythology, the nine Muses are the goddesses of inspiration in literature, science, and the arts; their "favor" implies poetic success.</note> favoring them, will grant me a name among future generations. In turn, I presented several things to you, which you yourselves heard with your own kind ears. You ensured that I spoke then with the most learned men, and that I saw all those endowed with every skill—whether it was some work perfected by their learned hands, or some enduring and masterfully crafted monument of that illustrious city <note>Vicenza.</note>.*
*Therefore, my mind returns to you as often as it can; for it wanders freely wherever it wishes. If you desire, I shall put forward the cause of this blessing: the only one granted to mortals amidst their many evils.*
*For since this mind was exempt by law from death, and unstained by the filth of a sordid mass <note>In Platonic philosophy, the "mass" (molis) refers to the physical body, which is seen as a heavy, corrupting influence on the pure spirit.</note>, having been established thus for the High Prince <note>original: "Principi"; here referring to the Creator or the Supreme Being.</note>, the good Gods then first took up this good thing, and soon placed around it a decaying and mortal body, adding also a second, perishable kind of soul <note>The author is describing the Platonic theory of the "tripartite" or "dual" soul, where the rational mind is immortal, but the emotional part of the soul is mortal and tied to the body.</note>, in whose breast every emotion lies hidden.*
*For here, pleasure is the greatest bait <note>original: "esca"; a common philosophical metaphor suggesting that pleasure lures humans into sin like a fish to a hook.</note> for evils, as is pain, which calls back and detains mortals by force and fervor, keeping them far from their duty. Here too are audacity and fear—two flighty and reckless advisors who counsel wrongly. Unbridled anger is also present, and empty hope. By whose fury the latter [soul] destroys all things,*

And it takes away the sharp judgment of a good mind.
But the other <note>Likely referring to grief or another emotion mentioned on the previous page.</note> undermines the constancy of the soul,
while it repeatedly makes us too credulous.
Therefore, carrying around a spirit subject
to death together with these—and with Love, who conquers all,
along with Sense, which is far removed from reason—
they <note>The Gods or the Creator.</note> established it as something purer than the body itself,
so that the divine mind might cling with a tighter bond,
tied to the sickly body.
But lest mortality touch that pure, eternal,
and vivid mind by its proximity or its situation,
they separated them into distinct locations:
dividing the head from the chest by the neck,
they placed the mortal kind of soul below the heart <note>original: "praecordiis"; refers to the diaphragm or the internal organs of the chest, traditionally seen as the seat of the "lower" emotional soul.</note>,
and situated the mind at the very summit <note>The head or brain.</note>.
There, always stirred by its own motion,
it flies wherever it pleases with speed;
no gap of time or place causes any delay,
but it is present everywhere at once, always free,
transforming itself into all forms as it desires.
Unless I am mistaken, these are the excerpts of Platonic doctrine <note>This refers to the philosophy of Plato, specifically the "tripartite soul" described in the *Timaeus*, which separates the immortal reason in the head from the mortal passions in the body.</note>
provided to you here as briefly as possible;
perhaps they should be read with more eager study,
since they were brought forth by so great an author.

->### IAMBICS <note>original: "IAMB."; refers to the iambic meter used in this poem, often associated with a more direct or biting tone than lyrical meters.</note><-

**Now the powerful favor secretly leaves me,**
<margin>1</margin> Which you, O Goddess <note>The Muse.</note>, had breathed into me:
Since I began to play the jests of minor poetry <note>original: "minuti carminis"; refers to light, playful, or short-form verse.</note>
At your bidding.
**But now, moving a greater work yourself,**
You rouse us with a greater force.
**And you advance your step a little further than before:**
And pull us along as we follow.
**Now we are not led through the depths of the valleys,**
Nor do we crawl prone upon the ground.
**But we climb the great ridge of the sacred mountain** <note>Likely referring to Mount Parnassus or Helicon, the traditional homes of the Muses, representing the difficulty of high art or complex subject matter.</note>
By a winding, panting path.
**And now we seem to gradually approach**
Very close beneath the high summit:
**Where the ground is not seen shining with flowers,**
Nor the vine leafy with buds:
**But rather prominent rocks, and oak groves**
That cling to the ruined cliffs.
**We are not stirred here by the beat of the sweet lyre,**
Nor by the plaintive voice of the flute <note>The lyre and flute symbolize lyric and elegiac poetry, often concerned with love and emotion.</note>.
**Here we do not play the games of a young lover,**
Or of a lovely girl.
**Or whatever stubborn pleasure might have trifled**
With the easy senses of mortals.
**But neither does the melodious breath**
Of clanging trumpets <note>original: "Tubarum"; the trumpet is the symbol of Epic poetry and themes of war.</note> ring in our ears.
**Nor does that Martian heat <note>Refers to Mars, the god of war; themes of battle and heroism.</note> now seize us,**
Nor the excellence of the highest song:

**BOOK II.**

But here, this gentle, flowery kind of style
Detains us, following after that other:
By which Nature might be called the parent of all things:
The things which the rich earth brings forth within,
Yet does not release from her hidden womb,
Unless human strength extracts them.
By this law and art, the greatest of <term>Poet-Prophets</term> <note>original: "Vatum"; a *vates* was considered a divinely inspired singer or seer, higher in status than a mere "poeta."</note> sings
Of those things which the earth brings forth to the surface:
By which she feeds and nourishes living creatures,
And adorns all things with fruits.
We strive to emulate him; it is a joy simply to follow him;
You, O Goddess <note>The Muse.</note>, grant that we may reach the goal;

### TO THE BOOK.

### IN PURE IAMBICS. <note>original: "EX SOLIS IAMBIS"; refers to a poem written entirely in iambic meters, traditionally used for direct, honest, or sometimes biting speech.</note>

### POEM XXXII.

Go, my beautiful creation, born to me with a sober mind:
<margin>l</margin> To be held by me as a token of pious devotion:
I pray for everlasting honor for you among those who come after:
May the kind <term>Muse</term> <note>original: "camœna"; the Roman name for the Muses, the goddesses of inspiration.</note> make this certain:
May you be read, therefore, for as long as you possibly can:
And let a just fame always resound from your pages:
And while your Poet still survives—if it be allowed—
May you often pass through the mouths of learned men.
I myself, being of sound mind and careful <note>original: "pugens"; likely a transcription for *prudens* (prudent/careful), commonly paired with *sanus* (sound of mind).</note>, 
Would not wish to desire or promise a grander gift;
Nor can anything greater than this be given to you;

->THE END.<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new collection within the volume: the "Sermones" (Discourses or Satires) of Giovanni Aurelio Augurello. The previous page concluded a book of shorter poems (likely his *Carmina*) with a valediction to the book itself. We now transition from the shorter iambic and lyric forms to the more philosophical and conversational "Sermo" style, modeled after the Roman poet Horace. The catchword "Vt" on the previous page's final (not shown) line would lead into the text here.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK OF THE
# DISCOURSES OF GIOVANNI
# AURELIO AUGURELLO,
# TO GIACOMO GABRIELE,
# A VENETIAN
# NOBLEMAN.

## THE FIRST DISCOURSE.

<margin>h</margin>
These poems, with which I sometimes addressed sweet friends,
Imitating the character of pure speech—
To whom rather, now that they have gathered into one body,
Should I grant them, Gabriele, than to you as a mindful gift?
You, who so often are accustomed to seek out our playful trifles:
As if you could draw something forth from these light follies:
Which might relieve an afflicted mind with a strong remedy,
If ever it is burned within by the fires of vices
It has taken in, and the help of virtue wanders far away.
Yet you do this not because you lack warnings, but to ensure
That you adorn yourself with the learned discourses of noble <term>Poet-Prophets</term>; <note>original: "vatum"; Augurello uses the term for divinely inspired poets to elevate the moral purpose of the work.</note>
But because those things suited to morals are dear to your heart.
To one whom virtue pleases, it is easy to apply himself to virtue;
But he who chooses vice does not abstain from it: and the wicked man,
By his own will, seeks out the causes and the fuel for his shameful deeds.

<summary>This page serves as the title and dedication for the first book of Augurello's "Sermones," addressed to the Venetian patrician Giacomo Gabriele. The opening lines establish the work as a collection of moral and conversational poems intended to act as a "remedy" for the soul's vices.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, Giacomo Gabriele, Sermones, Venice, Humanism, Moral Philosophy, Virtue and Vice, Satires</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening discourse of Book I of Augurello’s *Sermones*. Having just dedicated the work to Giacomo Gabriele, the poet now defines his style and moral intent by contrasting his approach with the great Roman satirists.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

Just as everyone desires to practice what they know, and does not stretch beyond
What is familiar: on the contrary, what they do not do, they hate.
You read these things constantly yourself, and also have them always on your lips—
Those words which that most learned man <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace, whose "Satires" (or *Sermones*) serve as the primary model for this work.</note>, grave yet wise among his
Laughing companions, once used to stir up:
So that he might play around the very heart-strings, hiding there, and so that
He might uncover any vice that might be present; and then guide it
Back toward the better in a gentlemanly way <note>original: "civili more"; meaning a refined, social, or non-aggressive manner characteristic of a "civil" citizen.</note>, not in that fashion
By which most others drive out vices—attacking them with
Naked insults and pursuing them openly.
Persius follows this custom, but the man of Aquinum <note>Juvenal, a Roman satirist born in Aquinum, famous for his harsh, biting critiques of Roman society.</note> does so more often,
So that they report misdeeds publicly and betray names.
But I labor to season all things with a light laugh:
So that the bitter things which are infused may lie hidden beneath the sweet:
And as if a bristling thorn might lie beneath tender rosebeds.
Yet this is not so that we might intend to establish a <term>Satire</term> <note>original: "satyra"; here referring to the specific Roman genre of moralizing verse.</note>, nor that
We might strive to pluck at anyone's impure morals.
But rather so that, while I attempt to play with the cares of men in this way,
I may learn to compose myself at least, if not others:
And take care above all to lead an innocent life,
Free from anxieties, and full of modest
Joy: through which I may lay aside whatever burden is on the mind.
This is why we once fitted rhythms to the lyre strings:
And filled tender ears with sweet song.
But now, a very rare kind of song—complete <term>Iambics</term> <note>original: "Iambos"; a meter traditionally used for invective or stinging poetry, which Augurello here claims to be reviving in a "pure" ancient form.</note>,
Which in ancient times scarcely one or two had published,
And even then but a few (let heavy envy be absent from my words)—
I might perhaps dare to attempt with an effort that is not in vain.

<vocab>Persius, Juvenal (the Aquinian), satire, Iambics, song, to practice, vices, with a laugh, modest Joy</vocab>

<summary>Augurello defines his poetic mission as a "gentle" satirist, explicitly choosing the playful, subtle style of Horace over the aggressive attacks of Persius and Juvenal. He frames his writing as a tool for personal moral refinement and a revival of rare ancient iambic meters.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book I of Augurello’s *Sermones*. It contains the formal dedication to the Venetian nobleman Giacomo Gabriele, establishing the work’s purpose as a collection of moral yet "light" poetic conversations modeled after classical traditions.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK OF DISCOURSES BY GIOVANNI AURELIO AUGURELLO, TO GIACOMO GABRIELE, A VENETIAN PATRICIAN.

## THE FIRST DISCOURSE.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;<margin>h</margin> These poems, with which I sometimes addressed sweet friends—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Verses imitating the feeling of pure speech—
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To whom rather, now that they have come together into one volume <note>original: "corpus"; literally "body," here referring to the collected poems joined into a single work.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Should I give them, Gabriele, as a mindful gift?
You, who so often are accustomed to seek out my playful trifles <note>original: "lusus"; refers to light, occasional poetry common in the Renaissance.</note>,
As if you could draw out from such light toys
Something to soothe an afflicted mind with a strong medicine,
Whenever it is scorched within by the fires of vices
It has let in, and the help of virtue wanders far away.
Yet it is not because you lack guidance that you do this, so that you might adorn yourself
With the learned discourses of noble poets;
But because those things which are suited to character are dear to your heart.
For him who is pleased by virtue, it is easy to devote himself to virtue;
But he who is pleased by vice does not abstain from it, and the wicked man even
Seeks out the causes and the fuel for shameful deeds.

<summary>In this opening dedication, Augurello presents his "Discourses" to Giacomo Gabriele, describing his poetry as "light trifles" that nevertheless contain moral medicine for the soul. He argues that while virtuous people seek out poetry to refine their character, the wicked only seek further justification for their vices.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, Giacomo Gabriele, Venice, Renaissance poetry, moral philosophy, virtue and vice, Sermones, Neo-Latin</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Augurello’s *Sermones* (Discourses) to Giacomo Gabriele. The previous page concluded with the observation that while the virtuous seek out poetry to improve themselves, the wicked seek only to justify their vices. This page further defines Augurello’s poetic style by contrasting it with the harsh Roman satirists Persius and Juvenal.</meta>

->### BOOK I.<-

Just as what one knows, one desires to practice, and does not strive beyond what they are accustomed to; on the other hand, what one does not do, one hates. 
You yourself read constantly, and always have on your lips, those weighty things which once the most learned man <note>original: "doctissimus"; referring to the Roman poet Horace, whom Augurello takes as his primary model for these "Discourses."</note>, wise among his laughing companions, used to discuss: how he might play around the hidden parts of the heart, and how he might uncover if there were any vice, and lead it toward something better in a certain civil manner—not in that way by which most people attack vices, so that they assault them with bare insults and pursue them openly. 

Persius follows this harsher custom, but the man from Aquinum <note>original: "Aquinâs"; referring to the satirist Juvenal, born in Aquinum, who was famous for his biting and direct attacks on individuals.</note> fulfills it more frequently, so that they report faults openly and reveal names. But I labor to season everything with light laughter, so that the bitter things being poured out may lie hidden within the sweet, and a bristling thorn might lie beneath tender rosebeds.

Yet it is not my intention to plan the composition of a satire <note>term: "satyra"; a genre of Roman poetry used to critique social vices. Augurello distinguishes his "Discourses" from the more aggressive Roman satires.</note>, nor that we should strive to pluck at anyone's impure character. But rather, while I attempt to play with the cares of men in this way, I may learn to compose myself at least, if not others. I wish to take care to lead a life that is primarily innocent, free from anxieties, and full of modest joy, by which I might lay aside any burden of the mind.

Hence it is that we once fitted rhythms to the lyre-strings, and filled tender ears with sweet song. But now, perhaps I might dare to attempt with no vain effort (let heavy envy be absent from my words) a very rare song—complete iambic verses <note>term: "Iambos"; a specific meter in classical poetry often associated with invective, though Augurello aims to use them with more refinement.</note>—of which in ancient times scarcely one or another had published even a few.

<sig>g</sig>

<summary>Augurello defines his poetic mission as "civil" and gentle, modeling his work after Horace’s subtle social commentary rather than the harsh, naming-and-shaming style of Juvenal or Persius. He explains that his poetry serves as a tool for his own moral self-composure and a way to find "modest joy" amidst life's anxieties.</summary>

<keywords>Horace, Juvenal, Persius, satire, poetic meter, iambics, moral philosophy, self-improvement, Giacomo Gabriele, Venice</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Augurello concludes his dedication to Giacomo Gabriele by mentioning Giacomo's brother, Trypho. He then begins the second "Discourse" addressed to Bertutius Lambertus of Treviso, shifting the focus to the vanity of human ambitions and the "empty cares" of mortal life.</meta>

->OF THE DISCOURSES.<-

These verses Trypho <note>Tryphon Gabriele, a prominent Venetian humanist and brother to Giacomo Gabriele, the work's primary dedicatee.</note>, my excellent brother, will read and approve: just as once he did not disdain to possess my Lyric poems, but wished to examine them and compare them with those greatest poets <note>Referring to the classical Roman poets like Horace and Virgil.</note>, whose footsteps I follow, always adoring them. I view them from afar, content to remain below them, and am delighted by staying fixed on that single gaze. He will walk through the high peaks of the Aonian <note>Aonia is a region in Greece, home to Mount Helicon and the Muses. To walk its "high peaks" implies writing lofty, epic, or "high" poetry.</note> hill; you, with me, will enter caves hidden in a secret valley, willingly covered with ivy and green moss. Here the grove and the clear springs know their own spirits; and the easy-going Muses were not ashamed to visit these low places.

## TO BERTUTIUS LAMBERTUS, PRECENTOR <note>original: "PRIM. TARV."; likely *Primicerius Tarvisinus*, the chief official or precentor of the cathedral in Treviso (Tarvisium).</note> OF TREVISO.
### That men do not seek the end they ought to.

## DISCOURSE II.

Often, Bertutius, with laughter suppressed in my chest,
<margin>5</margin> I marvel at mortals tossed about by empty cares.
Sometimes—so that no one can turn it into a fault against me—hardly fearing, if I care to permit my wit anything, I glide down there with such loosened reins <note>A common classical metaphor for giving one's creative or critical impulses free rein.</note> that I am scarcely able to restrain rising bursts of laughter <note>original: "Cachinnos"; refers to loud, unrestrained, or mocking laughter.</note>. For as often as I examine in my mind where our life's path tends, and I see things looking toward a nearly opposite mark...

<meta>catchword: Everything (Omnia)</meta>

<summary>Augurello concludes his preface by contrasting his humble, "lowly" poetic style with loftier works, then begins a critique of human vanity addressed to Bertutius Lambertus. He describes the difficulty of maintaining a serious demeanor when observing the misplaced priorities of society.</summary>
<keywords>Tryphon Gabriele, Bertuccio Lamberti, Treviso, satire, Muses, human vanity, poetic inspiration, Roman model</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author finishes his thought on his inability to restrain himself from commenting on human folly. This page begins a deeper philosophical exploration of the "stupor" of the human mind and the various branches of knowledge (the "arts") that Nature has provided to guide us toward truth and virtue.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

[...scarcely able to restrain myself from] all the arrows of cornel-wood <note>Cornel wood was famously used in antiquity to make spear shafts and arrows; here it symbolizes the sharp trials or pointed observations directed at human nature.</note> that are fitted against us:
It appears so often and clearly, what a stupor within
Possesses human minds: which, on a straight path,
Are unable to follow their purpose, nor to discern the end.
In the beginning, nature sowed in us eternal
Little sparks <note>original: "Igniculos"; a Stoic philosophical concept referring to the innate seeds of reason or divine light within the human soul.</note>, by which, being kindled, we might seek out
The seeds of things, and also set firm feet
Through slippery places, and stand upon the path of truth once found.
From this, various arts shone forth once the darkness was driven out:
Which might guide mortal hearts <note>original: "mortalia corda"</note> through a steady journey.
For some, according to how much knowledge <note>original: "cognitio"</note>
Alone can promise, being content with such light, offer
Themselves to us as companions—whether through the solid forms
Of eternal matter, subject to motion <note>This refers to Natural Philosophy or Physics.</note>, or through that
Which the Mind separates from them, and fashions as remote <note>This refers to Mathematics and Logic, which "abstract" or pull ideas away from physical matter.</note>:
Or whether the journey must be held through things truly
Removed from the senses, and the heavenly realms must be sought with a pure mind <note>This refers to Metaphysics or Theology.</note>.
But others, related even to these, explain what we ourselves are,
And what work is proper to us, and finally what is our end;
They lead the human race <note>original: "Humanum genus"</note>, the pious and the true, through actions,
So that it may fill homes with virtues and
Noble cares, and cities with good character,
And make mortal affairs equal through just laws:
From here gushes forth fully the pure fountain of clear honor <note>original: "fons purus honesti"; "Honestum" in Latin refers to moral integrity or virtue for its own sake.</note>:
From which we drink what we ought to know, and how much is fitting, and what things
It is proper to use, or what to bestow, and what must be given
To dangers or to labor, and in what part effort
Should be given to grace; and also how [to us...]

<header>OF THE SERMONS.</header>

These [verses] my most excellent brother Trypho <note>Likely a close associate or relative of the author, acting as a literary critic.</note> will read and approve; just as once he did not disdain to possess my lyric poems, and wished to scrutinize mine and compare them with the greatest poets, whose footsteps I follow, always adoring them. He shall walk through the steep summits of the Aonian hill <note>original: "Aonii collis"; a reference to Mount Helicon in Greece, the legendary home of the Muses and a symbol of high poetic achievement.</note>; you, with me, will enter the caves in the secret valley, willingly clothed in ivy and green moss. Here is a grove, and the clear springs know their own deities; nor was it a shame for the gracious Muses to visit these low places.

### SERMON II.

Often, Bertutius, with laughter suppressed in my <margin>5</margin> chest, I marvel that mortals are tossed about by empty cares. Sometimes—lest anyone be able to turn this into a fault against me—I am not afraid, if I should care to allow my wit any freedom, to let the reins go so far that I am scarcely able to restrain the rising laughter <note>original: "Cachinnos"; specifically refers to loud, mocking, or unrestrained laughter, often used in satire to describe the reaction to human stupidity.</note>. For as often as I examine in my mind where our [nature] tends, and see things looking toward a different sign...

All the spears of cornel-wood <note>original: "spicula cornus"; likely a metaphor for the sharp tools of perception or the various paths of inquiry available to humanity.</note> that are fitted for us:
It appears so often and so clearly what a stupor within

arts which might guide mortal hearts through a certain journey.

they lead the pious and the true through right Actions <term>Actus</term>

and fill its homes with noble cares, its cities with morals,

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Sermon II. After discussing the innate "sparks" of reason and the various branches of knowledge (theoretical and practical) that guide the soul, the author now turns to the essential foundation of all virtue—religion—and contrasts it with the pervasive moral failings of humanity, specifically greed and ambition.</meta>

<header>OF THE SERMONS.</header>

Religion and the worship of the Divine Power <term>Numen</term> <note>original: "Numinis"; refers to the divine will or the presence of God that governs the universe.</note> are instilled within us; without which none of these virtues can exist, nor can anything be of benefit. Since things are so, whom indeed among thousands might you find who cares for such great matters and not their opposites? Whom does the ignorance of all things not drive headlong, so that he never stumbles and is never carried away by some error? Yet, on the contrary, who does not get stuck at every little trifle and trip over the obstacles in the smallest cracks? 

Who does not strive to misuse what belongs to another, whether it be right or wrong? Who is now found to be kindly disposed toward honorable works? Who drives gloomy Sloth from a sluggish breast? Who is not accustomed to both doing and speaking every shameful thing? Finally, in whom does empty Superstition <term>superstitio</term> not leave an empty mind in suspense? 

What of the fact that Greed and the insane desire for honors <note>original: "honorum insana cupido"; a classic philosophical critique of political ambition and the pursuit of status.</note> so harasses wretched mortals and drives them through crimes and frauds, through violence too, and through every wild and harmful practice? Led by this madness, it <insert>greed</insert> spares neither itself nor others at any time.

O dreadful plagues of men and wounds of life! Nor does it escape my mind that these views might not seem acceptable to the common crowd; nevertheless, hold this truth yourself: there is no ruin that should be shunned by sublime minds as much as that which lies hidden under wretched Ambition <term>Ambitio</term> within a great heart. For by gradually eating away at that heart, it casts it down and soon forces the Soul <term>Animus</term> to become small-minded. No less must these minds shun the wicked love of possessing <note>original: "amor sceleratus habendi"; a poetic phrase for avarice, suggesting that the desire for "having" is a crime against the soul's nature.</note>, than which nothing is more sordid, and nothing more pulls those rushing toward the depths...

<meta>catchword: (it spares) us</meta>

<summary>The author argues that religion is the necessary foundation for all virtue, but laments that most people are driven by ignorance, superstition, greed, and ambition. He specifically warns high-minded individuals that ambition is a hidden poison that shrinks the soul.</summary>
<keywords>religion, divine power, human error, greed, ambition, superstition, moral philosophy, the soul</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discourse of Sermon II, continuing the critique of worldly "plagues" like greed and ambition, before transitioning into Sermon III. The previous page ended by describing how these vices harass wretched mortals.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

[These vices] drive us further and cast us down from the heights of honor.
How exceedingly happy mortals would be, if they could only look *within themselves*; for then they would not wander aimlessly through devious paths, but would establish a straight course and a certain return to that place from which it was once granted to them to reach the earth—returning there once more according to their merits. There, the mind, turned upward toward the supreme Beginning of all things, might intimately join itself—solitary and without a body—to the One <term>soli</term> <note>original: "solam sine corpore soli"; this is a classic Neo-Platonic phrasing referring to the "flight of the alone to the Alone," the union of the individual soul with God.</note>. There, let it enjoy a happy life, free from toil, and exempted from lusts and cares.
This was that "laughter of the Soul" <term>Animi risus</term> which openly scorns human affairs and secretly raises itself toward the high path it seeks and hopes for; a path for which it always pants until, having found it, it might rest through the gifts of gentle peace, stripped of the stain of bodily turmoils.
I write these things, not so that the path of Virtue <insert>Virtue</insert> <note>The OCR reads "Bertuti," a common transcription error for "Virtuti."</note> may be better known to you—since you are already wise to all these things through your own nature—but so that I might signify the small efforts of a mind at leisure; and so that, if I am now strong enough to be of any manly use, I might attempt to apply a spur to the running horse <note>A classical idiom (original: "currenti subdere calcar") meaning to encourage someone who is already making excellent progress.</note>.

### TO LORENZO LOREDAN,
### VENETIAN PATRICIAN, ON THE VARIOUS
### ways human cares are diminished.

## SERMON III.

Lorenzo, because you frequently read our learned [writings]...

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory portion of a discourse, following the author's modest dedication of his work to a person of high status (likely the patron addressed in the previous page). The text transitions from philosophical reflection on the soul to a more pastoral and personal discussion of how the elite find relief from the burdens of city life through rural retreat and literature.</meta>

<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

You turn these pages with your hand, and amidst your busy affairs <term>negotia</term> <note>original: "negocia"; the Latin term for business or public duty, the opposite of "otium" or leisure.</note> you lower your mind with a devoted heart to these light trifles <term>nugis</term> <note>original: "nugis"; literally "scraps" or "jokes," a common self-deprecating way for Roman-style poets to refer to their own work.</note>. You do this as one might mix a sumptuous banquet with the simple vegetables of a common supper, perhaps to remove that sense of distaste which sometimes strives to dull the palate’s appetite. Or perhaps it is because you believe that even great things depend upon small ones—for indeed, as the saying goes, the great are often aided by the small.

For you nobles <term>proceres</term> <note>original: "proceres"; refers to the leading men or aristocracy of the state.</note> sometimes, when your public duties cease, depart from the city to go elsewhere, having set aside the weight of your wealth and honors in the countryside. Here, either the cultivated seeds are entrusted to their own earth, or it brings a gentle joy to carry home the chosen fruits of Ceres <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture and grain.</note> or the liquor of Bacchus <note>The god of wine.</note>. Or perhaps you prefer to fill your hands with flowery bundles in the meadows, to bind your brow with wreaths, and to walk beneath the breezes of the sweet-smelling Zephyr <note>The West Wind, associated with spring and gentle weather.</note>.

One might hunt openly by whatever means or method, or watch the local youth playing at their dances everywhere, or even—if it is permitted—devote oneself to a rustic Love. No less does one give attention to the nymphs, wandering frequently among the Hamadryads <note>nymphs of the trees.</note> and the Oreads <note>nymphs of the mountains.</note> on every side. One might also apply the mind to the Muses <note>The goddesses of inspiration for literature, science, and the arts.</note>, meditating in the depths of the valley or upon the high hills, and "playing" at composing verses.

Yet, meanwhile, it is not so easy to set aside the cares of the world, which the city always brings—grafted into our deepest senses—so that the greatest part of the mind remains a slave to them, and suddenly regards all other things as small. But truly, he might easily consider all these things to be in the place of a restorative medicine—he who reads the playful works of the poets <term>vatum</term> <note>original: "uatum"; vates is a more prestigious term for poet, implying a prophetic or divinely inspired role.</note>...

<meta>catchword: That (Vt)</meta>

<summary>The author describes how the nobility seek relief from the "weight" of city business through rural retreats, nature, and the "medicine" of poetry. He uses classical pastoral imagery—gods, nymphs, and breezes—to frame literature as a necessary refreshment for the mind.</summary>
<keywords>leisure (otium), business (negotia), country life, Ceres, Bacchus, Zephyr, nymphs, Muses, poetry as medicine, nobility</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory portion of a discourse, following the author's modest dedication of his work to a person of high status. The previous page explored how the elite find relief from public burdens through rural retreat and literature.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

So that you, perhaps reading, may turn this to your own pleasing use—even though such industry is the natural habit of a noble character. Just as we are accustomed to fill our ears with sweet singing and the pleasant, tuneful melody of the lyre; and just as we are used to gazing upon statues skillfully carved with a sharp tool, giving the artist his due praise; so we continue to look at tablets painted with a learned brush, feasting our eyes on marvelous Art. Captured at last by these various charms, we who are of sound mind now seek these delights, now those. Truly, these are the comforts <term>solatia</term> <note>original: "solatia vitae"; the "consolations of life," a common Stoic and Epicurean theme regarding how art and philosophy provide refuge from the world's hardships.</note> of human life; for while life is tossed by the constant waves of labor, it still providentially finds rest for its weariness in a harbor.

<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

<meta>Catchword: Quod (Because/That)</meta>

*May it help us, and if anything is useful, may it benefit us.*
*Is there anything that men desire more than to lead a blessed*
*and happy life for a long time, competing for it?*
*Yet you will find nothing, though you search; and still, that very*
*life we long to live, we never actually live.*
*For who is so well off that if he were to sit with himself*
*for a short while, he would not suddenly fill your ear with a thousand complaints,*
*as if he were more wretched than anyone else,*
*and inferior in every possible condition?*
*But if he is poor—though perhaps he begs through his own*
*fault—he nevertheless blames the fates, and falsely claims*
*he was born under unfavorable stars.* <note>original: "syderibus... iniquis"; the author is mocking the common tendency to blame astrology or "bad luck" for personal failures caused by poor character.</note>
*Another part of humanity, if they stray even a little bit*
*from the right path they had prepared to follow:*
*immediately exclaim to themselves that they have been treated unhappily.*
*There is one whom no joys or charms delight for long;*
*he loathes everything to such an extent that he soon*
*peevishly rejects and hates what he just recently desired.*
*Thus everyone complains just as much; and while grieving, they lose*
*everything for which they are so sad, and they hasten to lose*
*the very life for which they always gape.*
*From this, I am bursting with laughter, because we always*
*seek to live, yet meanwhile it never happens that we live*
*without accompanying pain—unless, perhaps, you call that*
*“life” which gloomy Anguish embraces.* <note>original: "Angor"; a term for crushing distress or strangling anxiety. The author contrasts this psychological "choking" with the true life of the mind.</note>
*Therefore, I want this laughter of mine to smile upon you*
*in turn, and for you to lay down the heavy cares of your mind,*
*so that you might strive to live out a peaceful age <term>Aeuo</term> with me;* <note>original: "Aeuo"; referring to an age of time or a span of life, here used to suggest a lasting period of tranquility.</note>

<summary>In this verse discourse, the author observes that most people spend their lives complaining about their lot and blaming the stars rather than actually living. He invites his friend to join him in laughing at these human follies to find true peace.</summary>

# TO VALERIUS SUPERCHIUS,
# OF PESARO, AGAINST THOSE WHO
## LITTLE OBSERVE THE POWER
### OF THE DISCOURSES.

## DISCOURSE V.

There are those, Valerius, to whom this work seems so slight that it should be immediately scorned by all the learned; and even I, who strive to distinguish these *Discourses* <term>Sermones: a genre of conversational, often satirical poetry popularized by Horace, characterized by a "low" or plain style rather than epic grandiosity.</term> in humble verse, drawn from an easy and pure mouth, seeking no appearance of splendor, until the Mind openly brings forth from without what it conceives within. 
Although I agree with them to this extent—that I do not strive to gain any praise from this—it would nonetheless not be out of place now, perhaps, to relate in a few words what a wise cook once did for his master. That cook, a master of flavors, had taken care to make a Dinner <note>original: "Cœnam"</note> harmonious through every measure of taste, even though no foreign ingredients were used in it. 
But the master, as if every dish were tasteless <note>original: "fatua"; literally "fatuous" or "insipid."</note>, happened to despise them all. To whom the cook said: "Master, either your stomach <note>original: "Stomachus"</note> is not well right now, or these dishes do not please you because they are seasoned with no strange or monstrous flavor,"

<summary>In this opening to Discourse V, the author defends his plain, unadorned poetic style against critics who value complexity over substance, using a metaphor of a master who cannot appreciate a well-cooked meal because it lacks "monstrous" foreign seasonings.</summary>
<keywords>Valerio Superchio, literary criticism, Discourses, plain style, metaphor of the cook, Pesaro, humility in writing</keywords>

They are usually pleasing to those who are healthy, and beneficial for those who are sound. <note>original: "Grata ualentibus esse solent, et profore sanis." There are those, therefore, who adopt for themselves absolutely nothing else
Than the meter and the crash of words with a great sound,
And they look too little within; nor do they attend to what sense
Is enclosed in them, what quality it carries, or to what it aptly clings,
So that no foreign limbs <note>original: "aliena in corpore membra"; a metaphor for stylistic consistency, where every part of the poem must belong to the whole rather than being a "monstrous" collection of unrelated ornaments.</note>
Stand together in the body, but rather that what you speak
<margin>●</margin> Appears simple and singular, and is related purely and brightly.
And for this reason, a *Discourse* <term>Sermo: In this context, the "plain style" of poetry used by authors like Horace for philosophical or satirical topics, which "creeps" like prose rather than "soaring" like epic.</term> creeping along the ground, though well-cultivated,
Beneath which lies a weighty and fertile moral sentiment,
Is not approved by an ear filled with such filth <note>The "filth" refers to the "noise" or "crash" of empty, high-sounding words mentioned earlier.</note>.
But the learned—such as your elder brother, and you,
And others whom it is now impossible to include in a count—
Admire this work whenever they can weigh it for themselves
In private, as if it were a divine poem.
And we, if the Love of the Muses <note>The nine Greek goddesses who inspire the arts and sciences.</note> achieves anything, 
Sometimes descend to those small depths; and looking up at the heights, 
We wander, humble, in the lowest valley.
Oh, if only that high leisure <note>original: "alta otia"; the "productive peace" or freedom from public business that Humanists believed was necessary for high-quality writing.</note> I have long hoped and prayed for 
Would finally arrive! 
Perhaps then we shall also attempt the steep peaks of the sacred hill <note>A reference to Mount Parnassus, the home of the Muses, symbolizing the pursuit of "higher" genres like epic poetry.</note>
By a secret path, where, having dared to go a little 
While ago, we progressed so far. There,
I might dictate these verses under the name of a friend:
"Unconquered royal offspring of great-souled parents,
What feelings now rise in your youthful breast?
Or what work do you stir up, and what deeds—destined to thrive 
Through all time—do you meditate upon with yourself, and with a deep..."

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues Discourse VI, addressed to Leonardo Portius. On the previous page, the author began discussing why people value their own artistic and intellectual pursuits above others, attributing it to human nature.</meta>

<header>OF DISCOURSES.</header>

or because we especially praise those things in which
it pleases us to indulge, or which we believe we are capable of doing.
Hence one person is held by one interest, another by another;
the mind is affected by various things and applies itself to its own deeds.
And the diligent author, dwelling upon his own work which he performs,
sometimes admires himself so much that he thinks whatever is
placed below him is lesser, and considers it of little value.
Moreover, friends are sometimes too captivated by the affairs
of their friends; and things which they would perhaps reprimand
in others, they here admit and approve,
and they strive to prefer some people over others, as the situation demands.
It is also innate in many to always prefer ancient times <note>original: *tempora prisca*</note>
to our own, and to praise what is farther away,
and what has long since withdrawn from memory in a past age.
As if nothing should be praised by them until
a countless series of years has first passed by.
Indeed, I do not deny that antiquity <term>uetustas: The state of being old or ancient, often carrying a sense of venerable authority.</term>
should always be venerated, and that every portion of praise is owed to it.
But I wonder in what way that age prepared everything for itself,
so that it left nothing for us to do; and how it has scattered us,
willing though we are, in this wretched sluggishness: so that while
we despair of ever being like our great ancestors <term>Auorum: Forefathers; here referring to the great figures of the Classical past.</term>,
we think that absolutely nothing remains for us to do.
But enough of them. On the other hand, there are also those who would
equal themselves to ancient times, having followed I-know-not-what
obscure paths; which, if they had happened to be encountered,
the unshaven <note>original: *intonsi*; a reference to the early Romans who did not shave, symbolizing a primitive, rustic, or rugged era.</note> ancients would have shuddered to approach such rough thickets <term>Tesqua: Wild, rough, or deserted places; used here to describe difficult or archaic literary styles.</term>.
And so through all the rough patches and the unevenness of the terrain...

<summary>This page explores the psychology of intellectual bias, discussing how authors favor their own work and why many people over-venerate the past to the point of modern creative paralysis. The author argues against both blind worship of antiquity and those who try too hard to mimic ancient obscurities.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, antiquity, intellectual vanity, ancestors, tradition, nostalgia, style</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Discourse VI, which was addressed to Leonardo Portius. On the previous page, the author discussed how people often undervalue contemporary achievements in favor of antiquity; here, he shifts to mocking the various social and intellectual affectations of his peers before transitioning to Discourse VII, addressed to Julius.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

They stumble about here and there, so that I now believe the nails on their calloused feet are being completely upturned. What of those for whom the arts of their ancestors cannot be enough? Or those who speak at great length about every possible subject? Or those who strive to appear learned to others by remaining silent? And those who wish to become accustomed to everything and then, in turn, feel disgust for it all, unable to be led away from their own particular taste. Therefore, when you encounter these monsters <note>original: *monstris*; the author uses this strong term to describe people with absurd social habits.</note> so very often—for I believe it is hardly a rare occurrence—can you restrain your laughter? Do they not strive to burst forth from your whole chest? 

But I have long known you to be easily moved by these things, by Hercules! Ever since you threw those witty jests <note>original: *Salsos Lusus*; literally "salty plays," referring to sharp, stinging humor.</note> at Castora—that Castora, who was so out of his mind that while he prepared to respond, he actually said more contradictory things against himself than you did. For my part, to confess the truth about myself (which will perhaps offend certain people), I cannot bear it; I do not see why I should keep it inside, or why I should be unable to at least let a smirk sprinkle the edges of my lips. For a ready laugh has also shaped me, and yet Nature did not fashion me for unrestrained guffawing <term>Cachinni: A loud, immoderate, or mocking laugh, often associated with derision.</term>.

They stumble here and there with wide steps, so that I now think all the nails on their calloused feet are being turned back. What of those for whom the skills of the ancients cannot be enough? Do they not struggle to burst out from your whole chest?

But I have known you well in these matters for a long time, by Hercules! <note>original: "meHercule"</note> Ever since you aimed those witty jests <term>salsos lusus: Literally "salty games"; refers to biting, sharp, or sophisticated humor.</term> at Castor <note>Castor is likely a contemporary figure being mocked, or a stock character representing a foolish, self-contradicting debater.</note>—that madman who, while he prepared to respond, spoke more contradictions against himself than even you did! As for me, to confess a truth that will perhaps offend some, I cannot bear it; I do not see why I should keep it inside, or why I should be unable to at least let a smile play upon my lips. For Nature also made me prone to easy laughter, though she did not fashion me for the unrestrained guffaw <term>cachinnus: A loud, immoderate, or mocking laugh, often associated with derision.</term>.

### TO JULIUS, THE PERSONIFICATION OF SERVIUS PERRARIUS <note>original: "AD IVLIVM, SERVI PERRARI PROSOPOPEIA." Prosopopeia is a rhetorical device where an author speaks through the voice of another person or an inanimate object.</note>

#### DISCOURSE VII. <note>original: "SERMO. VII."</note>

If, Julius, you have some free time amidst the great affairs
    which you manage,
to listen for a short while to something of little
    weight,

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's personal reflection. While the previous page ended mid-word, discussing the author’s natural inclination toward laughter, this page shifts into a formal dedication and a declaration of intent. The author transitions from mocking the hypocrisy of others to explaining his own path, education, and loyalty to his patron.</meta>

<header>BOOK I. OF THE SERMONS <note>original: "SERMONVM." While "sermon" today implies a religious speech, in this classical context it refers to "Satires" or "Conversations" in the style of the Roman poet Horace.</note></header>

With this slender discourse, I wish to relate a few things to you. It is because I desire to give an account of a life already spent as a good servant to his master, and to show in what manner I strive to spend the remainder of the future. Therefore, I ask that you lend a calm ear to me here—that mercy which is now, and always will be, dear to your heart.

Ever since my prudent father ordered me to come here as a boy, so that he might mold me in my tender state from my earliest years, I have been held by such a great love of virtue <term>uirtutis Amore: A central Humanist concept where "Virtue" represents both moral excellence and intellectual power.</term>. Indeed, I follow its footsteps with such a close pace that I may never fail in my duty at any time; rather, I persist in it always, lest I be carried over as a deserter to the opposing sides, or be commanded to abandon my fixed standards <note>The author uses military metaphors (deserter, standards) to describe his unwavering commitment to his moral and intellectual principles.</note>, forced to turn even slightly from the right path.

Because of this, there is a perpetual fervor around my heart; it always desires to exert my strengths, however small they remain, so that I might be able to disentangle myself and escape through the hidden coverings of things <note>original: "condita rerum inuolucra." This refers to the "veils" or "wrappings" of allegorical truth, a common idea that deep truths are hidden behind complex layers.</note>. 

From this point, it also delights me to apply myself to two Muses <note>Likely referring to the dual pursuits of Serious Philosophy and Lighter Poetry/Rhetoric.</note> by a fixed law: whether when I study to release an idle heart from heavy cares, or when there is a need to bathe the mind in allurements and sprinkle it with honorable play. Meanwhile, it is no less pleasing if our diligence, however slight it may be, serves you at all times. If I also now begin to cast seed onto this soil <note>original: "huic glebae." A metaphor for the patron’s favor or the intellectual project itself.</note>, I have a hope—and not a vain one—that I may soon gather a similar harvest in abundance from your affairs. This is the continuous course of my life <term>uitae tenor: The sustained "tone" or "path" of one's existence.</term>; toward this I always...

<summary>The author addresses his patron, recounting how his father set him on a path of virtue and study from childhood. He pledges his loyalty and explains his dedication to both serious study and "honorable play" (literature), comparing his work to sowing seeds in the patron's service.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, education, Muses, patronage, moral duty, allegory, life's purpose</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's declaration of his moral path and loyalty to his patron. On the previous page, he spoke of his upbringing and his determination to follow the path of virtue, using military metaphors to describe his discipline. # BOOK I. <note>original: "LIB. I." This marks the beginning of the first book of the author's Satires or "Conversations."</note>

May God Himself preserve me in the place where He willed me to settle. And may no path be more dear to me than that which, by a straight track, leads me back to your commands should I ever wander away. I pray that I am not proven unable to avoid—by the example of what once befell a tiny ant—a similar fate. 

This ant, then, marveled at the habits of men <term>mores hominum: A classical phrase referring to the customs, character, and social behaviors of humanity.</term>—how they are rarely content to stay in one place, but instead are always moving and seeking new homes. She decided she too would travel abroad and journey across the world. 

She had already crawled forward for many yards <note>original: "ulnas." An *ulna* was a unit of measurement, roughly a cubit or an arm's length.</note> when, behold, she stumbled upon the bleached skull of a horse <term>caballi: A common Latin word for a horse, often used in a less formal sense than "equus."</term>. She gazed at it in wonder. It was as if she were looking at the walls of a great city. She wandered all around it, here and there, searching for an entrance until at last, with great effort, she managed to crawl inside. 

Tirelessly, she spent the moments of her brief life wandering through it all, until she reached the intricate inner chambers where the brain’s uneven seat is usually found. Then, full of joy, she cried: “This must be the palace of a great Prince! Since no people live here, it shall be mine!” She immediately took heart; in her own mind, she was already wealthy, the Queen of a newly discovered kingdom. 

But when nightfall reminded her of her need for food and drink, she realized that this "palace" was entirely empty of the sustenance she had hoped for. Returning to her hollow in the morning, she laughed at those "Royal gifts," and she hated herself for having been so foolish as to believe in her own delusion. 

These lessons are of great value for the conduct of life, however small they may seem. For one should never think that no help can come from humble beginnings <note>original: "è tenui." Literally "from the thin" or "from the slight," referring to the small size of the ant or the simplicity of the story.</note>.

<vocab>ant, horse, Queen, kingdom, God, habits of men</vocab>

<summary>The author uses the fable of an ant mistaking a horse's skull for a royal palace to warn against human vanity and the desire for constant change. He concludes that even small examples and humble things can offer great wisdom for living well.</summary>
<keywords>fable, vanity, ambition, moral philosophy, allegory, virtue, humility</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes a fable about an ant from the previous page and introduces a new fable about a lion and a mouse to illustrate how the small can aid the great. <header>OF THE SERMONS. <note>original: "SERMONVM." This refers to the genre of "Sermones," or "Conversations," a style of moralizing and satirical poetry modeled after the Roman poet Horace.</note></header>

The King <note>Referring to the Lion, the "King of Beasts."</note> had fallen into the spread-out nets of the hunters <term>Brutorum: Literally "of the brutes" or "beasts," here referring to the men hunting the lion.</term>. I will not delay you now with many words, for it is a brief little tale <term>Fabella: A diminutive form of "fabula," meaning a short fable or moral story.</term>. By chance, a tiny mouse found this four-footed creature bound by a twisted rope and stretched out across the whole cave. The Lion, acting as a humble suppliant, begged for the mouse's help; the mouse willingly performed the commands of his Lord, stood close by, and gnawed through the bonds. The Lion then departed, having promised to return the favor if he ever could. Thus, by a small force, he escaped great dangers.

I return now to the point from which my first discourse <term>Sermo: A talk, speech, or poem in the "Sermones" style.</term> had already flowed. No one, with any amount of wealth, could ever prevail upon me to diminish even a tiny bit of a habit hardened by long time—not unless I were to first pursue you and your instructions with all my care, effort, and duty, provided that you look upon me with a serene expression. Furthermore, if any excellent opportunities should appear, I would wish to strive "with hands and feet" <note>original: "pedibus, manibusq̄." A classical Latin idiom meaning to exert oneself with all one's might, similar to the English "tooth and nail."</note> (as the old saying goes) to approach and embrace them, as nature has breathed modest strength into me and experience has increased it.

### CONVERSATION VIII.

We each follow our own fated star, <margin>and</margin> <note>The marginal "q" is a common Latin abbreviation for "-que," the word for "and."</note> Bologni, wherever it pulls us; and it commands us in such a way that scarcely anyone can escape undergoing its orders. It will be permitted...

<meta>This page continues the poetic discourse from the previous page, shifting from the moral fables of the ant and the lion to a more personal and philosophical reflection on the influence of the stars and fate upon human life, health, and the political state of the world.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

Let it be necessary for no man at all to obey. Let this ancient opinion of learned men stick with you; there is none more powerful than this, nor any more sacred. Thus, while we are led by chance by a heavy and constant Star <term>Astro: From the Latin "astrum," referring here to a planet or a specific astrological influence governing one's destiny.</term>, we ourselves fall into a harsh form of disease. For that one born of Maia <note>original: "Maia genitus." This refers to the god Mercury, the son of Maia, who was the patron of scholars, commerce, and communication.</note>, who provides access to learning for those seeking it and for the studious, being himself not sufficiently capable—even though the other stars of heaven might favor him—has abandoned all those whom he once cherished. 

Hence I, who devote myself to the Muses—though my strength for songs is small—and try to remove myself from the crowd of the common people, have not been able to avoid the malignant lot of the star. Nor has the pleasing diligence of my studies harmed me, as you say; for what could be harmful that you do certainly with moderation, and do so willingly? Do not wonder if the heavy impulse of the Star presses upon us, for you see that the times of things are now indeed changed, and those same times are far different from the previous ones. From where did this come so quickly? Unless the stirred-up violence of fierce Mars <note>Mars: The planet and god associated with war and conflict.</note> should rage, and the cruel star of Saturn <note>Saturn: Traditionally viewed in astrology as a "malefic" planet associated with delay, hardship, and melancholy.</note> should linger: where the favor of Jupiter and Venus <note>Jupiter and Venus: These were considered "benefic" or helpful planets.</note> might be present, and Mercury himself might stick to evils, and the Moon might hasten her swift courses into Savage Rage; but also that Phoebus <note>Phoebus: A name for Apollo, representing the Sun.</note> likewise hides his lights—Phoebus, who provides all light. 

Do you not see how everyone now hastens with minds seized by Warlike things <note>original: "Bellica." Referring to matters of war or the current military conflicts.</note>, those who just now, peaceful, were so rejoicing to handle the gifts of peace with such great quiet. Clearly that fierce Martial Ardor <note>original: "Martius Ardor." The heat or passion of Mars, driving men toward war.</note> turns...

<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

Now the Lord, residing in the high part of the heavens, rules over all with the heavenly host and administers all things. Yet do not believe that these celestial signs show us only such things, but rather that they threaten far more grievous matters: exile, torments, famine, fires, and plagues; floods, the receding of the sea, and tremors of the earth; lightning bolts, terrors, thunder, lightning flashes, and comets. But above all, they portend wars—horrid wars—which anyone might already foresee, as one sees the darkened minds of princes prepared for battle. Toward these princes, everyone should offer suppliant prayers, that they might desist from such great strife; and it pleases me now to cry out in the verse of a sublime poet:
> "O leaders, do not, do not accustom your souls to such great wars." <note>original: "Ne proceres, ne tanta Animis assuescite Bella." This is a famous line adapted from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book 6, line 832-833), an appeal to cease civil conflict.</note>

They do not wither from the heat, nor from the hardening force of cold. Harmful birds stay far away, nor does any slippery snake swim in the waters, nor does the evil viper crawl upon the ground. No wild beast stays there to fill its maw with violent, hooked claws or ravenous teeth. From here, **Pomona** <note>The Roman goddess of fruit trees and gardens.</note> fills her baskets with the produce of the blessed orchard, while the Goddess <note>Likely Flora, the goddess of flowers and spring.</note>, seated with her robes pulled back, weaves beds of violets from fragrant garlands in her lap of flowers. His nourishing mother <note>Venus, the goddess of love.</note> accompanies him in his games, and responds to his Jests with equal cares, and joins in of her own accord.

## TO LORENZO ABSTEMIO, TO WHOM HE COMMENDS HIS CONVERSATIONS, WHICH *he published at his encouragement;* <note>Lorenzo Abstemio (c. 1440–1508) was a prominent Italian humanist and librarian who was well-known for his collection of fables.</note>

### CONVERSATION X.

<margin>5</margin>
*If there is anything, Lorenzo, that compels us*
*to love those whom we have never seen,*
*it is, above all, splendid virtue.*
*Then it is the feeling of being loved by those who are absent.*
*But whether I respect you because of your virtue, or because of the love*
*with which you, though distant, have long embraced me while I am distant,*

<header>BOOK I.</header>

*Someone might rightly call it into doubt, as one who*
*weighs the importance of both sides with a balanced scale.*
*You have not been given the last place among the honorable*
*and the learned men of our age, but rather the next best to those equals*
*who strive in vain to surpass others in their studies.*
*This means that I, being just, should follow you with the honor you deserve.*
*But because you deign to favor us and our works with your Love,*
*my own feelings acknowledge a mutual debt in return,*
*and a grateful mind offers equal thanks.*
*For as to the fact that you urge me in a sweet little letter <note>original: "Epistolio"</note> to publish the*
*poems <note>original: "Carmina"</note> with which I once addressed my dear friends,*
*Love—not the merit of the work itself—is the cause of this; for Love overlooks many things*
*and does not notice the blemishes <note>original: "naevos"</note> scattered on a beloved body.*
*For what can a simple jest in a poem offer*
*that a wise and highly learned reader would desire?*
*I have played, I admit, and I have seen the Muses <note>The nine goddesses of the arts and sciences.</note> even here,*
*at the very foot of that noble mountain <note>Referring to Mount Parnassus, the home of the Muses; the author suggests he is writing "low" poetry compared to grand epics.</note>, walking with a slow pace.*
*I saw them myself, daringly, and I did not compose these light trifles easily,*
*even though the labor and the glory strive together in a slight matter <note>This is a literary allusion to Virgil’s *Georgics*, suggesting that even small or "slight" poems require great effort and can bring fame.</note>.*
*Since you seem to wait for them so eagerly,*
*I will let them go: just as when the mother bird has been left,*
*the light fledglings <note>original: "pulli"</note> leap from the nest and are carried off haphazardly,*
*so let these "children" of mine <note>original: "pignora," literally meaning pledges or dear children; a common metaphor for a writer's books.</note> leave their father at your bidding.*
*Do not allow these trifles, now committed to your trust,*
*to wander aimlessly; take care that they do not fall into traps.*
*For here, as is the way of the world, nets <note>original: "Retia"</note> are spread everywhere,*
*especially while their father still enjoys the breath of life;*
*indeed, the fact that their father is still alive is an obstacle to them <note>The author hints at the common idea that critics are harsher toward living authors than toward the deceased.</note>.*

# C O N V E R S A T I O N . X .

<margin>S</margin> If there is anything, Lorenzo, that forces us to love
Those whom we have never seen,
It is, above all, splendid virtue.
Furthermore, we love the absent if we feel ourselves loved by them.
But whether it is virtue or Love that makes me respect you—
You who, though absent, have long embraced me, who am also absent—

<summary>This page concludes a mythological scene featuring a drunken Silenus being rescued by satyrs and nymphs, then transitions into a formal dedication to the humanist scholar Lorenzo Abstemio, praising virtue and long-distance friendship.</summary>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Lorenzo Abstemio from the previous page. The author, having established his respect for Abstemio, now discusses the merits of his own work and his decision to publish at his friend’s request.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>
<sig>h iii</sig>

Anyone might rightly call it into doubt, as one who
Weighs the importance of both <note>Refers to the "virtue" and "love" mentioned on the previous page.</note> on an equal scale.
No lowly place is given to you among the honorable
And the learned men of our age; rather, you are next to those
Who strive in vain to outrun others in their studies.
This ensures that I may follow you with just honor and fairness.
But because you deign to favor us and our works with your Love,
My own affection confesses that it owes a debt in return,
And equal gratitude is owed to a grateful mind.
For as to your urging me in a sweet little letter <note>original: "Epistolio"</note> to publish the
Poems <note>original: "Carmina"</note> with which I once addressed dear friends,
It is Love, not the work itself, that does this: Love, which hides
Many faults and does not notice the blemishes scattered on a beloved body.
For what "simple laughter" <note>A self-deprecating reference to the light or humorous nature of his writing.</note> can a poem contain
That a reader, at once prudent and most learned, might desire?
Indeed, I have played <note>The author uses "lusi" (played/trifled) to describe his poetic composition.</note>, I confess, and I saw the Muses <note>Goddesses of inspiration and the arts.</note>
At the foot of the noble mountain <note>Mount Parnassus, the home of the Muses.</note> proceed with a slow step:
I saw them myself, daring much, nor did I easily "play" these slight things,
Though effort <note>original: "labor"</note> and glory <note>original: "gloria"</note> may contend together in a slight matter.
Since you seem to wait for these so eagerly,
I will let them go: just as when the mother is left behind
In the nest, the light chicks leap out and are carried along rashly;
So, at your advice, let my offspring <note>original: "pignora"; literally "pledges," but used here as a metaphor for his poems as his children.</note> leave their father.
Do not allow what has been committed to your trust to wander
To just anywhere; and beware lest they head into traps.
For for these works—as is the custom of men—nets are stretched
Everywhere, as long as their father enjoys the vital breeze <note>original: "uitali... Aura"; a poetic way of saying "as long as the author is still alive."</note>
Especially, for the fact that the father lives is a hindrance to them <note>The author suggests that critics are harsher toward a living author than a dead one.</note>.

<summary>The author expresses his deep respect for Lorenzo Abstemio and explains that he is publishing his poems only because of Abstemio’s loving encouragement. He uses the metaphor of chicks leaving a nest to describe releasing his "literary children" into the world, asking his friend to protect them from harsh critics.</summary>

<keywords>Lorenzo Abstemio, dedication, poetry, Muses, literary criticism, fatherhood metaphor, friendship, humility</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s dedication to Lorenzo Abstemio. After expressing humility about his own work, the author describes the "ideal reader"—someone who judges a work by its own merit and craftsmanship rather than the fame of the author or the age of the text.</meta>

# OF DISCOURSES

Yet there are those of an excellent character who weigh the substance of things, and do not consider whether something is modern or who the author might be until they have turned their mind to the work itself. When they see everything guided by a single thread, hanging and proceeding from a single path, they perceive it with a mind gifted with sharp and upright judgment. These men are moved by the work, not by the author’s name or by a thousand years of antiquity.

But just as when someone lingers over a painter’s panel, at first sight unknown to them—because the painted scene pleases them, they soon ask for the name of the artist to be praised, and his homeland, so that they might hold in high regard the person whose work they have observed. In this same way, the prudent and the learned look upon the works of others as if they were their own. They offer praise and favor to all, and do not ask to whom a work belongs before they have frequently honored it with well-deserved praise in public—provided it is deemed worthy of such praise.

They wish a long life for the author, so that he may produce many more "plays" <note>original: "ludat"; the author uses the language of play or "trifles" to refer to poetic composition.</note>, and they offer their congratulations both to him and to the Age under which it was the good fortune of the rising poet to grow to maturity. Such men I myself, if it is permitted, worship with veneration here on earth—those who bear the most radiant sacred offerings to the Aonian <note>The Aonians are the Muses, the goddesses of poetic inspiration.</note> deities among the Nymphs and the spirits of the high summit <note>Likely referring to Mount Helicon or Parnassus, the mythological homes of the Muses.</note>, from where the sweet liquid flows through the grasses of Hymettus <note>A mountain near Athens famous in antiquity for its honey and marble.</note> and shines more pure than yellow gold.

<vocab>Sermonum (Discourses), Hymeti (Hymettus), Aoniis (Aonian/Muses), Nymphas (Nymphs), poeta (poet), ars (art), iudicium (judgment)</vocab>

<summary>The author describes the ideal critic who judges literature based on its internal merit and craftsmanship rather than the author's reputation, using an analogy of a person admiring a painting before knowing the artist's name.</summary>

<keywords>literary criticism, artistic merit, Muses, poetic inspiration, Mount Hymettus, ideal reader, craftsmanship</keywords>
