# THE SECOND BOOK OF DISCOURSES
# BY GIOVANNI AURELIO AUGURELLI
# <note>original: "Ioannis Aurelii Augurelli Sermonum Liber Secundus"; Augurelli (c. 1441–1524) was a celebrated Italian humanist and alchemist.</note>
# TO NICCOLÒ FRANCO,
# <note>Niccolò Franco was the Bishop of Treviso from 1485 to 1499.</note>
# BISHOP OF
# TREVISO.
# <note>original: "Tarvisinum."</note>

### DISCOURSE I.

*Once mindful of my flourishing and sweet youth,*
*I played <note>original: "lusimus"; humanist poets often used the language of "play" or "trifles" to describe their early, lighter works.</note>, and I allowed no small thing to those tender years,*
*Whether in elegies or soon in lyric poems; with rhythms and modes*
*Of various kinds, the Muse revealed the desire of my mind to me.*
*And I could have, and certainly now at last, Franco, I would have been at rest,*
*Had not your grace—as often at other times, so lately in its kindness—*
*Stirred me from my idleness, or indeed from being occupied*
*With many different studies, while I myself strive*
*To return thanks to you as much as I am able.*
*And no thing would be more pleasing or more delightful to me*
*Than if I should seem to have spoken things worthy of you,*
*And to reach such great ears with serious voices.*
*But what shall be the measure? Or where indeed can the mind*
*First take its stand? Shall we carry our feet onto the field*
*Where we have already been accustomed to run,*
*And upon a worn and common path?*
*Or rather that which a more mature age loves,*

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Book II of Augurelli's "Sermones," featuring a formal dedication to Niccolò Franco, Bishop of Treviso. The author reflects on his transition from writing youthful, light poetry to more serious, mature works at the Bishop's encouragement.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Aurelio Augurelli, Niccolò Franco, Treviso, Neo-Latin poetry, dedication, Muses, poetic maturity, Sermones</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening discourse of Book II, following the poet's transition from the light poetry of his youth to the more serious philosophical themes of his maturity. He addresses the Bishop of Treviso, Niccolò Franco, as a model of these higher virtues.</meta>

And which your reverence displays in your whole countenance,
Shall that be our path? So that, straying far through byways,
And leaving itself among the fickle crowd,
A straight path, scarcely known to a few,
Might lead us, where it offers itself narrower to them.
O <term>Pierides</term> <note>original: "Pierides"; a poetic name for the Muses, the goddesses of inspiration, named after their birthplace in Pieria.</note>, since you are not ignorant of how high
The journey stretches, show the path in short stretches:
And grant easy labor to one climbing through difficulties.
Once there had been a difficult question, and one of no small
Sharp-wittedness, debated among the wise ancestors:
Was there some <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "finis bonorum"; literally the "end of goods." This is a foundational question in ancient philosophy, famously explored by Cicero, concerning the ultimate goal of human life.</note> to be sought by us
Under its own true image, or must we wander forever?
Once that was found, another more serious question succeeded it
And persuaded each man to let his own desire dictate and bring
Both the making and the keeping of the limit and the end of goods.
For as soon as it was established that this goal was one, and that
One should not strive beyond it, but rather that all human things 
Should be referred back to it, then it was variously asked what that might be:
Toward which men might direct themselves, and refer all they do.
From this, different things seemed best to different men following various paths:
Such as **virtue**, such as **honor**, such as **wealth**, or **slavish pleasure**.
While I seriously weigh these things within myself to the best of my ability,
And while it is necessary to know beforehand the <term>duties</term> <note>original: "officia"; a reference to moral obligations and social responsibilities, a major theme in Stoic and Humanist thought.</note>
Of men, then you occur to me—how great you have seemed
In those duties, and indeed how you still appear, when you handle 
Public and private **affairs** <note>original: "negocia"; the active business of life, often contrasted with "otium" or contemplative leisure.</note> with equal virtue, and when
In great and small things you appear as one and the same man.
And these things, as if having proceeded from a divine mind

<summary>Augurelli invokes the Muses to guide him on a narrow, philosophical path as he investigates the nature of the "highest good" (finis bonorum). He identifies the Bishop of Treviso as a living example of one who balances public and private duties with unwavering virtue.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical inquiry of the previous page to the personal praise of the poem's dedicatee, Bishop Niccolò Franco. ### LIB. II.

May they come to pass, whatever counsels have once escaped
From your lips or from your deep heart; then immediately
Those good men who have need of them take them up and guard them as sacred.
For this reason, because the <term>highest fathers</term> <note>original: "summi patres"; this refers to the Popes, specifically those like Sixtus IV or Innocent VIII, who entrusted the Bishop with diplomatic missions.</note> have often
Entrusted very many things to you, which you soon brought to completion—
All of them—in various parts of the world, not in one region alone.
Hence also, when you were sent to visit foreign kings:
While you did many welcome things for them and brought back great reports,
[You went to] the <term>Persians</term> <note>original: "Persas"; a reference to the diplomatic efforts to form alliances with the Safavid dynasty in Persia against the Ottoman Empire.</note>, so that they embraced you with a wondrous love,
Which may He [God] wish to have endured and keep even to these times.
Here too, because you performed a great and sacred office
Among the <term>Venetian nobles</term> <note>original: "uenetos proceres"; the Bishop served as a papal legate to the Republic of Venice for an exceptionally long period.</note>, a thing which happened to no one before:
You completed seven whole years there.
Indeed, your people here—now that you have ceased to handle
Public affairs and now as a <term>shepherd</term> <note>original: "pastor"; a standard metaphor for a Bishop's care for his diocese.</note> guard your sheepfolds more closely—
Do not envy any other people their scout or guardian:
And they adore you as a <term>divinity</term> <note>original: "numen"; while traditionally meaning a god, in this Humanist context it is a hyperbolic way to describe the Bishop's immense moral authority.</note>, if such a thing is permitted on earth.
I myself also—for small things are wont to stand beside great ones—
Following behind, and pondering these things with a tranquil mind,
Do what I can: I attempt an approach to the <term>Muses</term> <note>original: "Musas"; the nine Greek goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences.</note> and to songs:
So that while the fates allow, the present <term>Age</term> <note>original: "Aetas"</note> may hear me
Singing of your good deeds, and the future may not be ignorant of them.
In no other way did excellent <term>bards</term> <note>original: "uates"; a Latin term for poets that implies they possess a prophetic or holy inspiration.</note> receive well-deserved thanks
From kind <term>princes</term> <note>original: "principes"; here meaning patrons or high-ranking leaders in general.</note>, and they received worthy
Rewards; nor could they return anything greater to them
Than praise, and a name remaining eternal through the ages.

<summary>The poet praises Bishop Niccolò Franco's successful diplomatic career as a papal legate to Persia and Venice, contrasting his past public service with his current devoted care for his "flock" in Treviso. He expresses his intent to immortalize the Bishop’s virtues through poetry, comparing his role to that of the great bards of antiquity.</summary>

# TO GIOVANNI ANTONIO
# SCHOLA, THE PRELATE FRANCO'S <note>original: "Franchipontificis"; a Latinized portmanteau of the Bishop’s surname, Franco, and "Pontifex" (prelate or bishop).</note>

## DISCOURSE II.

Why, Schola, do they claim that the root of Vir-
<margin>tue</margin> <note>The margin note "e" in the original appears to be a correction or continuation of the word "Virtutis" which was hyphenated in the text.</note>
is bitter, but that her fruits, full of a wonderful sweetness,
hang from a tree spread far and wide?
Is it because, while she alone can make and keep men Blessed,
she wished to present these same labors before herself?
Or is the fault truly in the soul? Which, soft and sickly,
finds excuses in that which is free from disease, and, being wicked,
mixes Virtue itself with vice because of compelling lust.
For there will be no labor at all in grasping virtue
anywhere, if a noble will is present for you
with passion—a will which is strong enough to soften hard things,
let alone being able to embrace sweet things with a studious heart.
If by chance she seems heavy to you at the beginning,
do not believe it to be truly so; rather, know that she then
appears different to inward, corrupted senses, and is seen
not clothed in that appearance which she ought to have had.
And just as sweet things placed before the taste of those who are
afflicted <note>This refers to the "sickly" mentioned earlier; the poet uses the medical analogy of a fevered person to whom honey tastes bitter.</note>, an unwelcome Bitterness [Amaror] mocks their sense:
so it is for those who have an ulcer in the soul, which dire desire [Cupido]...

<summary>This page begins a moral discourse addressed to Giovanni Antonio Schola, the nephew of Bishop Niccolò Franco, using the classical metaphor of virtue having a bitter root but sweet fruit. The author argues that any difficulty in pursuing a virtuous life stems from a "sickly" or corrupted soul rather than from Virtue herself.</summary>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral exhortation to Giovanni Antonio Schola. Having previously discussed how a "sickly soul" perceives virtue as bitter, the text now warns against the influence of corrupt men and directs the young man toward the virtuous examples found within his own family—specifically his grandfather and his uncle, the Bishop.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

...soul. If they have done so, they are unable to exercise the sharpness of an oppressed mind, so that they might know how much pleasure <term>voluptas; here meaning true, lasting satisfaction</term>, though seemingly separated by labor, joins eternally with her companion, virtue <term>virtus</term>. 

Avoid this class of men, lest their evil contagions creep into you, before you begin to be guided by firm strength and while the powers within your breast are still tender. For indeed, your examples need not be sought from elsewhere; you have those at home whom you may imitate, whether it pleases you to lead a life in the studies of learning <term>doctrinae</term>, or you wish to exert yourself in active affairs and extend your fame through your deeds. 

On one side let your Grandfather, and on the other let your Uncle <note>The Latin "auunculus" refers to a maternal uncle, specifically Bishop Niccolò Franco.</note> be present; let them add a spur to you as you run. You know how excellently the former lifts his mind on high, whenever he fills it with celestial light kindled by ethereal fires. 

As for the latter, see how he sustains the massive weight of affairs upon his broad shoulders, as often as he is ordered to undertake duties which the Majesty of the Supreme Pontiff <note>original: "Pontificis summi Maiestas"; referring to the Pope, whom the Bishop served as a diplomat.</note> would never have better entrusted to anyone else, nor in which any other man, however accustomed to the work, would have accomplished more across the diverse world. 

Therefore, let these leaders, under such great light, show you the Principal Path, by which you may safely proceed to the top of the tree, and—having left the bitter root behind—you may be able to reach the sweet fruits of virtue.

<header>SERMONS. 112</header>

# TO GIOVANNI ANTONIO
# SCHOLA, NEPHEW OF THE BISHOP
## OF FRANCO, an exhortation
## toward virtue.

### SERMON II.

<margin>c</margin>Why, Schola, do they claim that the root of Virtue <term>virtus; a term implying both moral excellence and the strength of character</term> is bitter, but that fruits full of a wondrous sweetness hang from her widely spreading tree? Is it because, since she alone is able to make and keep men Blessed, she wished to also present labors before herself? Or is the fault truly in the soul? A soul which, being soft and sickly, finds cause for complaint in that which is free of disease, and, being wicked, mixes Virtue itself with vice because of a driving lust. For indeed, there will be no labor anywhere in the embracing of virtue, if a noble will is present within you with an ardent spirit—a will which is strong enough to make hard things grow soft, let alone being able to embrace sweet things with a studious heart. If by chance she <note>The author personifies Virtue as a female figure, following the classical tradition.</note> seems heavy to you in the beginning, you should not believe it to be truly so; rather, she then appears as something else to your corrupted internal senses, and does not seem clothed in that appearance which she ought to have. Just as when sweet things are set before the taste of those who are physically afflicted <note>original: "affectis"; likely referring to those suffering from a fever or illness that distorts their sense of taste</note>, an unpleasant bitterness deludes the sense; so it is for those who have an ulcer in the soul, which dire desire...

<summary>This page introduces Sermon II, using the metaphor of a tree to argue that while virtue may seem bitter at first, its fruits are sweet, and the perceived difficulty lies in the "sickness" of the observer's soul rather than in virtue itself.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Antonio Schola, Virtue, moral philosophy, metaphor of the tree, soul's health, perception of vice</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Sermon" addressed to Giovanni Antonio Schola. Having established that the perceived "bitterness" of Virtue is a result of a clouded or "sickly" mind, the author now warns Schola to avoid the company of those who cannot see the joy in moral excellence. He transitions into a specific exhortation to follow the living examples of his own family members—his grandfather and his uncle, Bishop Niccolò Franco—who serve the Pope.</meta>

<header>BOOK II</header>

...bitterness. If they have done so, they are unable to exert the sharpness of a weighed-down mind to realize how much eternal pleasure <term>voluptas; here, the deep satisfaction of the soul</term>, though seemingly separated from labor, joins together with her companion, Virtue <term>virtus; moral excellence and strength</term>. Avoid this type of person, lest their evil contagion crawl into you, before you begin to be guided by a firm strength and while the powers within your heart are still tender.

For indeed, so that you need not seek examples from elsewhere, you have those within your own home whom you may imitate—whether you find joy in leading a life dedicated to the study of doctrine, or you wish to involve yourself in the management of affairs and extend your fame through your deeds. Here is your Grandfather <note>original: "Auus"</note>, and here is your Uncle <note>original: "auunculus"; likely referring to Bishop Niccolò Franco</note> at your side; let them add a spur to you as you run. 

You know well how excellently the former <note>The grandfather</note> lifts his mind to the heights, whenever he fills it with celestial light kindled by ethereal fires. This latter man <note>The uncle</note>, however, supports the massive weight of great affairs upon his broad shoulders, as often as he is commanded to undertake duties which the Majesty of the Supreme Pontiff <term>Pontifex summus; the Pope</term> has never entrusted to anyone better, and which no other man, accustomed to such things, has performed more extensively across the wide world. 

Let these men, therefore, be your guides under such great light; let them show you the special path <note>original: "Præcipuum Callem"</note> by which you may travel safely to the top of the tree so that, leaving the bitter root behind, you may finally be able to reach the sweet fruits of Virtue.

<summary>The author warns Schola to avoid corrupting influences and instead look to his grandfather and uncle as models of intellectual and political virtue. These family members, serving at the highest levels of the Papal court, illustrate how the "bitter root" of discipline leads to the "sweet fruit" of success and moral clarity.</summary>

# TO N. FRANCO, AN EXHORTATION
# THAT AFTER MANY LABORS
## he finally give himself

### SERMON III.

> *If it is worth the effort, when first from the shore*
> *the Helmsman <term>Moderator; one who governs or guides, here used as a ship’s captain</term> has loosened the rope*
> *and surrendered himself to the deep,*

So that even in the slightest danger, he must take care for all things, if he wishes himself and his companions to be safe when he is carried along by a powerful storm. In the same way, for one to whom the governance of affairs has fallen—for he will be tossed by no smaller a wave—it is necessary, Franco <note>Niccolò Franco (1444–1499), a prominent bishop and papal diplomat to whom this poem is addressed</note>, that he maintain all his strength and reason together, so that he may be able to overcome the rising tempests. For this reason, you know that Kings are accustomed to seek out from the whole multitude those men distinguished by virtue and suited by character, who may be ready to assist them with wise counsel. 

What then must we think of the "Little Ship" <term>Nauicula; likely a reference to the "Navicella" or the Ship of the Church</term> of the Supreme Father <note>The Pope</note>, pressed under such a massive weight of the sea? And under what a varied bundle of cares is he held? For you—whom your own prudence has made a master of your own skiff <term>Cymba; a small, fast boat</term>, so that you might occasionally handle its ropes and run across the vast sea with sails extended—now sustain so many and such great affairs, of which...

<header>BOOK II.</header>
<b>b</b>

You take on a greater burden than is equal for your shoulders to bear, though they have carried such great things for so long. Meanwhile, it does not occur to you how vain it is to gaze at the marble <note>original: *marmore*; likely referring to the white, gleaming limestone cliffs or a permanent, solid coastline</note> shore from afar, hoping to hide yourself in a safe harbor, unless you finally begin to turn the keel in that direction. But if by chance you ask where that place of rest might be: I could answer you by pointing to your own mountains <note>Likely referring to a specific villa or estate in the hills where Franco sought retreat</note>, relying on your own words and on the healthful reality of the place. They excel in the temperance of the air, such that they might even now restore you to health and proper strength quite quickly. Here too, an abundance of all things is supplied. There is also a pleasant field, secluded on all sides by retreats, where the mind can lay down its heap of troubles. Yet this place, although provided with the use of so many things, will not shake off the accustomed cares from an aching breast unless the mind is composed; the mind itself must make itself worthy of the place, it must guide the tiller, and it must finally enter here safe as a dedicated offering <note>original: *hostia*; the author uses the language of sacrifice to describe the traveler finally reaching the "altar" of the home or harbor</note> with full sails.

Your arrival here had been dearer to me than gold, dear Tryphon; but your departure is indeed unpleasant, and even...

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous dedication to Tryphon Gabriel. The speaker is now addressing Gabriel directly, reflecting on his sudden departure and transitioning into a philosophical critique of the "serious" affairs of the world.</meta>

### SERMONS.

It was no less unlovely than bitter aloe or gall, that you 
Left us so quietly and so suddenly. 
We had lived together for barely three full days, as we usually do, 
When we were both perhaps kept indoors by the rain and snow. 
But since chance itself has brought this about, I will now explain 
To you in this discourse what we had begun—and not without a smile, 
Which sometimes tastes good when it is appropriately sprinkled over serious matters. 
If you were to look closely at those things they call "great," and which they 
Fill with loud shouting, you would see they differ very little 
From the smallest things—even from those things they did in their childhood years 
With lighter and more playful concerns: 
Back then, they did everything moved by their senses as if by masters, 
And even now, driven by those same masters, 
They carry out these "greater" deeds—but not by those senses alone. 
For indeed, an evil mind has joined them, a wicked sister 
Leading a restless troop of frauds and ambitions 
Amidst the filth of greed and the weariness of luxury. 
And lest I delay you much, or let you go 
Too far, you yourself know—what is obvious everywhere— 
How sometimes vain trust <note>original: *fides*; here referring to political good faith or alliances</note> stands in the way of mortals, 
Preventing them, perhaps, from enjoying a desired peace for long. 
Now a deal is struck, and they come together; now there is 
Great joy throughout the forum—laugh! "Now we have conquered, everything," 
Laugh, "will be prosperous!" But indeed, it is not for a long time, and those 
Who are now allies, soon stand on opposing sides; oh, 
They run back into frauds again and again, into false 
Treaties <note>original: *foedera*</note> in brief moments; would you not choose to laugh? 
If only [you could see] how they differ from children playing with loose...

<summary>The poet laments the sudden departure of the humanist Tryphon Gabriel before turning to a satirical critique of the "great" affairs of men, which he compares to the fickle games of children driven by greed and ambition.</summary>

<keywords>Tryphon Gabriel, ambition, political satire, greed, treaties, childhood vs adulthood, human vanity</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's philosophical critique of human behavior. Having just discussed how adults engage in "games" of political fraud and shifting alliances, he now reflects on how these "serious" matters of war and statecraft are viewed by those who mistake them for true greatness, contrasted with the value of a just and contemplative life.</meta>

BOOK II.

If I were to see them agreeing with one mind in their games,
What lighter spectacles would they offer me then? Or why
Would a greater laugh overflow from my heart to my lips?
These same people even consider battles to be wonders and the greatest
Of affairs, and they think that the commanders are the first among men.
But I, considering wars to be monstrous works,
Condemn them, unless the sake of a better peace
Has moved the leaders, and the defense of what is right has armed the good.
For good men love the sacred things of peace;
Nature herself has kindly implanted a gentle feeling within their breasts.
Either Nature, our parent, has put these just, prudent,
Brave, and modest men in charge of managing affairs—
Whose lives are full of good things, yet full of labors—
Or she has established them as similar to the gods,
And taught the wise the causes of things, and granted them
To lead a happy life through golden leisure <term>otia</term>; <note>The Latin *otia* refers to "learned leisure"—time spent away from public business to focus on philosophy, writing, and self-improvement.</note>

# TO ALOVISIO ALEOTO OF TREVISO <note>original: *C. TAR.* likely refers to *Civis Tarvisinus*, a citizen of Treviso.</note>
## WHOM HE URGES TO BETAKE HIMSELF
## TO THE COUNTRYSIDE.

### DISCOURSE V.

The swallow both returns and, approaching us, crosses the 
<margin>❧</margin> sea;
Yet you, Aleoto, still care for the business of the City;
Nor does the grace of Pansacco Hill <note>Likely a reference to a specific rural estate or hill near Treviso or the Veneto region.</note> come to your mind;

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

As the new spring presents itself most beautifully everywhere,
In which it desires you to rise up with varied cultivation.
In the city, you manage so many things for yourself, your kin, and your friends,
As if you should place all your own concerns after the interests of your friends;
A great duty indeed, which sets you alone apart from the common crowd,
And adds you to the heavenly beings while you still walk the earth.
In the country, you nourish and refresh the mind along with a strong body,
Where suitable rest and easy labor bring balance to them both.
All things here are joyful by day, all things pleasing by night,
When weary limbs find rest from light exertion.
The tiny ant, while winter rages, consumes in its narrow hollow
The food it gathered all through the summer;
Soon, when the Dog Star <note>original: *Sirius*. In antiquity, the rising of the star Sirius was associated with the "dog days" of intense summer heat.</note> burns the earth, that same ant leaps
From its hiding place, and, nimble under the open sky,
Deploys its thick column; each diligent ant hastens to its task,
Persisting in its work. Thus, perhaps, in these same seasons
We ought to dwell in the country or the city;
For the times of human life are divided—
If only corrupt custom did not subvert these very rhythms.

# BOOK II

*i*

Long ago, you became accustomed to seeking such laughter;
I long to laugh with you again at something that offers itself
As worthy and suitable for our own guffaw <note>original: *cachinno*. This refers to a loud, unrestrained, or mocking laugh.</note>.
Here, therefore—if ever your own mind was suited
For joking matters, if ever you wished to be admitted
Among the humorous, so that you might touch others
With laughter—give yourself to me easily here, so that you too,
While laughing, might become the one mocked by the entire crowd.
The common people sometimes confess they do not know
What things are good and what are not, whenever no passion <note>original: *libido*. In this context, it refers to irrational desire or emotional impulse that clouds judgment.</note>—
Which is rare—has cast deep shadows over their minds.
Yet sometimes the crowd marvels at itself for its skill
In knowing these things; they do not believe anyone else
Has a better grasp of these distinctions than those whose teachings
They receive with gaping mouths, crowded shoulder-to-shoulder
Beneath the high pulpits <note>original: *pulpita*. The raised platforms from which a preacher or orator speaks to a congregation.</note>.
Just as we saw recently: while the crowd attends
The holy rites, so that they might build up a high heap of piety:
Early in the morning they gather in a throng, a mix of men, tender girls,
Old men, and boys. It was their anxious care
To seize a spot beforehand—perhaps one they had hoped for
In the midst of the shade, or had already prepared at nightfall—
So that, packed close together and with ears pricked up nearby,
They might carry home the words they heard from the kindly old man.
Therefore, when the people have heard him and those like him so aptly,
Do you think they will actually do anything? Whatever one of these
Men might command—even if he were truly wiser than that man
Who was once snatched up from the earth to the high heavens <note>original: *æthera*. A reference to the Apostle Paul, who in the New Testament (2 Corinthians 12:2) describes being caught up to the "third heaven."</note>,
And saw the gods more closely and heaven <note>original: *cælum*</note> opened wide:

As she <note>Likely referring to Nature or the Earth personified.</note> presents herself everywhere at her most beautiful in the new spring:
Where she longs for you to ascend in varied cultivation.
In the city, you manage many affairs for yourself, your family, and your friends:
As if you put your own interests behind the needs of your friends.
A grand gift indeed, which singles you out alone from the common crowd:
And it adds you to the company of the heavenly ones while you still move upon the earth.
In the countryside, you nourish and refresh your mind along with a healthy body,
When fitting rest and easy labor temper them both.
Here all things are joyful by day; all things here are pleasing by night,
When tired limbs have rested from light labor.
The tiny ant <note>original: *formica*. This is a classical reference to Horace’s Satires, where the ant is used as a model of foresight and hard work.</note> in her narrow hollow, while winter rages,
Consumes the food which she had prepared all summer long.
Soon the same creature emerges from her hiding places when <term>Sirius</term> <note>The "Dog Star," whose rising in the summer was associated by the ancients with the most intense, parching heat of the year.</note> burns the lands:
She leaps out, and, nimble under the open sky, she unfolds her
Dense column; and each one hurries to her own labor
Diligently, persisting in the work. So, by chance, under these same
Conditions, we must spend our time either in the country or the city:
For the times of human life are divided into their own seasons—
If only wicked custom did not pervert these very things.

# BOOK II.

For a long time now, you have been accustomed to seek such laughter, and I am eager to laugh with you once more at something that offers itself freely—something truly worthy and fitting of our shared mockery <note>original: *cachinno*. This refers to a loud, unrestrained laugh or a jeer, often used in satirical poetry.</note>. Therefore, if ever your mind was suited for humorous things, or if you ever wished to be admitted among the jokers so that you might touch others with your wit, give yourself to me here—provided that while you laugh, you are prepared to be laughed at by the whole crowd in return. 

The common people sometimes admit they do not know what is good and what is not, as long as no whim <note>original: *libido*. Here meaning a passionate desire, impulse, or caprice that clouds judgment.</note> has cast deep shadows over their minds—though this is a rare occurrence. Yet at other times, the commoner marvels at his own ability to recognize these things; he believes no one has a better sense of judgment than he does, even as he huddles with others, shoulder to shoulder, before the high pulpit <note>original: *pulpita*. A raised platform for a speaker, teacher, or preacher.</note>, catching every word with an open mouth.

This is just like the scene we saw recently: while the crowd attends the holy services, which were meant to build a great heap of piety <note>original: *pietatis*.</note>; in the morning, a frequent throng of men mixed with tender girls, old men, and boys. Their anxious concern was to seize a place beforehand—perhaps choosing a spot in the shadows or even preparing it since nightfall—so that, packed tightly together and with ears pricked up, they might bring home the words heard from the lips of the nourishing old man. 

When the people have listened aptly to him and his kind, do you think they will actually do anything? Do you think they will follow what one of these men has commanded, even if that speaker were truly wiser than the man who was once snatched up from the earth into the high heavens <note>original: *aethera*. A reference to the "third heaven" or the dwelling place of the divine.</note> and saw the inhabitants of heaven and the open sky <note>original: *coelum*.</note> from up close? <note>This is a likely reference to the Apostle Paul, who in 2 Corinthians 12 describes being "caught up to the third heaven." The author is questioning whether the crowd's devotion is merely performative, as they rarely act on the wisdom they hear.</note>

<i>

<summary>The author begins Book II by inviting a friend to join him in mocking the hypocritical behavior of the masses. He describes a crowd eagerly gathering for a sermon, noting that despite their outward devotion and effort to secure good seats, they rarely put the teachings into practice.</summary>
<keywords>satire, crowds, hypocrisy, sermons, Book II, human nature, piety, religious devotion</keywords>

# OF THE SERMONS.

Do not believe it for a moment, for as soon as he departs, right then and there he leaves behind the **instructions**, the laws he was commanded to follow, and the morals; he is immediately carried back along his own previous footsteps. For he either seeks out vain honors once again, or an unhealthy pleasure <note>original: *malesana uoluptas*. This refers to a "sickly" or "insane" pursuit of physical gratification that clouds the mind.</note> attacks him once more. Or, if he is a bit more sordid—which happens to him quite often—he runs through a thousand dangers of a harsh death toward immoderate gains and a paltry bit of profit <note>original: *lucellū*, a diminutive of *lucrum*, implying a small, shameful gain.</note>. 

Not to mention every single detail: the wicked man is led away from there by as many ruinous paths as there are cares disturbing his wretched heart. Clearly, he feels and approves of an entirely different "good" than what he has heard so many times, and which everyone else praises to the heavens with well-deserved acclaim—those who adorn the most holy <term>Dogmata</term> <note>These are established philosophical or religious principles; in this context, the "truth" being preached from the pulpit.</note> with truthful words. 

For he considers especially venerable that which his own greedy desire <note>original: *cupido*</note> dictates and persuades to his weak soul <note>original: *animo molli*</note>. Nor does he long for anything more than what he currently lacks; whatever he needs, he thinks that alone is the ultimate goal of all "goods" <note>original: *finem... bonorum*. This refers to the *summum bonum*, or the "highest good," which philosophers argued should be virtue, but the commoner identifies only with material needs.</note>. 

Because of this, you might see some—and who would not laugh at this?—rushing headlong as if driven by biting puppies. Then you might see others, by contrast, moving in the sickly manner of a slow tortoise <note>original: *testudinis*</note>, measuring everything with a fixed pace, proceeding and counting out their steps by number. Between these, you might find some in the middle: who you might find now so sluggish, and then so hasty in a single moment of time, behaving so differently that you would say they were almost dissimilar, and not the same people at all. Nor do they stop showing themselves in many different appearances...

## BOOK II

i ii

You might consider that man a beast, one accustomed to reflecting whoever it carries on its back or whatever color it touches. How diversely such people desire things that ought not be desired! And what of those driven by gloomy fear and a mind always conscious of its own guilt? How pale they appear to be!

I would imagine that even those pale ghosts—which appear in such strange ways to weary mortals in the dead of night—are not so ghastly or sallow. It has pleased the vain and wretched fear of humanity to call these phantoms <term>Lemures</term> <note>original: *lemures*. In Roman mythology, these were the restless spirits of the dead who returned to haunt the living, often requiring rituals to appease them.</note>, <term>Larvae</term> <note>original: *laruae*. These were perceived as malevolent spirits or "frighteners," often associated with the masks worn to represent the dead.</note>, and <term>Horci</term> <note>original: *horcos*. Referring to Orcus, a god of the underworld; here used to denote demons or spirits of the dark abyss.</note>.

Doesn't laughter burst forth from deep within your chest when you see how slight causes often creep into the minds of certain men? Out of nothing, they imagine for themselves the wealth of kings, great empires, and sublime status. And so, puffed up beyond what words can easily describe, they strut about. They exult in every form of foolish joy, just like a ball filled with empty air <note>original: *inani concita flatu... pila*. A vivid metaphor comparing a person's vanity to an inflated leather ball that is kicked by a youth and fills the streets and crossroads with its aimless jumping.</note> that is kicked along by a youthful impulse.

Where are you dragging me, you mournful ones? You who cling to your sorrow and are forever stained by your cheap complaints? How much laughter do you think you have provoked in me with your cracking voices and the foul lamentations from your mouths? Even your faces are clouded with a dark gloom! And for what? Perhaps over the smallest matter. Even if it seems like a great crisis to you, there is no ready help for it, or rather, your soul admits no aid by which its weakness might be relieved.

<summary>The author mocks the psychological instability of humans, comparing the guilty to pale ghosts and the vain to air-filled balls, while criticizing those who wallow in self-pity over trivial matters.</summary>

# OF THE SATIRES
<note>original: *Sermonum*. This title refers to the Horatian tradition of "Sermones," or "Conversations," a genre of verse satire that discusses moral failings and social habits with a mix of humor and philosophy.</note>

Have you not heard the wise man say? Such things never
Befall him; I know you have heard these things often: and yet nothing
From those lessons comes to you. Therefore my laughter 
Is deservedly doubled here. Therefore, my dear
Castrus <note>The addressee, likely a friend or patron of the author.</note>, smile upon me more readily. Consider these works 
To be quite mad, and as a sane man, flee whatever 
The common crowd relishes. 
Nor should you desire anything too much—neither my things nor your own—
Nor should you pursue joys themselves excessively, and above all, 
Cast away sad cares: 
Which, although they may affect all other things, 
Can in no way persist in any wise man. 
Nor should you provide the material for laughter yourself, 
You who are accustomed to sprinkling others with honorable wit. 
I wanted to address you with these things, and also to play 
With you as a friend now, and I was preparing to laugh with many others: 
But a new and true laughter has prevented me, so that I 
Can hardly contain myself from being led into the highest joys: 
And perhaps I display a gladness that seems excessive 
To the stern and the serious. But who would not allow anyone 
To rejoice in such true and solid goods? 
But truly, so that the prosperous things that recently 
Befell me might not be hidden from you, and so that I might be able 
To make you a partner in my affairs: I will delay you again with these few words. 
You know the Friend whom fate has granted me— 
Perhaps happy in this one thing, though fate is often contrary 
In other matters, and has tossed us about in various injustices. 
While he was recently traveling to and from the city <note>original: *urbem*. In this context, it likely refers to Rome or the primary administrative center of the region.</note> 
And even to the Venetians <note>original: *venetos*. Referring to the territory or people of Venice.</note> concerning great matters, 
An illness <term>full of dread</term> <note>original: *formidine multa plena ualitudo*. This suggests a sudden, terrifying medical crisis rather than a lingering ailment.</note> invaded him, by which he, for many [days]...

### BOOK II.

Heavily afflicted by night and by day, he finally 
Prevailed over the cruel force of the disease, thanks to the gods <note>The author uses the plural *diis* (gods) in the classical style common among Renaissance humanists, even when referring to Christian providence.</note>; and now, 
As is his custom, he approaches great duties with his 
Highest strength, and performs various and faithful service 
To the supreme Pope <term>Pontifici: The Pope, the head of the Catholic Church</term>, a service that no other could have 
Handled better for the benefit of Christians <note>original: *christicolis*, literally "Christ-worshippers."</note>. For besides the 
Honorable and vast experience of affairs which remains in him, 
And his powerful intellect, you should believe that nothing 
Is done by him without the divine will of the gods; 
So much does he apply a mind equal to his virtues, 
So much does he hate the mockeries of the common crowd, 
While the religion of our fathers is purely preserved. 
Indeed, he himself might even be justly called 
To this very laughter—he who has allowed us to compose 
Such playful works, and other more serious ones, which I think 
He will grant when he arrives, so that I might be able to add 
Even more to them. 
Pray to the gods above together with me that they keep 
Him safe for me; for I do not desire them to grant or 
Return anything greater to us now than this. 
Nevertheless, meanwhile, endure with me the harsh 
Attempts of that cruel goddess <note>A reference to Fortuna (Fortune), often depicted as a fickle and sometimes cruel ruler of human affairs.</note> who they say reigns, 
And as often as she sets herself against you, meet her and 
Overcome Fortune with virtue <term>uirtute: moral excellence and manly courage</term> and brave deeds;

# AFTER DECLARING TO HIM VARIOUS

## SERMON VII.

*¶ "Where am I being swept away? From where have I strayed? What insane*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*desire* <note>original: *cupido*. Referring to passionate or erotic longing.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*transforms wretched me? Why are those same things not*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*dear to my heart,*
*Which for so long were so exceedingly pleasing?*
*Why do I now beg the gods above, with every form of*
*supplication, for what recently displeased me? I pray that they*
*might deign to grant me that same thing; and having worshipped*
*at every altar with my prayers,*
*I fall prostrate, and turning only this over in my hardened heart,*
*I pray: may these new vows replace my old, contrary ones?*
*Ah, it is foolish for mortals to hope that anything remains*
*safe for long! Ah, why should I, in my misery, have feared such a thing?*
*For me, whose promised loyalty, preserved through so many years,*
*was so quickly dissolved by the poison of Fortune,*
*collapsing in the sudden moment of a brief hour."*
*As I was passing by, Antonio, I heard a young man lamenting*
*these words, which I recorded with a mindful soul;*
*and pitying his unfair lot within myself,*
*I approached more closely, for he happened to be a companion*
*of mine, and I inquired what indeed might be the cause of such*
*great sorrow.*

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page where the poet, having encountered a young man lamenting his lost love and the cruelty of Fortune, approaches to offer comfort. <header>BOOK II.</header>

He, however, neither replied to me in these matters, nor
Did he let any words of consolation reach his ears:
But rather, he poured out an immoderate flood of tears:
Looking at me, and embracing me like one insane, until,
Overcome by my entreaty and with his weeping finally subsided,
He began to open his mouth thus:
"Alas, do not seek to be taught the hard fate of another:
A fate which can never be softened by any means.
And believe that nothing is more faithless, nor more cruel,
Than the loving mind of a woman: <note>original: *muliebris amans animus*. A common trope in Renaissance and Classical poetry regarding the perceived fickleness of a lover's heart.</note> if by chance for a short while
Love should slacken its previously intense and solid hold."
Only these things did he say: and again, as tears welled up,
He embraced me and then departed of his own accord,
Leaving me there in pity.
Astonished by this act, I can hardly believe
Any mind could be so ungrateful: that it would not only
Permit someone to be so deceived, but would even disdain a lover.
Has anyone loved so patiently—for we know of all such cases—
Or been able to endure such great labors for so long?
Who cherished his Mistress with better verses? <note>original: *numeris potioribus*. Literally "better numbers," referring to poetic meter and the quality of the love poetry written for her.</note> And who,
To speak more precisely, said or did more for the benefit
Of any person than he? But indeed, all things in their turn
Rotate in a cycle, and Loves <note>original: *Amores*. Here personified as the various experiences or spirits of romantic passion.</note> are especially overturned.
Therefore, the more tender this age of yours is, the more
Is it necessary for me to linger with you over these matters:
Things which may benefit you now, and perhaps more healthily
Assist you later, when the same age comes to you matured by years.
In the beginning, Nature <note>original: *Natura*. Presented here as a creative, governing force.</note> granted to weary mortals <note>original: *mortalibus ægris*. A classical poetic expression (found in Virgil and Lucretius) referring to the inherent frailty and suffering of the human condition.</note>
A certain soft care, which is called "to love":
And she added sharp fires mixed with sweet ones.

->i iiii<- <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of the book in the correct order.</note>

<vocab>Loves, Nature, a woman's loving mind, weary mortals</vocab>

<summary>The poet recounts a brief, emotional encounter with a heartbroken friend who warns against the fickleness of love before departing in tears. This prompts the poet to begin a philosophical discourse on the dual nature of love—both sweet and painful—as ordained by Nature for humanity.</summary>

<keywords>unrequited love, fickleness, nature of love, poetic devotion, youth and age, human suffering, Giovanni Antonio Schola</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the poet's philosophical inquiry into the nature of love following his encounter with the grieving young man. Having discussed the fickleness of the beloved, the poet now explores the internal psychological conflicts and the various classifications of lovers.</meta>

<header># OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

With allurements, lest that state should not be varied, and the same 
Might fight within itself with those same opposing parts.
From this it follows, that those who love often, of their own will,
Disagree with themselves, and soon desire things different from before. 
Nor could you find almost anyone, for a long time or much duration,
To have loved—even if he were loved in return—
Who did not at last love in vain, frustrated in the end. 
Desire <note>original: *cupido*</note> itself, also divided into many parts,
Defends itself through many and various authors. 
For indeed, a base lust <note>original: *libido*</note> delights some so much
That they think nothing is more to be sought by man than this. 
Of these, it is as shameful for us to speak as it is for them
To act obscenely: whom we should deliberately pass over,
Lest our discourse begin to grow filthy with such stains. 
There are also those whom a pleasant <note>original: *genialis*</note> impulse holds and carries
Above all, but reason <note>original: *ratio*</note> accompanies them, and 
Does not allow them to go further than is right: although these too often
Turn aside from the set path, and through detours at last
Are sometimes carried headlong into the wide Abyss. <note>original: *Barathrum*. In antiquity, this referred to a deep pit in Athens for criminals, but in poetry it signifies the depths of ruin or the underworld.</note>
Therefore, it is not easy to grasp their various habits in number,
Since for these, while loving, now the spirit <note>original: *animus*</note> persuades one thing,
Now the mind <note>original: *mens*</note> another: which, soon conquered,
Succumbs, and allows him loose reins. 
But there is a part—though it is now most rare—which
Admires only beauty, if nature has created any
That is exceptional, and has shown it to be seen in our world. 
Whom no one would refuse to call excellent observers of beauty,
Nor would they deny that these same are Lovers.

<meta>catchword: With praises</meta>

That they are worthy of eternal and outstanding praise:
From this, one who is outstanding in wondrous genius, whether in 
Composing verses, or who is elegant in leading his whole life 
With good morals, or who is fit to exercise any 
Measure of virtue; whenever he descends into these pastimes <note>original: *ludicra*. Refers to the "games" or "pursuits" of love.</note>, he is far from
Losing any of these qualities: rather, he renders them better 
And more powerful at every hour, aided by a new grace.
No less from this source, his breast girt with strong might,
The Lover himself also sometimes takes strength for himself.
Since we think you have already read of these strengths in many books:
Now, lest I detain you by lingering over the worn-out examples 
Of the ancients—which are open to many seekers—hear 
What your reverend uncle <note>original: *auunculus*. This refers to the author's maternal uncle, likely a diplomat or high-ranking cleric.</note> here recently reported having seen 
There at the very edge of the world, while he was honorably 
Performing his service for Pope Sixtus <note>original: *Xisto*. Likely Pope Sixtus IV (reigned 1471–1484), known for his patronage and political maneuvers.</note>; which service now,

Of another supreme father <note>The reigning Pope at the time of writing.</note>, he performs 
Well-respected among the Venetians <note>original: *Venetis*. The citizens of the Republic of Venice.</note> and pleasing to both sides.
He, therefore, as he is sometimes accustomed to do when he sets aside 
Serious matters, began courteously to relate to us a lover’s 
Wondrous deed <note>original: *facinus*. While this can mean a crime, in this context it refers to a remarkable or bold act.</note>: To whom, while he was boasting to his mistress
That he would do all things she commanded, she suddenly 
Had commanded that he bring back the heads of three slain enemies.
From this, therefore, he said that the man, trusting in the 
Strength of love, had gone forth, and in the midst of the battle, 
Having dared to immediately challenge any man, however 
Strong and brave, he soon put three young men to death with his 
Powerful sword: and the three faces, a gruesome sight, wet with fresh...

...carried [the trophies] bound in a single bundle before her modest feet, still [covered] in blood and foul filth alike. <note>original: *Sanguine adhuc turpiq; ſimul ſquallore*. This beauty alone might rouse your heart with a perpetual light, and keep you far removed from vice;

<header>BOOK II.</header>

Might be, and the same seems worthy to be read by you. <note>original: *Eſſe poteſt, eademq́; legi tibi digna uidetur*. This completes the sentence from the previous page: "Than that which might be [possible]..."</note> 
From this, the pleasure I perceived from you inflames me again, and the bonds, which once happily joined me in blessed duties, compel me to dare [to seek] holy draughts from the Aonian springs <note>original: *fontibus Aoniis*. In classical mythology, Aonia was a region of Boeotia containing Mount Helicon; the "Aonian springs" are the waters of the Muses, representing poetic inspiration.</note> in a long retreat. 
And now, stirred by the favor of the divine liquid, the Mind burns to reveal what it has long predicted for itself. 
Many things strive to display the kind appearance of your fortune: 
whether one considers in their mind the great advantages of the Kingdom <term>Regnum</term> <note>Referring here to the unified kingdoms of Spain under the Catholic Monarchs.</note>, which yield to no other happy lands; 
or that you will be the sole heir <term>Hæredem</term> to such great kings, upon whom all the hope and care of your parents <term>Parentes</term> rests; 
or whether one weighs the immense love of the peoples, and the rewards of a friendly nation, which are brought to you everywhere with a grateful heart. 
Although all these things proceed with an excellent and constant course, and are able to remove you alone from the mortal crowd, and need no other powerful help from elsewhere, nonetheless, it must be thought that not in a lower place is the fact that, endowed with a sacred character, you already reflect your lofty Parents with a nearly Royal virtue. 
Whom no one ever surpassed in the violence of war nor the gift of peace, and who worships the venerable divinity <term>Numen</term> with more holy religion <term>Relligione</term>, and venerates all the blessed heavenly beings <term>Cœlicolas</term> <note>original: *Cœlicolas*. Literally "those who dwell in heaven," referring to the saints or the divine court.</note> with a wholesome law. 
From which mortals are able to know with easy sense that a greater empire is owed to them, since no one serves men better, nor the Divinities.

<summary>In this opening to Book II, the author expresses his renewed poetic inspiration and praises Prince Juan de Aragón’s fortunate position as heir to the Spanish throne. He concludes that the Prince's true greatness lies not just in his power, but in his virtuous imitation of his parents' religious devotion.</summary>

### A LAMENTATION ON IMMINENT TIMES
<note>original: "Imminentium Temporum Querela". A poetic "complaint" or lament regarding the dangers facing the state or Christendom.</note>

### DISCOURSE IX.
<term>Sermo</term> <note>While "Sermo" often means a sermon in a religious context, in Renaissance Latin it frequently refers to a formal verse discourse or "talk" in the tradition of the Roman poet Horace.</note>

<margin>¶</margin> *By what movement of minds? Or what are these strivings? Toward what end, or from where does such great madness rush headlong?*
->*everywhere, far and wide*<-
*To be mixed into a horrendous crime, and to draw along with it in a narrow space the common, ignoble crowd mixed with the Princes, to whom the power over affairs has been granted. I myself, who am accustomed to season and scatter all things with laughter,* <note>The author compares himself to a satirist who usually uses wit, but finds the current situation too grave for jokes.</note> *now can scarcely refrain from tears; for never has the age that just passed sensed human affairs tossed and turned by a greater whirlwind. Do you not see how a dark storm* <term>Tempestas</term> *suddenly surrounds us? For the fierce wrath of the gods* <note>original: "Ira deum". A classical poetic way of describing divine judgment or natural disaster.</note> *strikes from on high at close quarters, and a dire fire rages more clearly. And the fatal sword hangs exulting over our necks. Alas, how badly it will have gone for wretched mortals, for whom countless deaths and wars are now prepared; they shall have no limit until scarcely a tiny, pitiable hand of the living survives from the vast slaughter.*

<summary>This page concludes a dedication to Prince Juan of Aragón and introduces a poetic lament (Sermo IX) regarding a period of extreme social and political turmoil. The author shifts from the language of courtly praise to an apocalyptic warning of war, divine wrath, and the "madness" of the masses and rulers alike.</summary>

<keywords>Prince Juan of Aragón, Lamentation, divine wrath, war, social upheaval, Renaissance poetry, Sermo, Spanish history</keywords>

### BOOK II.
<meta>Visible ghosting of text from the reverse side of the page.</meta>

Alas, toward what years do we, an unhappy race, decline?
But what use will unceasing voices and such great laments be, 
if the fates command and the stars show
an unspeakable destruction, and a monster that none can overcome:
Whether hellish Bellona <term>Bellona</term> <note>The Roman goddess of war, often associated with the fury of battle.</note> wearies the gatherings of men,
Or the dreadful sister of the Eumenides <term>Eumenides</term> <note>The Furies, ancient Greek deities of vengeance and divine retribution.</note> and Discord harass us,
Or if Father Mars <term>Mauors</term> <note>An archaic name for Mars, the Roman god of war.</note> himself sharpens his weapons for the battles of war.
How a varied and altogether different sentiment from our usual one
has settled upon us—we, whom for so many years
a nourishing quiet had long cherished under sweet peace.
Iron is in every hand; there is no one who does not now
practice for battle, meditating within himself, nor does he look back
at the dark aftermath: what dire hunger, what ghastly plague,
and what violence follows with headlong step.
We did not lead our lives in this way during the years gone by:
these things were absent, and for a long time such great mercy of heaven <note>original: "clementia cœli"</note>
presided over affairs, so that all things flourished on the earth.
Indeed, the noble arts <term>artes bonae</term> <note>Literally "the good arts," referring to the liberal arts and humanities that flourish in times of stability.</note> grew, and no one lacked
the means to learn as much as he wished.
From this, so many refinements of manners exist in the peaceful cities,
And such great cultivation <term>cultura</term> by the farmers for the tilling of the fields.
From this came that abundant supply of books <note>original: "librum numerosa facultas." Likely a reference to the rise of the printing press during this era.</note>:
And a wonderful ingenuity for things came to men.
After these things, even certain lesser matters returned:
but pleasing ones, delighting the minds of many men:
Such as those which imitate living faces in marble <note>original: "marmoribus"</note>,
or in bronze, or those which represent everything in liquid color. <note>The author is describing the "Sister Arts" of sculpture and painting.</note>
Or those which wonderfully soothe the ears with sounds and songs.

<summary>The author laments the end of a long "Golden Age" of peace and prosperity, describing how the flourishing of the arts, printing, and agriculture is now being threatened by the sudden, divinely-ordained onset of war and pestilence.</summary>
<keywords>War and Peace, Bellona, Mars, Liberal Arts, Renaissance culture, Printing, Sculpture, Painting, Decline of Society</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Discourse IX, transitioning from a lament over the loss of a peaceful "Golden Age" to a philosophical discussion on free will versus astrological fate. It then introduces a formal dedication to Andrea Trevisan and begins the tenth Discourse.</meta>

<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>

*And those arts which renew cities with lasting ornaments,*
*And the various hands of craftsmen, which softly add*
*Beauty to ivory or silver; indeed, much labor*
*Is now spent on gold, so that it might stand out as far more precious.*
*And a new grace is added to natural gems.*
*Truly, that powerful age of old had already begun*
*To return, driven by the turning courses of the stars:*
*Now that same age is carried backward, having slipped away more quickly*
*Than the new beginnings of such great things might demand.*
*An unquiet star <term>sydus</term> <note>original: "sydus." The author refers to a celestial body or astrological influence believed to burn or "brand" humanity with the current era's suffering.</note> burns this wound into us.*
*But even if the cause remains in heaven, it does not force*
*Anyone to submit to the violence of fate <term>fatum</term>, nor does the divine will*
*Give free necks to a slavish yoke unless they do so willingly.*
*Therefore, whatever the stars prepare, whatever they threaten,*
*We, if we are wise <term>prudentes</term> <note>Refers to the concept of "Prudentia," or practical wisdom, which was considered a cardinal virtue capable of steering one through the dangers of fortune.</note>, are not unable to avoid them.*
*These things which hang over us are dire, if I am not mistaken,*
*Grave for everyone, and linked to various dangers:*
*But they must be avoided nonetheless; for they can indeed be made*
*Lesser, if in response a mind is given to us from heaven,*
*And if our wisdom is greater than the fate of things;*

### BISHOP OF FELTRE,
### A COMMENDATION
### *Of the Discourses to be Published.*

### DISCOURSE X.

Whoever adopts for himself children of another lineage,

<summary>This page concludes a poem on how human wisdom (prudence) can overcome the negative influences of the stars and fate, followed by a formal dedication to the Bishop of Feltre for the publication of these Discourses.</summary>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Andrea Trevisan, following the author’s philosophical reflection on fate and wisdom. Here, the author employs the common Renaissance trope of "books as children," explaining his hesitation and eventual decision to publish these discourses after long refinement.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

He loves these, and as a wise man <term>prudens</term> he nourishes them as his own; and yet within
He preserves the innate love of his own offspring.
But he who receives children from a fertile wife,
Prefers them to all other things with a marvelous effort;
And so much so that sometimes he considers them more important than himself,
Such that he neglects to avoid the dangers of a varied fate.
Thus I have prepared these discourses <term>sermones</term> <note>original: "sermones." These are the "Discourses" or formal essays that make up the volume.</note> as if they were pledges <term>pignora</term> <note>original: "pignora." Literally "pledges" or "tokens," a poetic term frequently used by Roman and Renaissance authors to refer to their children.</note>
Led out and nourished from my mind with equal strength.
For their sake, it is now necessary that I experience what fortune can do,
And as a parent together with my offspring, I must undergo
Judgments feared by me according to an ancient example.
For just as a young girl, under a strict mother, grows eager
To be seen by crowds of young men, yet is incautious and fearful,
So my little books <term>libelli</term> <note>original: "libelli." A humble diminutive for his writings.</note>, perhaps cautious with a fear that is not quite right,
Desire to be unrolled and read by a learned reader.
And yet I have nourished these within the thresholds of a quiet house,
And I have adorned them, and warned them, lest by chance they should go out
Into the public unkempt, or touched by any stains at all.
For this reason, they are now proceeding to see their ninth summer <note>This is a reference to the Roman poet Horace’s advice in the *Ars Poetica* that a writer should wait nine years before publishing a work to ensure it is sufficiently polished.</note>
Since the time I—standing nearby, diligent and serious—prepared them 
With no easy or small labor; and I urge them 
Not to be so pleased with themselves until they first test 
The ears of a proper judge, such as those who, through the art of Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and craft. The author is complimenting Trevisan's intellectual discernment.</note>,
Like you, are purged of the filth of empty talk.
Yet they think they must be published, since they have endured
A delay for the proper amount of time, and the labor of the file <term>Limæ laborem</term> <note>original: "Limæ laborem." A classical metaphor for the tedious process of editing, revising, and "smoothing out" a piece of writing.</note>.
Therefore, come now; with the gods helping well and beautifully, 
We loosen the cable from the shore, and stretching the sails of our little boat <term>Cymbæ</term> <note>original: "Cymbæ." A small skiff; a common literary metaphor comparing the act of writing or publishing to a voyage at sea.</note>

<summary>In this passage, the author compares his writings to his children, explaining that he has spent nine years refining them before publication. He pays a high compliment to Andrea Trevisan’s judgment while using classical metaphors of craftsmanship and seafaring to describe the start of his intellectual journey.</summary>

<keywords>paternal metaphor, book publishing, Horace’s rule, Pallas Athena, literary refinement, Andrea Trevisan, sailing metaphor</keywords>

# ON THE DISCOURSES.

We enter the vast sea while the West Winds <term>Zephyris</term> <note>original: "Zephyris." The gentle west winds of spring, often symbolizing a favorable start to a poetic or intellectual journey.</note> are breathing.
But if my little boat <term>phaselus</term> <note>original: "phaselus." A light, narrow galley; a common metaphor in Roman poetry for a small or humble literary work.</note>
Should strike any rocks or hidden shallows—for many such things are encountered 
By those wandering far and wide—bring your help immediately, O best of men,
And act according to the custom of one who is noble, good, and learned.
Perhaps many others will also be with you for my sake, just as those 
Who care to join learning to an excellent nature,
Being already learned, have attained the venerable name of the "good."
From there, as often as the breeze of our ship <term>Ratis</term> <note>original: "Ratis." The author shifts from a tiny boat (phaselus) to a more substantial vessel (ratis) as he gains confidence in his supporters.</note> shall breathe,
She will, in her happiness, avoid those heavy, swelering tides.
I was singing these things to you while resting under a leafy cliff
Of Montello <note>Il Montello is a distinctive red-earth hill in the Treviso province of Italy, famous in the Renaissance for its woods and as a retreat for Venetian humanists.</note>, which rejoices in the eager love of the Nymphs:
After Bellona, the glory of the Water-Nymphs <term>Naiadum</term>, withdrew to this place,
And the river Sile <term>Silim</term> <note>The Sile is a major river in the Veneto. It is unique because it is fed by "resurgences"—underground springs that bubble up into the light.</note> grew stiff as it fled from its hidden
Sources, through which it wanders here in long, winding paths
Beneath the earth, and then exits again, limpid, into the light.
For they say that as soon as she was turned, by the nods of the Gods,
Into a green hill in exchange for her virginity,
An innumerable number of Goddesses came to this place.
Here came the Glade-Nymph <term>Napæa</term>, her head crowned with flowers and garlands;
Here the Wood-Nymph <term>Dryas</term>, encircling her temples with the acorn-bearing oak;
And the Mountain-Nymph <term>Oreas</term>, sitting upon the very peak of the mountain;
Likewise the Water-Nymph <term>Nais</term>, closest to the gliding water.
Together they all directed their steps, and to them was added
Echo, who rejoices in springs, caves, and mountains:
But they favor no part with more devotion
Than that where they wander upon the fruitful hills of Marcello <note>The Marcello family were prominent Venetian patricians. This likely refers to their villa and agricultural estates in the hills of the Trevisan March.</note>;
And they show respect to the Lord of the place, who has contributed many things
Through his cultivation

She brought forth beautiful things for her own sake, and did not merely surrender them; she, though once inert, loved them by the pious will of the earth; and by which protector, now worthy of the Nymphs, the nymphs themselves are worthy of him.

# TO ALBERTO VONICO,
## KNIGHT OF TREVISO AND JURIST.
->Gold-making <term>Chrysopeia</term> <note>original: "Χρυσοποιΐα." A Greek term literally meaning "the making of gold," referring to the alchemical process of transmutation.</note><-
### DISCOURSE XI.

<margin>u</margin>So that you may know, Vonico, what things I happened to see, once the business of the day was set aside for a short while, hear things that might perhaps seem empty to you if you consider them lightly, yet they are true nonetheless. Do not believe them to be mere dreams. Be as a wise man, who sees many things once the veil <term>velamine</term> <note>The "veil" is a common Renaissance metaphor for the outer layer of poetry or allegory that hides deeper, often alchemical or philosophical, truths from the uninitiated.</note> is moved—that veil which, placed around us, so often obstructs misty eyes, so that they can scarcely discern the obscure differences of things. 

But proceed, O Muses, with this your work; let us now sing of things seen by few men. I had already crossed the middle of my journey <note>This is a likely allusion to the famous opening of Dante’s *Divine Comedy*: "Midway upon the journey of our life."</note> having myself endured a thousand monsters with horrific dangers in my fierce mind, when the weariness of a difficult life seized me. And now, exhausted, I was turning my back on those hostile places, had not the holy deities moved me to such great labors. For who could have crawled over so many vast crags without celestial power? Or through the woods and the dens of wild beasts?

<sig>K</sig>

<vocab>Alberto Vonico, Tarvisi, Chrysopeia, Sermo, Musæ, Nymphæ, alchemical poetry</vocab>

<summary>The author begins his eleventh discourse with a formal dedication to the Trevisan jurist Alberto Vonico, framing his alchemical and philosophical insights as a vision granted after a period of great personal struggle. He invokes the Muses and uses the metaphor of a "veil" to describe the hidden nature of the truths he is about to reveal.</summary>
<keywords>Alberto Vonico, alchemy, Chrysopeia, Muses, spiritual journey, Treviso, allegory, philosophical vision</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical journey introduced on the previous page. The speaker, having moved beyond the local geography of Treviso, now enters a metaphorical wilderness or "dark wood," common in alchemical and philosophical literature to represent the beginning of the Great Work or an intellectual quest.</meta>

<header>OF THE DISCOURSES.</header>
<meta>catchword: Quid</meta>

Would he have crossed the rivers running between severed valleys with his life intact, and safe from evils? 
But God brought aid to me in my exhaustion, and a certain path <term>Callis</term> <note>original: "Callis." In Latin, this refers to a narrow track or a mountain footpath, often used to signify a difficult or hidden road to wisdom.</note> immediately shone for me under a pure light, 
where previously shadows <term>tenebre</term> presented themselves on every side. 
At the beginning, I approached a rock torn from a great body, standing upright with a steep mass, 
where only a thin fissure <term>rima</term> opens to the traveler. 
And where Phoebus <note>The sun god Apollo. In alchemical texts, the absence of Phoebus often signifies the "nigredo" or blackening stage, where the "solar" light of reason is temporarily lost.</note> brings forth neither rays nor light, 
I wandered in blind darkness and deep night, 
until, having often retraced those foul places with a heavy step, 
I halted there, where a brief light revealed itself to me. 
Here, truly, I first sat down exhausted; looking forward and casting my eyes around at each thing, 
I was struck with fear, and perhaps would have turned my step back— 
but the moisture falling from the cliff in tiny drops restrained me from doing so. 
It urged me to relieve that sharp thirst which had long clung to my dry throat, 
and it persuaded me never to abandon the undertaking. 
So, refreshed now by a brief rest and small draughts, 
I resolved to continue the journey I had begun where the path was passable, 
once more under a dim <note>original: "maligna." Literally "malignant," but in this poetic context, it describes light that is stingy, deceptive, or barely visible.</note> light. 
I entered a deep valley <term>Convallem</term> darkened by mists and soaked by rains, 
prepared to endure whatever greater hardships might come. 
And here, planting slow footsteps in the crumbling soil, 
I spent forty-four days and nights. 
And now, I finally seemed to have reached the summit of the way, 
when all light completely vanished from my eyes.

<summary>The narrator describes a harrowing allegorical journey through a dark, sunless chasm where he is nearly overcome by fear and thirst. He is sustained by water from a rock and perseveres for forty-four days through a misty valley before reaching a summit where he is plunged into total darkness.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, spiritual journey, nigredo, darkness, perseverance, forty-four days, the path, thirst</keywords>

### BOOK II.

<sig>K ii</sig>

What was I to do? Or by what way could I be brought forth from such great shadows? 
Or where was I to turn myself back, without the accustomed light? 
Nor do I recount to you what streams and lakes were crossed in the meantime, 
which I escaped; or how often, in the midst of the waves, 
I held my ground, and plunged my very sides up to my neck. 
What vows did I not offer to God? Or what cries did I not 
endure to send forth? Or what just rites did I not perform in my mind? 
Then, amidst my prayers, a huge cloud seemed to me to be torn asunder 
with thunder, and through the pure Ether <term>Æthera</term> <note>The "Ether" refers to the high, clear air of the heavens, thought in ancient and alchemical philosophy to be the "fifth element" or the realm of the stars.</note> to be carried 
hastening away, and a most peaceful light was restored to the lands. 
And having meanwhile now passed beyond the dark valley, 
I had emerged into high fields, opening wide. 
And here, a sheep <term>ouis</term> sprinkled with diverse spots <note>The "diverse spots" and "varied colors" are classic alchemical markers for the *cauda pavonis* or "Peacock’s Tail" stage, where the matter under transformation displays a rainbow of colors before reaching the final white or red state.</note> over its whole 
body, and then by chance shimmering with varied color, 
sought a thin mantle <term>Amictum</term> of white-hot wool <gloss>lanæ candentis</gloss>
busily near the water, where a glassy stream <term>Riuulus</term> with its current 
passes by, immense with its waters, and around the flowery 
fields sounding with springs, it is carried with a gentle murmur. 
When I, having completed my vows, saw this creature very near, 
plucking the grasses, and at the wave of the shining river 
drying her fleece <term>uellus</term>, which she had dipped in the river before: 
immediately I stood amazed, kindled with a wondrous Love <term>Amore</term> 
to follow this excellent creature—to where she might stretch her steps 
from there, or wherever she might turn herself. 
While I weighed these things thus, revolving them in a steady mind, 
and now stretch my steps directly, and greedily draw near, 
she, with her neck held high, having left the tender flowers 
on the ground, went rejoicing toward me, and sought me out herself.

<summary>The narrator escapes the dark, watery trials of the previous stage and enters a bright field where he encounters a multi-colored sheep washing its fleece. This encounter represents a transition in the alchemical process, moving toward the "whitening" or purification stage.</summary>
<keywords>Ether, alchemical sheep, purification, cauda pavonis, fleece, spiritual journey, illumination</keywords>

# DISCOURSES.

And as if it had recognized its master, it immediately and of its own accord 
began to fawn upon me, to offer itself to my lap, and often to show 
its golden horns, and often its hooves, and sometimes to shift its 
many-colored side; it seemed to exult for me alone, who tended it 
with such great devotion and such great resources. 
But I, having honored it with as much respect as I could, 
received the creature, and gently cherishing it, I led it into a cave <term>antrum</term>: 
which by chance stood open, hollowed out of the rocky cliff, 
covered with green moss and thick clusters of berries <term>Corymbis</term> <note>original: "Corymbis"; these are clusters of fruit or ivy berries, often associated in classical poetry with lush, secluded natural settings.</note>. 
Then, having spread a bed <note>original: "thoro"</note> of chosen leaves and fresh foliage 
beneath it, I provided clear springs and rich 
fodder, and I labored to match its needs with equal care. 
Here, having cast off its old coverings <note>original: "exuuiis"; literally "spoils" or "sloughed skin," suggesting a metamorphic shedding like that of a snake.</note>, it first put on 
new fleeces <term>vellera</term>, and in its changes, it took them up again in so many turns 
as the many colors that Iris <note>The personification of the rainbow.</note> draws from the opposing 
splendor of the sun, when she reclines, curved from the heavens to the earth. 
Or as many as we often see the necks of birds display, 
varied with light movement—those birds which Juno <note>The queen of the gods, whose chariot was pulled by peacocks.</note> deigned to yoke 
to her chariot before all other birds, and upon whose feathers 
she wished to fringe the hundred eyes of Argus <note>In mythology, Juno placed the eyes of the giant Argus onto the peacock's tail. This is a poetic description of the *cauda pavonis* or "Peacock's Tail," a famous stage in alchemy where the matter displays all the colors of the rainbow.</note>. 
I passed twelve months <note>original: "Bissenos menses"</note> while these colors were thus shifting, 
until I saw everything begin to be

my breast, even as a joyful care beat within me. 
For I was contemplating what that rich fleece <term>uellus</term> had begun 
to turn into, and the rabid mouths [of wolves] brought no small 
fear to my mind—mouths which my watchful guarding 
could scarcely keep away from the lair, whether of wolves
