### BOOK II.

There was gluttony, or the madness of lions that had to be driven off;
And I feared those whom insane envy <note>original: "uefana [vesana] inuidia"</note> agitates.
But that fear was nevertheless in vain, nor was any force of men
Able to harm me further, nor any force of wild beasts.
The sheep fed here and there on flowering herbs,
Wandering through sunny fields, where no eternal
Winter <term>hyems</term> burns the crops with ice, nor summer <term>aeftas</term> with its suns.
Such is the temperateness of those fields, and the grace of heaven.
And a Tree <note>original: "Arbos"; capitalized in the text, perhaps personified or signifying a specific Tree of Life</note>, teeming with fruit, formerly offered me mild apples
As a pleasing nourishment from its bending branches
Always, and watered springs filled my cups.
And meanwhile, a second year had rolled through its arc:
And the day of the promised gift was pressing on, so that now
I happily hoped for a return, after a long time,
To my fatherland, and that the end of such great labor was near—
Unaware that there were six months of departing heat
For me to observe, and also just as many months under the cold.
For that which, growing up white with a marble-like color,
Already surpassed shining ivory <term>candens ebur</term> and bright swans,
Having gradually turned into some saffron redness,
Began little by little to be painted with Tyrian Purple <term>Murice</term> <note>original: "Murice"; a dye-source obtained from sea snails, symbolic of royalty, wealth, and spiritual perfection.</note>.
When I saw this, I then hoped for a greater work, then for golden backs;
And having allowed no labor to be preferred to such a great task,
I decided to spend all my time upon it equally.
Again, I gird myself to conquer such great burdens <term>moles</term>
Under better auspices.
Until, the cycle of allowed time being completed:
All the white parts began to reflect a purple color.

K iii

This transition suggests an allegorical or alchemical process of perfection requiring great patience.</summary>

And from the purple, a sudden golden color emerged.
Without delay, the sheep immediately breathes out gold
From its mouth and nostrils; and a heavy weight of fleece
Is present on its hollow body, and its feet and horns are golden.
<note>original: "aureis"; completed from the catchword on the previous page</note>
Here I was then, deeply struck by this sharp turn of events
And by joy; and eager to depart from the place, I first
Pay my vows to the gods and give thanks to the kind deities.
Soon, placing the burden upon my shoulders, I head for

## DISCOURSE XII.

Maximus, you who are so full of faith, and always

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's discourse to L. Maximus of Padua. Following the metaphorical journey of the Golden Fleece on the previous page, the author now addresses the reality of public criticism and the internal struggle to maintain one’s reputation against the "insane rumors" of the common people.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

I also used to pray to the heavenly beings <term>caelestes</term> <note>original: "cæleſtes"; the gods or divine powers.</note>: that I might be able to defend myself, exposed as I was to the insane rumors of the common people. But when neither the pious care of my parent <note>The previous page mentioned the speaker's mother; here he reflects on the limits of even a parent's protection against public scorn.</note> seemed to benefit me, nor even those prayers which I often made to the gods above, I collected myself and withdrew an account of time past. I calculated it, as if I were being led from the first beginning of the world to this highest age under which we now live. There, I found the vice of every age complaining about itself, putting nothing under itself without accusation, and praising nothing except what had arisen in years gone by. I found that divine poets <term>poëtas</term> were often made objects of mockery for the people from this very cause, while it was their lot to lead their lives; and likewise, those professing holy dogmas, who delight in the title of "wisdom," were everywhere despised before their death. Thus envy <term>livor</term> <note>original: "liuor"; literally a "bruise" or "bluish color," frequently used in Latin to describe the "discoloring" effect of spite or malice.</note> attacks all things with its venom, and badly consumes them with the bite of its black tooth. When I had often turned these things over in my mind, I thought that all these belonged to reckless Fortune <term>Fortuna</term>, and were not to be feared on that account, but rather scattered with laughter and held in contempt. You should also consider this, if by chance someone should choose to pluck at my songs <term>carmina</term> <note>original: "carmina"; can refer to poems, songs, or incantations.</note> or—what I would wish even less—opt to criticize my character: debate it with yourself, and assist yourself with a similar remedy <term>medela</term> as I have: for no one is able to restrain the stings of envy <term>invidia</term> <note>original: "Inuidiæ"; the classic personification of jealousy or the "evil eye."</note> when he perceives that the greatest things are attributed to the living; or when he sees things he cannot perform himself stored under another’s mind, and sees these things always becoming greater as time begins to gradually proceed further, and he grieves to be left behind in the middle space.

<sig>K iiii</sig>

<summary>The author reflects on the historical inevitability of envy, noting that great poets and philosophers have always been mocked in their own lifetimes, and urges his friend Maximus to view such criticism as a product of fickle Fortune.</summary>

# OF THE CONVERSATIONS.
<note>original: "SERMONVM"; referring to the *Sermones* or "Satires," a genre of poetry established by the Roman poet Horace consisting of moral reflections and social commentary written in a conversational tone.</note>

If ever anyone should charge me with deeds shameful to do,
I immediately call upon my mind, which, conscious of the truth
within itself, may claim for itself the fair role of a chosen judge
and openly declare at once what it has perceived.
A mind conscious of its own deeds is a certain judge <term>Iudex</term> for itself.
But if anyone should perhaps attack the jokes and serious matters I
weave together in verse: I appeal to holy posterity <term>posteritatem</term>,
which will burn with no envy and no vice of a malicious
mind; instead, weighing all things with an upright
judgment, it will never wish to have valued
those works at less than they are. But perhaps it will think
these same things are even greater, as Time <term>aetas</term> <note>original: "Aetas"; here personified as the progression of history or the "Age" to come.</note> carries itself forward into the long future;

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page asserts that a clear conscience is the only defense against personal slander, while the merit of one's creative work is best left to the impartial judgment of future generations. The author expresses hope that "Time" will eventually increase the value of his poetry once the envy of his peers has faded.</summary>

BOOK I.

# THE FIRST BOOK OF POEMS BY GIOVANNI AURELIO AUGURELLO, DEDICATED TO PANDOLFO MALATESTA, THEN A YOUTH OF TENDER AGE AND PRINCE OF RIMINI.

## ODE I.

<margin>1</margin>
*Illustrious offspring of ancient*
->*Leaders*<-
*Set to increase the Italian military*
->*glory*<-
*Of old, if from such excellent*
->*character*<-
A mind foreknowing the truth foretells from afar:
Pandolfo, for you we strive to tune this lyre as fitly as we are able;
though our skills be slight, it has long been adorned 
with the strings of the Aonian <term>Aoniis</term> <note>original: "Aoniis"; referring to Aonia, a region in Boeotia, Greece, home to Mount Helicon and sacred to the Muses.</note> Muses. 
Upon this lyre <term>Barbiton</term> <note>original: "Barbiton"; a multi-stringed instrument of the ancient Greek world, similar to a lyre but with a deeper tone.</note>, I shall play for the attentive ears 
of posterity those brave deeds you will soon perform.
But lest perhaps you should ask here in doubt:
What indeed can a slender Muse with her graceful sound 
add to your brave and deserving merits?
Know then how easily those things immediately 
raise themselves up, which have sprung from modest beginnings

Slowly they come forth through their own rhythms <note>The Latin "numeros" refers to poetic meters or musical measures.</note>. 
Therefore, for the future, we shall practice this [lyre] 
so that, drawn out from thin voices 
amongst the sounds of the ancients, it may already 
harmonize with deeds even now close at hand. 
Indeed, when by the divine power of the gods your 
excellent deeds grow, a higher spirit will also 
rise in us from our humble state, and swift, 
it will carry us through your praises. Meanwhile, 
whatever first-fruits this light Lyre <term>Cithara</term> <note>original: "Cithara"; an ancient Greek musical instrument in the lyre family, often used in the Renaissance to symbolize lyric poetry.</note>

might not be so great, but that you might 
receive it with a fair ear. Things once 
neglected, if there is time to look deeper within, 
are often of no small value later on;

### ODE II.

<margin>Virtue</margin>The gods have given nothing of greater good than virtue,

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of Ode II from Book I of Giovanni Aurelio Augurello’s lyric poems. On the previous page, the poet argued that human virtue remains hidden and uncelebrated unless it is given "brilliance" and fame through the works of poets. Here, he provides historical and mythological examples of how poetry immortalizes heroes and concludes with a personal dedication to Glycere.</meta>

-># BOOK I.<-

Revealed by a sharp hand <note>The "hand" of the poet or the stylus.</note>, let it <note>Referring to "virtue" from the previous page.</note> shine forth. It is through poetry that the fathers were able to see Aeneas as a local god <term>indigetem</term> <note>original: "indigetem"; a title given to a hero who has been deified and belongs to a specific land.</note>, and likewise saw Achilles, proud with the spoils he took from Hector.

But lest these heroes perish in the long passage of time, the Muse has brought them down even to us, their descendants, through perpetual song, so that they are as well known to us as they were to their own grandfathers.

To the Bards <term>Vatibus</term> <note>original: "Vatibus"; plural of "vates," a term for a divinely inspired poet-prophet.</note> belong both trust and grace; theirs is a sweet spirit of speech and a Love such as no one else could provide, even with a quiver full of lifeless arrows.

Orpheus visited the unholy realms of pale Orcus <note>A name for the Roman underworld.</note>, entering the company of the unlovely spirits and the horrifying Furies <term>Eumenides</term> <note>original: "Eumenides"; the "Kindly Ones," a euphemistic name for the goddesses of vengeance.</note>, who serve the terrible Tyrant <note>Pluto, the king of the underworld.</note>.

From that place, he nearly brought back his wife who had been snatched away by a bitter death, for he played with the powerful Muses of song <term>Camoenis</term> <note>original: "Camoenis"; the Roman deities of song and inspiration, later identified with the Greek Muses.</note> and all his Thracian skill.

Indeed, even when he himself was torn apart by a cruel slaughter across the dry fields, his voice still sought his Eurydice; with his final breath, he called out nothing but "wretched Eurydice."

And I, captivated by your charming manners and your virtue, shall sing of the modest Glycere upon this slender lyre, honoring her in both my first and my last songs.

<summary>Augurello argues that poetry is the sole force capable of preserving the fame of heroes like Aeneas and Achilles, and even overcoming death, as shown by the myth of Orpheus. He concludes by dedicating his own "slender" lyric poetry to the virtue of Glycere.</summary>
<keywords>Aeneas, Achilles, Orpheus, Eurydice, immortality of poetry, Bards (Vates), underworld, Glycere, virtue</keywords>

# ON THE DEATH OF ROBERTO MALATESTA,
# UNCONQUERED DUKE:
## *A consolation to the citizens of Rimini* <note>original: "Ariminenfes"; the people of Rimini, the city-state ruled by the Malatesta family.</note>

### ODE III.

*Will this song, O Pierian* <note>original: "Pieri"; a reference to the Muses, who were said to be born in Pieria.</note> *Muse, diminish the heavy tears*

<meta>This page continues the funeral ode for Roberto Malatesta. <header>BOOK I</header>

Alas, by how great a wave we wretches are overwhelmed
  before we can glimpse the calm harbor
near at hand.
  You promised us a return, and at first
you filled our doubting minds with good hope:
  but your words were uttered
to the airy winds. <note>original: "flatibus... Aëreis"; a poetic way of saying the promises were carried away or left unfulfilled by death.</note>
  For the Fates were insisting that a weighty
and illustrious deed be granted to you:
  so that men might know what powers of counsel
lay within your vigorous mind:
  what work of valor <term>virtutis</term> <note>original: "uirtutis"; in a Renaissance context, this implies both moral virtue and the "manly" military excellence of a leader.</note> you performed in arduous times:
and what strength also persisted
  in your heart—to which, alas, the now more violent
force of Fate arrived as a companion.
  So that these same men might scarcely look upon
this heap of such great riches from afar:
  once snatched from their eyes, they would continue
to seek it in vain, lamenting for a long time.
  For from that day you appeared terrifying
to your fierce enemies:
  their army—insolent from much slaughter
of men, trusting in the excellence of its leader,
  and at the same time relying on its own strength—
fell utterly prostrated
  before you in an instant, as if pierced
by the spears of the Gods:
  or by the right hand of Jove brandishing thunderbolts. <note>original: "Iouis fulmina"; Jove (Jupiter) was the king of the Roman gods, whose primary weapon was the lightning bolt.</note>

<summary>The poet laments that Malatesta’s promised return was cut short by Fate, contrasting his military brilliance—likened to the power of Jove—with the sudden void left by his death.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the funeral ode for Roberto Malatesta (1440–1482), the Lord of Rimini and a celebrated condottiero (mercenary captain). Following the previous page’s lament on his sudden death, this section reflects on the honors he received from the Pope and the shared grief of Italy.</meta>

<header>POEM I</header>

But while you go forward, having performed
    your honors: with those gifts which the Roman
Pontiff <note>original: "Pontifex Romanus"; referring to Pope Sixtus IV, who commissioned Malatesta to defend Rome against the Neapolitans.</note> had given to you,
    inflamed by your merits: alas, an all too fleeting
day has snatched you from us:
    from us, and from your fatherland, O good Duke:
the common glory of Italy:
    and the pillar of the weeping military <term>militiae</term> <note>original: "militiae"; refers to the profession of arms or the collective body of soldiers who served under him.</note>: but alas,
from us, alas, more than from all others,
    from us you are snatched away: we who were already
accustomed to live in the hope of your favor.
    These things I say to myself, and these things we citizens
too often complain of together in vain,
    our hearts troubled by empty cares.
But by no lamentations
    does the tenacious Parca <note>original: "Parca"; in Roman mythology, the Parcae were the three Fates. This refers to the one who cuts the thread of life, signifying that death is irreversible.</note> care to restore the threads
previously broken on her distaff.
    Therefore, shall we wretches be tormented
by perpetual grief forever? And will it not
    come to mind—lest we be ungrateful
and impious regarding our own benefits,
    having set aside the turn of another?
For just as the glory of that man’s name
    has filled the mindful lands:
so [Nature/God], leading him with a steady hand,
    has claimed him for the heavens above, where
he might match his rewards to his brave merits;
    and perpetually among...

Here he enjoys eternity with the Gods:
    Happy among the Sky-dwellers <note>original: "Cœlicolas"; a poetic term for the inhabitants of heaven or the gods.</note>, and a lofty,
    exceptional Hero among Commanders.
But yet he did not even then look out
    only for himself:
But looking back at us, as was fitting,
    he also wished to comfort his own:
He who gave to us such great offspring <note>Likely referring to Roberto's son and successor, Pandolfo IV Malatesta, who was meant to embody his father's virtues.</note>,
    so like himself, and who reflects him so well,
that by the sight of him, the grief driven
    deep into our innermost hearts
might finally end, broken: and more swiftly,
    by this likeness, find solace
so that our drenched Souls, filled
    with equal joy, might be restored:
As if no losses had been suffered before;

#### ODE VI.

Hey, boy! Why do you delay,
<margin>h</margin> <note>The letter 'h' in the margin likely serves as a reference mark for the reader or printer.</note> slow to bring the cups to me?
Not with those waters from the springs you see
tempering the parched fields—
the water that leaps from the steep mountain
far away,
and from there irrigates the Master’s
pleasing little garden on the ridge.
Hurry here, running with wines—
either Caecuban or Chian <note>Caecuban and Chian were among the most prestigious wines of the ancient world; Caecuban came from Latium in Italy, while Chian came from the Greek island of Chios.</note>;
or that wine which is produced from these
nearby hills by the favor of a god.
Come, let it serve here to quench
the sad cares of the heart;
care which, if it takes root too deep,
consumes us entirely
with its excessive fire.
O you who wash the virginal—

—body in the pure wave,
O Nymph, it is right to draw the sacred liquid

mixed for you with Bacchus <note>original: "libero"; another name for Bacchus, the god of wine. The poet is mixing wine with the cool spring water.</note>,
who brings no shame upon the Nymphs;

and washed the scorched god—
the one snatched from the womb of burning Semele
for the sake of Jove <note>original: "Ioui"; the king of the gods, Jupiter. In Greek and Roman myth, Semele was destroyed by Jupiter's divine fire, and the infant Bacchus was rescued from her womb and raised by nymphs.</note>.
It is right to wash away thirst in the meantime,
while the golden radiance of Phoebus <note>original: "Phœbi"; the sun god, Apollo.</note>

he who now rages fiercely
across the broad back of the Lion <note>original: "Leonis"; refers to the zodiac sign of Leo, indicating the "dog days" of mid-to-late summer when the sun is at its hottest.</note>.
Soon, refreshed by your cups,
and setting my hand to the lyre,
I will gladly share my songs
with a ready voice.
O, who then will pour forth
sounding words more powerfully than I?
Who will better strike the sweet strings
with a pleasing thumb?
I will sing how your little streams
run in a perpetual path,
wherever the Master of this place <note>The Bishop of Treviso, owner of the estate.</note>
has led them through learned rows <note>original: "doctos ordines"; a reference to the sophisticated and intellectual design of the garden’s irrigation or landscaping.</note>.
I will sing how you, clearer than glass,
flow over the beds spread here and there—

is more bitter than gall to me.
But with them as my companions:
may it be sweeter than honey to me.
If ever, under the favor of the Gods,
I might lead such a life by my own will;

# TO DAPHNIS.

## ODE VIII.

<margin>Q</margin>You, who in perpetual love or honor are
<margin>o</margin>second to none for me:
Daphnis, you who shall never at any time depart
from my heart because of your merits:
<margin>A</margin>What I should reflect upon regarding hope, or in what way I should strive
to lead what remains of my life:
<margin>N</margin>Lest it happen to me to say at the end, “We become wise
<margin>N</margin>too late within the Trojan horse.” <note>original: "Troio Sero in equo sapimus." A proverb meaning it is useless to become wise after the disaster has already occurred, referring to the Trojans realizing the trap of the wooden horse too late.</note>
Nor let anything new happen which I have not been able
to ponder in my mind beforehand.
<margin>S</margin>But may I be strong enough, if anything should come beyond expectation,
<margin>S</margin>to count it all as profit.
And so, may the mercy of the gods favor my undertakings
and my plans,
<margin>V</margin>So that for me the track is directed through the right path,
however I may be carried along.
<margin>E</margin>There is a twofold kind of men, and a double path among them,
and a fork in the road. <note>This refers to the Pythagorean "Y," a traditional symbol of the choice between the path of virtue and the path of vice.</note>
<margin>A</margin>And indeed, each path maintains itself with worthy praise
and its own protection.
<margin>S</margin>There are those who, born ever-watchful for transacting business,
hold the one path.
<margin>Q</margin>Who strive to bring the help of safety to their fatherland,
their kin, and their friends.

<note>The letters in the margin—Q, O, A, N, N, S, S, V, E, A, S, Q—are the start of an acrostic signature used by the author, Joannes Secundus.</note>

<summary>Janus Secundus addresses his friend Daphnis, reflecting on the need for foresight in life to avoid regret. He introduces the philosophical concept of the "two paths" of life, starting with the active life of civic duty and service.</summary>
<keywords>Daphnis, Janus Secundus, acrostic, Trojan Horse, two paths of life, civic duty, Neo-Latin poetry, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues Ode VIII to Daphnis. The previous page established the two paths of life: the active life of service and the contemplative life. Here, the poet Janus Secundus describes his own choice to follow the "pleasant places" of the Muses and recounts a specific moment spent on Daphnis's estate.</meta>

<header>POEMS.</header>

A great work, and memorable to all: such as you also
*Often handle with a kind hand:*
And you outrun in fame those whom
*It is your care to follow.* <note>The poet suggests that while Daphnis looks to great men of the past as role models, his own achievements are already beginning to surpass them.</note>
There are others, for whose talents another path
*Is worn in wonderful ways:*
To whom the noise of the City and the crowds of the Forum <note>The Forum was the heart of Roman public, legal, and political life; the poet uses it here to represent the "active" or "busy" life of a citizen.</note>
*Are certainly less pleasing to the mind.*
And therefore, the retreats of deep groves and rivers
*They always love to seek.*
Now, having found these, they enjoy sweet rest
*And well-arranged comforts.*
Nor are they so sluggish that they themselves
*Find nothing for mortals to use.*
But in their hidden breast, a skill for serious things
*Thrives with great strength.*
They who seek a lasting work for posterity
*Through their writings with much effort.*
Whom I follow, as far as the strengths of my talent allow,
*Through these pleasant places.*
And now I strive to lead my right foot through the highest hills
*Into the heights.*
Now it pleases me to wander with the light Muses <note>The "light Muses" (levibus musis) usually refers to the goddesses of lyric or elegiac poetry, which dealt with personal feelings and nature, rather than the "heavy" Muses of epic war poetry.</note> in a secret valley
*Through the joyful fields.*
Just as indeed when I was lately walking in your fields,
*As I happened to be nearby,*
I saw with these eyes the source of that hurrying water <note>original: "aquai." Secundus uses an archaic Latin form for "water" here to give the verse a classical, sophisticated flavor.</note>
*Gently before its head.*

From which <meta>catchword: Equibus</meta>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Ode VIII, where the speaker is led by the Muses to a sacred spring. <header>BOOK I.</header>
<sig>i</sig>

After one of them invited me to quench my sharp thirst 
*at the sacred fountain,* <note>original: "sacro... fonte." This likely refers to Hippocrene or Castalia, the legendary springs of the Muses that inspired poetic and spiritual insight.</note>
"From here," she said, "will come all the hope of the life you desire, 
*and of your peace.* 
This healthful water brings with it various draughts 
*and many medicines.* 
This water alone is able to heal all the sad 
*anxieties of the mind.*" 
She had spoken: and soon, bowing with bent knees, 
*I worshiped the water, lying face down.* 
And greedily, I had barely touched the liquid with the edge of my lips, 
*when suddenly there grew strong* 
A greater will to live far away from cares, 
*and a more certain one for me,* 
Which may the gods make my own: lest by chance I slip back 
*any further, as I am wont to do,* 
Into the waves of another life: and be tossed by the storms 
*of a more dreadful water;* <note>The "dreadful water" represents the chaotic, stormy nature of public life or worldly ambitions, contrasted with the healing, still waters of the Muses' spring.</note>

## TO JULIUS ANTONIUS CHEREGONIUS ON HIS HARD LOVE FOR ALDULA.

## ODE IX.

In the countryside, at home, in the forum, in the city, the soldier, 
*full of years, rests with his weapons laid aside:* 
The aged oxen refuse the plows: <note>The poet uses the imagery of retired soldiers and old oxen to argue that everything in nature eventually seeks rest—except, perhaps, a lover.</note>

<summary>The poet describes a transformative experience drinking from the Muses' fountain, which grants him the resolve to seek a life of quiet contemplation. The page then begins a new poem addressed to Julius Antonius regarding his difficult romantic pursuit of a woman named Aldula.</summary>
<keywords>sacred fountain, spiritual healing, poetic inspiration, retirement, Julius Antonius Cheregonius, Aldula, unrequited love</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues Ode IX, addressed to Julius Antonius Cheregonius. While the previous page established that all things in nature—soldiers, oxen, and even the poets themselves—eventually seek rest and retirement, this page introduces the "exception to the rule": the lover, whose internal turmoil persists even into old age.</meta>

<header>SONGS</header>

A decaying boat <note>original: "Phaselus." This refers to a light, fast ship, a common metaphor in Roman poetry (famously used by Catullus) for the journey of life or a poet's career.</note> grows old in thin seaweed, 
resting likewise in secluded quiet. 
But what old age will release me from Love? 
Not even if the days of Nestor <note>Nestor was the legendary King of Pylos who lived for three generations; he represents the extreme limit of human longevity.</note> 
or of Tithonus <note>Tithonus was a mortal granted eternal life by the gods, but because he was not granted eternal youth, he aged perpetually until he became a cicada.</note> are given to me, will I ever, wretched as I am, 
find a limit to such great labors. 
Alas, it would have been better to serve a harsh Tyrant, 
and to groan, shut within your bull, Perillus, <note>Perillus was the inventor of the "Brazen Bull," a hollow statue used for execution by fire; he was famously the first person the tyrant Phalaris forced inside it.</note> or to harden 
at the Gorgon’s <note>The Gorgon Medusa, whose gaze turned men to stone.</note> face, or to feed the ravenous 
bird of the Caucasus <note>A reference to the Titan Prometheus, who was chained to Mount Caucasus and had his liver perpetually eaten by an eagle as punishment for giving fire to humanity.</note> with my frequent breast: 
Than to bear the rule of a wicked girl. 
For although you may walk alone and happy, 
and hold your pride in the fullness of Love, 
nevertheless, no girl remains stable <note>original: "Pondus... habet," literally "has weight" or "constancy."</note> for long; 
she is fickle-willed, and malicious, and bold. 
Nor are the waves so moved by a breeze, 
as various storms weigh upon her mind. 
Yet I will resist: and I will strive to make her, who is flighty and proud, 
steadfast and kind in time, 
through prayer, and gifts, and threats. 
In this way, stones are hollowed by soft water; 
in this way, swords are worn away by rust. 
But if such strong and powerful persistence <note>original: "Acumen," meaning sharpness of mind or concentrated effort.</note> 
does not succeed: nevertheless, 
no old age will release me from this headstrong Love.

<summary>The poet laments that while all other things find rest in old age, the pain of love is an inescapable labor more grueling than legendary mythological tortures. He vows to persist in wooing his fickle mistress, hoping that like water carving stone, his efforts will eventually soften her heart.</summary>

<keywords>Nestor, Tithonus, Perillus, Prometheus, Medusa, constancy, fickle love, persistence, metaphors of aging</keywords>

*Joyful and wise, as if looking toward the future*
*harvest, I shall enter upon this journey for you, companions,*

      *flourishes green.*
*And I will prepare him with care and every effort*
*as a cultivator for the fertile crop,*
*one whose Athenian Muses <note>original: "Actaeae... Camoenae." Actaean refers to Attica, the region of Athens, signifying refined or sophisticated Greek-style poetry. The Camoenae were the native Italian deities of song, identified with the Greek Muses.</note> are carried*
      *by a fluent tongue.*
*Perhaps I myself may yet bring forth something*
*from this worn-out soil, unless an empty weight*
*around my breast and cold blood deep within <note>Ancient medical and poetic theory held that the blood of the elderly grew cold, which was thought to stifle the "fire" of poetic inspiration and creativity.</note>*
      *stand in the way.*
*Or at least I, a guide for another through the course*
*of the proud dust, will be present for those*
*released from the starting gates <note>original: "Carcere." In a Roman circus or racetrack, the carceres were the stalls where chariots waited for the signal to begin the race.</note> at the very*
      *finish of their labors.*
*I shall perform the role of a whetstone for the hard sword: <note>This is a direct reference to Horace’s *Ars Poetica* (304–305). The poet claims that while he may no longer "cut" (write) effectively, he can "sharpen" (instruct) the younger generation.</note>*
*which, though incapable of cutting, is able to make*
*the blade sharp; and I shall greatly rejoice*
      *in the fulfillment of a duty well-done.*
*O happy day: when it may be permitted for me,*
*having spoken to you in such terms, to say:*

### ODE XI.

Lest perhaps, Glycera <note>The name Glycera is derived from the Greek word for "sweet." It was a common name for a mistress or beloved in classical Latin lyric poetry.</note>, I should seem the lowliest

&nbsp;&nbsp;Gift from a devoted little friend:

Having slipped with a gliding foot,
&nbsp;&nbsp;He clothed my tender stalks as I clung to him.

# TO P. GLYCERA <note>A Greek-derived name meaning "Sweet." In Latin lyric poetry, Glycera is a frequent name for a beloved woman.</note>

## ODE XII.

We are spending the ninth summer of our life <note>original: "Estatem nonam agimus uita." This likely refers to the ninth year of their relationship or a significant period of their adult lives together.</note>
<margin>a</margin> Since, by your favor <note>original: "mercede tua." Can also mean reward or kindness.</note>, it has come to pass:
That I did not fall, wounded by the fiery
Blow, with which harsh Love <note>original: "Amor." Referring to Cupid/Eros, often depicted as a cruel deity who "brands" or "wounds" lovers.</note>, present in my breast,
Had branded me with destruction;
And though I was relieved, I was still weighed down
By this perpetual wound: which I cherish
More dearly than my own heart.
Without which I would not wish to lead my life:
Not even if I were gifted the empire of the world
Would I be able to be without this pleasing fire:
Which burns you with no less sharp a heat
Than the flames it stirs within my bones.
And it is not your beauty alone that moves me,
Nor do I only admire the various
Arts of Pallas <note>original: "Palladis artes." Pallas Athena was the goddess of wisdom and crafts; her "arts" usually refer to spinning, weaving, and embroidery.</note> which you handle so well,
Nor does the honey-sweet charm <term>Lepos<gloss>Refers to a refined, polished wit or grace in speech.</gloss></term> of your words,
Added to your constant and natural modesty,
Touch my mind quite as much as:
Your incorruptible and inviolable
Faith <note>original: "fides." A central Roman virtue meaning loyalty, constancy, and trustworthiness.</note>, beyond any artifice: and the way you
In turn watch over me with mutual devotion,
Just as I pursue you with a matching spirit.
Such you remain, my Glycera, always...

<note>Glycere (Sweet), Ode (Song), Arts of Pallas (weaving/intellect), Lepos (wit), modesty, faith/loyalty, Love (Cupid).</note>

You will remain close to me: you, and those ways of yours, and the exceptional glory of your virtues, and these features of your face—a model of divine beauty—and whatever you have done or ever spoken well, I shall not deprive of their well-earned praise. To the best of my ability <note>original: "pro uirili parte." Literally "for a man's part," a common idiom meaning to do one's utmost.</note>, I will store them away for the memory of a grateful posterity.

## CONSOLATION TO GIOVANNI ALESSANDRO CAPELLA <note>Giovanni Alessandro Capella was likely a contemporary humanist or associate of the author. Consolatory poems were a popular Renaissance genre meant to soothe grief through philosophical or pastoral imagery.</note>
## ON THE DEATH OF HIS
## MOST ILLUSTRIOUS FATHER. <note>original: "Clariss. V." for "Clarissimi Viri," a title of high social or academic standing.</note>

### ODE XIII.

Why—since we have barely climbed these steep hills—do we not recline for a little while in the green grass under this leafy canopy of the woods, Iulus <note>A poetic name, possibly used here as a nickname for Capella or a companion in the poem's dialogue.</note>, not by mere chance?

Around which the summer breeze, striking the leaves, murmurs back and carries various harmonies of birds to our ears—beside which you might despise the melodies of any mortal man.

Or if it pleases us more to enter this cave, the silence of whose entrance I already revere; or perhaps that pure spring with its splashing waters calls us to it first.

<vocab>Alexandrum Capellam (Giovanni Alessandro Capella), Consolatio (Consolation), Ode (Ode), posteritatem (posterity), uirtutum (of virtues)</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the concluding lines of a poem praising a woman's enduring virtues for future generations, followed by the opening of a consolatory ode. The new poem invites the grieving recipient to find peace in a lush, pastoral landscape filled with birdsong and quiet caves.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Alessandro Capella, consolation, pastoral poetry, virtue, immortality, nature imagery, mourning</keywords>

### POEMS, BOOK I.

<meta>The catchword "Illhinc" at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the next page.</meta>

Here, see, I bring those songs which I have long desired to set to the lyre <note>original: "fidibus." Literally "strings," referring to the lyre, the traditional instrument for lyric poetry.</note> for you; songs which—unless my mind now deceives me—might lighten your weary anxiety.

This anxiety, holding you fast in longing for your most excellent father, never allows you to rest, as it remains perpetually poured through your innermost being <note>original: "artus usque per intimos." Literally "throughout your deepest limbs."</note>.

And who would not constantly and heavily bear the loss of such a person <note>original: "iacturam capitis." In Latin, "caput" (head) is often used as a metonymy for the whole person, particularly someone of great importance or a beloved soul.</note>, who was so outstanding to all, and who might have restored the solid glory of the virtues?

Who among mortals would not grieve this hidden light of the Divine? A light which, to all who beheld it, gave evidence that it carried within itself some likeness of the gods.

But however the harsh Fates <note>The Parcae or Fates of Roman mythology, who spun and cut the threads of human life.</note> have turned these heavy changes for us—which you cannot alter with any amount of tears—there is nonetheless some way of consoling the wretched.

Even you, whom this singular grief concerns: you, whose innermost heart a fresh care always justly tears apart, and never for a moment leaves.

Therefore, with your two eyes <note>original: "gemina acie." Literally "with double sight," referring to the eyes.</note>, behold now this thin Aether <note>The "Aether" was considered the pure, upper atmosphere where the stars and gods resided, and where the souls of the virtuous were thought to return.</note> which rolls around us, and from here look up, as much as you are able, to the eternal home of those above.

<summary>This page contains the middle stanzas of a consolatory poem, moving from an acknowledgement of the friend's deep grief for his father to a philosophical exhortation to look toward the heavens for comfort.</summary>
<keywords>consolation, Giovanni Alessandro Capella, grief, fatherhood, virtue, divinity, Aether, Fates</keywords>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

From that place <note>original: "Illhinc." Referring to the divine realm or the origin of the soul.</note>, as those tell us who reach closer to the Gods with their souls, you may know that a small, ethereal spark of a starry mind <note>original: "Aetheream... Mentis sydereae particulam." This reflects the ancient belief that the human soul is a "spark" or "fragment" of the divine celestial fire.</note> is granted
->To those who have forgotten bodily decay.<-
As long as this spark remains at least somewhat free from human chains, if one can keep it whole and free from shameful acts, to this man at last a path opens easily
->For a return to heaven,<-
Where, in his accustomed place, he may spend an age forever with the beings above, where the Milky Way <note>original: "lacteus... circulus." In classical thought, the Milky Way was often described as the path or dwelling place of the souls of the virtuous.</note> shines through the starry orbit
->In the world's path.<-
Here a dwelling is likewise decreed for the pious without end, but they especially inhabit it—those who, for their country <note>original: "pro patria." Reflecting the Roman ideal that service to the state is a path to divinity.</note>, with the sharpness of their minds,
->Have diligently performed any great deeds.<-
Therefore, as soon as your father, now more blessed, went forth shining from that decaying mass <note>original: "E putri... mole." A reference to the mortal body, seen as a heavy or crumbling burden compared to the light of the soul.</note> and reached this place, then those who because of such merits had previously
->Set foot therein,<-
All strove, each for himself, to applaud for the one returned to the dwellers of heaven, and they restore him to the center of the sacred council, where
->He may drink of perpetual nectar,<-
Which only those above are permitted to taste, or those who made themselves so like the gods while on earth that they were able to utterly
->Cast off the heavy yoke.<- <note>original: "detrectare iugum." This "yoke" refers to the weight of earthly passions or the physical constraints of mortality.</note>

<header>POEMS</header>

Happy souls, to whom it was granted to look up
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;more deeply than the common lot of men, so that
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;they believed they were born not for themselves
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;alone, but for their Country and for all people.
Among these souls, that man <note>Giovanni Alessandro Capella, whose death is the subject of the poem.</note> had stood out
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;more kindly than all others while he was closer
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to us; now, more than all the stars, he shines
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;as a conspicuous constellation.
These are the things, Julius <note>original: "Iule." Referring to Giovanni Giulio Capella, the son of the deceased.</note>, which I was able to
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;summarize briefly from many; but since many more
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;remain, and the declining day has just now
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;warned us to rise,
you—for you are able—should join what follows
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;to these things with serious study; thus you may
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;consider nothing more holy to be done by you,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and thus you are most like your father.

To you, we, the eager Chorus of the Muses <note>original: "Pieridum Chorus." The Pierides are another name for the Muses, the goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences.</note>...

# TO NICCOLO LEONICO TOMEO <note>Niccolò Leonico Tomeo (1456–1531) was a celebrated Venetian scholar and professor at the University of Padua, known for his translations of Aristotle.</note> REGARDING
# FAUSTO ANDRELINI <note>Publio Fausto Andrelini (c. 1462–1518) was an Italian humanist poet who became a leading figure at the University of Paris.</note> AND ANDREA BRENTA <note>Andrea Brenta (c. 1454–1484) was a scholar from Padua known for his translations of Greek medical and philosophical texts.</note>

# HIS HOMELAND, THE LATTER IN HIS
# HOMELAND, BUT BY THE VIOLENT
# BLOW OF AN ENEMY.

## ODE 15.

To what end do you recall me, learned Leonico,
<margin>¶</margin>as I am barely able to speak for my tears?
Is it so that with the lyre, upon which I was 
accustomed to play of the sports of young girls,
I might now—alas—tune a mournful Dirge <note>original: "Nænia." In ancient Rome, this was a funeral song performed in praise of the deceased.</note>
for the harsh deaths of my companions?
Once, singing in lament,
Sappho compelled her companions to lay down
the hair from their tender heads <note>A traditional Greek gesture of mourning.</note>;
while she grieved that the too-swift, dark hands
of Libitina <note>The ancient Roman goddess of funerals and the dead.</note> had been laid upon them...

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...subject to the wretched accidents of Fortune?
Who, hearing that you, Brenta <note>Andrea Brenta (c. 1454–1484) was a prominent humanist scholar and translator of Greek medical texts.</note>, a soul far

fly from mortals! You who wish to live
happily, cease to put too much trust

# TO PRINCE PANDOLFO
# MALATESTA.

## ODE 16.

An image reflecting your great ancestors,
<margin>O</margin> noble one, by the beauty of your own character <term>indolis: innate talent or natural disposition</term>,
a hope surviving even your father’s virtue,
and a rising glory for your kin,
Pandolfo <note>Pandolfo IV Malatesta (1475–1534), known as "Pandolfaccio," was the last Malatesta ruler of Rimini before it was incorporated into the Papal States.</note>, see—whatever my lyre <term>Barbitos: an ancient Greek stringed instrument, similar to a lyre, used by lyric poets like Sappho and Alcaeus.</term>
strives for with its resonant strings,
it offers itself to you; it has no greater wish
than that it might occasionally play
something to soothe your ear,
and which might be able to lighten dark cares,
should any exist.

<header>POEMS:</header>

If, however, this work can be deemed worthy 
of such great honor, by the grace of the King of the Gods <note>original: "Superum... rege," referring to Jupiter, the ruler of the heavens.</note>, 
so that it may be equally helpful and beneficial. 
Toward you, no other gratitude is offered, 
except that which once a faithful hand 
offered to a powerful Persian Master <note>This refers to a famous anecdote about King Artaxerxes of Persia, who graciously accepted a gift of water from a poor subject named Sinas when the man had nothing else to give. It is a common literary trope used to justify modest literary dedications to powerful patrons.</note>: 
Which, when it could do nothing else, 
reverently offered him what was at hand: 
water drawn from a nearby small spring <term>Fonticulo: a diminutive form of 'fons' (spring), emphasizing the modesty of the gift</term>. 
The King measured the gift <term>Munus: a gift, duty, or offering</term> 
not by its lowliness, but by the intent of the heart 
and mind; he received it just the same, 
returning his goodwill to the one who gave it. 
Relying on this principle, farmers 
worship the Great Gods with little garlands <term>Corollis: small wreaths or crowns of flowers</term>, 
or with apples and cheap sacrificial cakes <note>original: "libis," cakes often made of flour and honey used in religious offerings.</note>. 
Nor is it right for a poor man to give what would 
be a burden if he gave more; the gods themselves 
take account of a man’s means, nor is any 
offering more pleasing to the deities than goodwill <note>original: "uoluntas." The poet argues that the spirit of the giver is more important than the material value of the gift.</note>. 
I bring this goodwill to you, long since 
dedicated in my songs <term>carminibus: poems or songs</term>, though I wonder 
if these are consistent with the dignity I wish for them. 
I wait in doubt to see how those of learned 
mouths, who cultivate a fluid Minerva <note>Minerva was the goddess of wisdom and the arts. "Cultivating a fluid Minerva" refers to those with natural literary talent or refined critical judgment.</note>, 
might weigh them in their judgment;

<meta>catchword: TO</meta>

<summary>In this continuation of Ode 16, the poet uses the classical anecdote of King Artaxerxes and the peasant's water to argue that the humble nature of his poems is offset by his sincere devotion to Prince Pandolfo. He emphasizes that the gods and great rulers alike value the spirit of the giver over the price of the gift.</summary>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

# TO VENUS.

## ODE XVII.

What business do I have with you, Venus? <note>original: "Quid mihi tecum Venus esse negotI?" A classic rhetorical opening asking why the goddess of love is bothering the poet at his age.</note>
<margin>¶</margin> Why do you test my liver <term>iecur: In ancient and Renaissance physiology, the liver—rather than the heart—was often seen as the seat of passion and desire</term> again with new fire, 
when my age has already hastened to close 
->its seventh Lustrum?<- <term>Lustrum: a period of five years. The poet is stating he is thirty-five years old (7 x 5), a time when he feels he should be past the volatility of new romance.</term>

Stop, I beg you; Glycere herself is enough, 
the one woman for whom my passion burns me. 
And for me, there is nothing left that another 
->fire might seize.<-

That passion rages in my very marrow, 
hoping neither to be diminished by the force 
of any wave washing over it, nor to 
->increase by any other flame.<-

May you only make this gift firm and my own, 
the gift with which you, Goddess, have already enriched me 
quite enough and more; there is no reason 
->to add anything further.<-

Rather, bound to you <note>Addressing the beloved or perhaps the singular bond of love</note> by a single knot, 
let me cling much more tightly and always closer 
than if I were pulled apart into 
->various different interests.<-

<sig>m iiii</sig>

<summary>In this ode, the poet addresses Venus, the goddess of love, arguing that at thirty-five years old, he is content with his current love for Glycere and desires no new passions. He uses the metaphor of fire and knots to describe a singular, stable devotion rather than the flickering whims of youth.</summary>
<keywords>Venus, Glycere, Lustrum, middle age, devotion, classical metaphor, liver as seat of passion</keywords>

<header>ODES.</header>

# TO GLYCERE.

### ODE XVIII.

<margin>S</margin> For a long time, Glycere, my heart 
sat deep within my breast, 
released from all cares; 
it led a quiet life, distributed 
through my bodily limbs.

<margin>D</margin> Until that lovely, eternal grace 
of your heavenly face—sent from the gods above—
made its way in through my burning eyes <note>original: "ardentis ocellos"</note> 
with its powerful commands.

At once, driven by this, my 
heart leaves me; stripped from its seat, 
it flies forth and soon hides itself 
deep within the lap <note>original: "gremium"; here referring to the inner sanctuary or bosom</note> of your blessed breast.

<margin>I</margin> And there it dwells with you, so powerful 
that it has gradually driven your own 
sweet heart from its nourishing temple <term>Almo... templo: The poet describes the beloved's breast as a "nourishing temple," a sacred space for the soul.</term>, 
where my spirit now resides as a presiding priest.

<margin>A</margin> But as soon as your heart, now an exile 
from its home, senses my own 
empty breast, it hastens its entry 
with a lovely flight into such 
modest and vacant nests <note>The poet uses the metaphor of "nests" (original: "nidis") to describe the chambers of the heart, suggesting a bird returning home.</note>.

<margin>H</margin> This is the source of our mutual Love; 
from this comes that single feeling 
by which we cling to one another, Glycere, 
and live—each of us drawing breath 
from the other’s soul <note>original: "sub Aura"; literally "under the breeze" or "breath," signifying that the lovers now share a single life-force.</note>.

<meta>catchword: TO</meta>

<summary>This poem explores the Renaissance literary conceit of the "exchange of hearts," where the poet's soul leaves his body to reside in his beloved, while hers moves to inhabit his. This reciprocal movement creates a spiritual union where the lovers are no longer two separate beings, but live through one another's breath.</summary>
<keywords>exchange of hearts, mutual love, Glycere, spiritual union, Neo-Latin poetry, Maffeo Barberini</keywords>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

# TO SEPTIMIUS AND FUSCIUS 
# THOMEUS, ON THE HIGH VALUE 
# OF PETRARCA’S POETRY.

## ODE XVIII.

Septimius and Fuscius, poets renowned 
for your mastery of both ancient and modern 
tongues <note>original: "Impari lingua"; literally "unequal tongues," referring to the poet's skill in both the prestigious Latin of the ancients and the "modern" Italian vernacular.</note>, and to me, dear companions 
->joined to my heart,<-

I strive to know whether the smooth, 
finely-spun, and charming song 
of the Lydian bard <term>Lydii vatis: "The Lydian bard" is a reference to Francesco Petrarca (Petrarch). The term "Lydian" refers to a musical mode associated with sweetness and elegance, fitting for Petrarch's lyric style.</term> singing of his lady 
Laura, might move you for a moment.
->I long to know.<-

As for me, whenever that polished 
volume is taken up in my hands, 
it holds me fast with its eternal 
and ever-fresh artistry, 
->as often as I read it,<-

whether it lingers upon the powerful 
affections of the soul, or through some 
wondrous beauty, or whether it fills me, 
held captive, with the lady's 
->blessed character.<-

Indeed, he seems to have desired 
to be placed among the foremost 
of poets, so much so that I would deny 
he is truly second to any—
->or at least to very few.<-

<summary>In this ode, Marcantonio Flaminio addresses his friends Septimius and Fuscius, praising the enduring beauty and emotional depth of Petrarch’s poetry. He argues that Petrarch deserves a place among the greatest poets of history, regardless of the language used.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarch, Laura, Septimius, Fuscius Thomeus, literary criticism, Renaissance poetry, Lydian mode, vernacular vs Latin</keywords>

# TO FUSCIUS THOMEUS, ON HOW HAPPY ARE THEY WHO,
->*abandoning all other cares, devote themselves to the Muses.*<-

->ODE XX.<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Thrice happy and more is he,*
<margin>o</margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Whom a mind ignorant of cares can*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*weave into the light dances of the Muses <term>Pieridum: The Pierides, a traditional name for the Muses, the nine goddesses of artistic and intellectual inspiration.</term>.*
*He does not commit himself, laden with much merchandise, to the deep*
*sea, nor does he cast himself into the midst*
*of slaughter, advancing against*
*fierce and savage enemies.*
*And long since indifferent to the breeze of popular favor <note>original: "populari Auræ"; a common Latin idiom for the fickle nature of public opinion or fame.</note>,*
*nor gaping after the wealth of kings, the Muse*
*receives him into her modest, secret cave.*
*How it delights him to touch with a firm foot*
*places accessible to few, and to hear*
*unusual voices, and to behold the faces*
*and countenances of the gods from nearby,*
*and to see the poet <term>vatem: A term meaning both "poet" and "prophet," suggesting the writer is divinely inspired.</term> now arise and grow,*
*destined to survive with ample praise*
*even unto his well-remembering descendants.*
*Just as we recently saw you, Fuscius, released*
*from every anxiety,*
*by the secluded waters of the river Permessus <term>Permessi: A river in Greece sacred to the Muses; in poetry, "drinking" from or "walking near" it represents achieving high poetic status.</term>,*
*entwined in the dances of the Muses.*
*While I, thither wounded and barely steady <note>The poem cuts off here as the narrator begins to contrast his own troubled state with his friend's tranquility.</note>...*

<summary>In this ode, Flaminio celebrates the poetic life as a sanctuary from the stresses of war, commerce, and politics. He praises his friend Fuscius Thomeus for achieving this state of divine, creative peace.</summary>

**H**aving departed hence while scarcely out of my boyish years,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I came here at my father's command,
**W**here I first chose for myself dear companions,

**T**hen, from that first moment, I grew accustomed to
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;cultivating my mind in the ways of the ancients <note>original: "ueterum... modis"; refers to the study of classical Greek and Roman literature and philosophy, a hallmark of Renaissance education.</note>.
**D**o I seem to have undertaken little: my studies, and Love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and fellowship?
**I**f anyone should ever take even one of these from me,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;he would have taken away both my eyes.
**H**ow great a thing it is to lay aside unpleasant cares from the soul
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;with sweet conversations,
**E**ither when we meet with wise companions, or when
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;we are received with the gentle
**C**harms of a pleasing mistress, compared to which
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;you would think all other things are lesser to us.
**I**t is also a great thing to have traveled some distance,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and not to have feared the high path <note>A metaphor for the pursuit of higher knowledge, virtue, or poetic fame that sets the scholar apart from the unlearned.</note>,
**W**here you might climb so high that you look down on the common crowd <note>original: "uulgus"; the uneducated or "vulgar" masses who do not pursue the arts.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;crawling vainly below.
**T**hese are the things which, far from my fatherland <note>Verona, the home of both the poet and the recipient, Marcantonio Pastio.</note>—though it
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;be most sweet—are able to hold me back;

<summary>The poet lists the three primary reasons he remains in Padua: the bonds of deep friendship, the presence of his beloved mistress, and his devotion to classical studies. He argues that these intellectual and emotional rewards justify his continued absence from his "sweet" fatherland of Verona.</summary>

<keywords>Padua, friendship, humanism, classical education, romantic love, Verona, Marcantonio Pastio, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

### ODE . XXIII .

You will lead a happier <note>original: "Aetius," likely a spelling variant or error for "Laetius" (happier).</note> life, my friend
L. Campus, if you are held by no snares <note>original: "uinclis"; literally "chains" or "fetters," a common Renaissance metaphor for the entrapment of romantic passion.</note>,
Which Cupid <note>The Roman god of desire, often portrayed as a hunter laying traps for the unsuspecting.</note> is accustomed to stretch out
For unwary souls.
Nothing is sweeter than a mind free from cares,
And nothing, by contrast, should be thought more heavy,
Than to be held by a mind repeatedly shrouded
Beneath a wandering veil.
If, however, this is dear to your heart, and if
This one desire has settled deep within you—
To surrender your free head, and to be sent
Under tight shackles—
At least provide for yourself such a Mistress
Who may be carried with you under an equal yoke <note>original: "iugo... parili"; a classical metaphor for a balanced partnership, suggesting a relationship of mutual respect rather than one-sided obsession.</note>,
To whom neither servitude nor command
Could ever be a burden.

<header>SONG I. III.</header>

O Glycere <note>A name derived from the Greek word for "sweet."</note>, the illustrious glory of maidens,
<margin>d</margin> At long last, set a limit to your tears,
And cease to cast a dark, damp cloud
From your eyes over your bright face.

Ah, although the lamented Catulus Orius <note>original: "Catulus Orius." While "Catulus" can mean a puppy or small animal, in this poetic context it likely refers to a deceased youth or a beloved pet personified. "Orius" is a proper name.</note>
Departed for the gods above on an unfair day,
Will you continue to stifle the breath of your peaceful breast
With such harsh sorrows?

He, now added to the dwellings of the pious, does not wish for this;
Neither does your own virtue, nor your chastity—
Which shines from your beautiful body
Like a gem set in golden necklaces.

Nor do the charms of Elysium <note>The paradise of the afterlife in classical mythology.</note> desire this.
Charms which anxious Penelope <note>The wife of Odysseus, famed in Greek mythology for her faithfulness and chastity.</note> offers to him 
In her chaste lap upon the tender grass, 
While he flees from dreadful Pasiphaë <note>The Queen of Crete, who in mythology represented unnatural and uncontrolled passion; here she likely represents the opposite of the "chaste" afterlife.</note>.

From there, hidden within a purple flower,
He playfully mocks you—you, who have become a Laura <note>A reference to Laura de Noves, the muse of the poet Petrarch, implying Glycere is now a legendary figure of beauty and inspiration.</note> 
Celebrated for all eternity 
By the recent songs of the beloved Poet.

From here he seeks the Aeolian Sappho <note>The famous Greek lyric poetess from the island of Lesbos (Aeolia).</note>, from there the lyric 
Muse, while the clever little charmer 
Seeks one most like his Mistress; 
Now he leaps playfully into the silent lap 
Of the Goddess of the Underworld <note>Proserpina (or Persephone), the queen of the dead.</note>.

## ODE 25.

> <margin>m</margin>*In the beginning, when God*

<summary>This page presents an ode arguing that marriage was divinely established to end the violent chaos and incestuous confusion of primitive humanity. <header>POEMS, BOOK 1.</header>

**D**isplaying her eyes, in which
   Modesty shone forth, mingled with piety.
**H**ere, dedicating a pleasing wife to the Youth
   in sacred and stable marriage,
**P**rimitive man first revealed to the ignorant
   the piety of the son, and the devotion
**A**nd fervor of parents; by what easy law
   wives should honor their husbands; and what more closely
**V**nites and holds together cities and peoples,
   preserving both the kingdoms of princes
**S**overeign, and the homes of the poor.
   Therefore, give yourselves entirely, young men,
**T**o such bright and pious customs.
   And produce children similar to your fathers.
**V**ou too, happy maidens, having
   embraced the duties of great Hymen, <note>original: "Hymênis." The Greek and Roman god of marriage ceremonies, often depicted holding a torch.</note>
**R**estore the grandfathers of old,
**F**elicitous in offspring, and return them to your husbands.

## TO PANDOLFO 
## MALATESTA.

### ODE. XXVII.

If by chance some farmer of a tiny field <note>original: "Agelli." A diminutive form of field, used here to emphasize the humble, modest status of the giver.</note>
<margin>5</margin>Should give you the flower of a rose, Pandolfo, or waxen plums,
Or apples which he has plucked from a hidden tree,
Surely you would not despise these, but receive them
With an open face, and think to yourself: "Love
These spirits, which are no less for their lack of elaborate care."
Driven by this hope, therefore, the garlands which we have gathered
With a slender fingernail in the deep valleys
Of Aonia <note>A region in Boeotia, Greece, sacred to the Muses. The poet is saying he has gathered his "flowers" (poems) from the home of the Muses.</note> we now send—as small things from small people,
And as those crawling across the open ground. But if you deign
To bind your head with these modest wreaths, and if at any
Time you breathe upon me with a favorable divinity as I climb the hill,
Perhaps I myself, returning from the sacred summit, will be the first
To begin weaving chosen laurels with ivy,
Which may always grow green around your golden temples.

<summary>This page concludes an address to the Muse by acknowledging the poet's current limitations, then transitions into a dedicatory poem to Pandolfo Malatesta. The poet uses the metaphor of a humble farmer's gift to ask Pandolfo to accept his modest verses in hopes of one day writing a truly great epic.</summary>
<keywords>Pandolfo Malatesta, Aonia, Muses, poetic humility, laurel wreaths, lyric poetry, patronage</keywords>

In the cycles of the pleasing, returning
-> Year, <-
Which, by the stable Law of the gods
-> Commanding, <-
Bring back the honors <note>original: "honores." In classical poetry, the "honors" of the earth usually refer to the blossoms and greenery that return with the seasons.</note> owed to the lands

POEMS.

->Covered in dust?<-

O lyre? <note>original: "Barbite." The *barbitos* was a stringed instrument similar to a lyre, associated in antiquity with Sappho and Alcaeus, and used here as a symbol for lyric poetry.</note> It was not with that intent that you hung from bronze pegs for me, though I myself, with a clean hand, left you placed in a high part of the
->temple:<-

When, in a few verses, I asked the sweet Muse <note>original: "Camœnæ." The Camenae were Roman fountain goddesses later identified with the Greek Muses.</note> for a brief indulgence, until I might approach the rich hidden depths of the Greeks <note>original: "Dites danaum recessus." Augurello is describing his time spent studying Greek literature and philosophy, portraying himself as a "poor man" seeking the "wealth" of ancient Greek knowledge.</note>
->as a poor man.<-

From there, as I searched deeply for whatever might present itself to me, I might bring back everything I gathered to use with you once more upon my
->return.<-

Behold, then, the riches <note>original: "opes." This refers to the intellectual and stylistic gains from his Greek studies.</note> which you yourself can henceforth use happily as you wish; behold those things by which you will not need to yield to others in polished
->refinement.<-

Do not think this is said boldly or lightly; for you do not desire too much, and I have prepared, to the best of my ability, what is
->sufficient.<-

And since you conduct yourself with such modesty, you should rightly believe that it is most fitting that your work labors neither under excessive ornament nor any
->unrefined grime;<-

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: Lyre, Muses, Of the Greeks, Rich, Or more lightly.</note>

<summary>In this poem, Augurello addresses his lyre, explaining that his temporary abandonment of Latin poetry was a pilgrimage to study Greek literature. He returns now to his primary instrument, enriched by the "wealth" of Greek knowledge which he intends to incorporate into his own work.</summary>
<keywords>lyre, barbitos, Muses, Greek literature, poetic refinement, intellectual wealth, Humanism</keywords>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

# TO JULIUS ALEXANDER
# CAPELLA, AN EXHORTATION
# AGAINST LOVE.

## ODE II.

If I have learned anything from the practical experience of things, Julius <note>original: "Iule." This refers to Julius Alexander Capella, a Venetian humanist and close friend of the author.</note>, and if I judge correctly what wise men have claimed they are able to prophesy, behold, I now speak as a Prophet <note>original: "Vates." A term for a poet-prophet, implying that the author's insight is divinely inspired or deeply intuitive.</note>.

From this excessive paleness of your face, and from the constant sighing of your breast, and from your words that stumble so much, I divine that perhaps some mad anxiety <note>original: "curam." In the tradition of Latin love poetry, *cura* refers to the obsessive and often painful "care" or "worry" of being in love.</note> is now gradually creeping around your heart. By its own will, it forces you, even against your will, to live in this harsh condition.

Unless you resist this vigorously and with a brave spirit at the very beginning, you will later struggle in vain once a heaped-up mass of troubles, piled upon you, presses down more powerfully than you are able to bear; for once that fountain of anxieties has been tasted, it rushes in all the more abundantly. Yet there is no plague you must avoid more sharply than being brought under the savage law of grim Cupid <note>original: "Cupidinis." The Roman god of desire, often depicted in lyric poetry as a cruel tyrant rather than a playful child.</note>.

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: Julius Alexander Capella, Cupid, Exhortation, Ode.</note>

<summary>In this poem, the author warns his friend Julius Alexander Capella that his physical symptoms—paleness, sighing, and stuttering—betray that he is falling in love. He urges his friend to resist these feelings early before they become an overwhelming and inescapable burden.</summary>

<keywords>Julius Alexander Capella, Cupid, love-sickness, prophecy, Stoic resistance, symptoms of love</keywords>

<meta>The text on this page continues the warning to Julius Alexander Capella against the dangers of passion. <header>POEM III.</header>

**This** *is fixed deep in the internal marrow,* <note>original: "medullis." In ancient poetry, the marrow was considered the innermost seat of intense emotion and desire.</note>
**And** *clinging with its teeth,*
**It** *feeds only upon the vital limbs.*
**This** *also strives to enter the citadel of the mind,* <note>The "citadel of the mind" is a common metaphor in Stoic philosophy, representing the rational part of the soul that must be guarded against the "enemies" of irrational passion.</note>
**Which** *has been shaken by the torture of allurements*
**By** *destructive enemies.*
**It** *is necessary that you drive them from their high*
**Throne** *with strong efforts, and far*
**From** *thence, banish them to the furthest part.*
**And** *as often as the image of the wicked god* <note>original: "dei improbi." Referring to Cupid (Amor). The term "improbus" suggests he is persistent, shameless, or unrelenting in his attacks on the heart.</note>
**Appears** *before your eyes,*
**Turn** *away and restrain your sight, and*
**Lest** *his deceptive breeze*
**Hold** *you with false flatteries, beware.*
**Meanwhile,** *listen—if you have the time—to the words*
**Which** *once, while she perhaps anxiously sought her wandering*
**Son,** *Venus spoke for the sake of health.* <note>The poet here begins a Latin translation of a famous Greek idyll by Moschus titled "Eros Drapetes" (The Fugitive Love). It frames the warning against love within a charming mythological story.</note> "O, if anyone
**Has seen** *Love wandering in the*
**Crossroads,** *he is my fugitive; as a reward*
**The informant** *shall carry away a little kiss from Venus.* <note>original: "Basiolum." A tender or playful "little kiss," a term made famous by the Roman poet Catullus.</note>
**But** *if you lead him here to me,*
**I would** *not only want a little kiss for you,*
**But** *you, stranger, shall have much more.*
**The boy** *is remarkable; you may*
**Recognize** *him by his own marks. For in his body*
**He is** *not white all over, but like*
**Fire,** *whose eyes [shine with] flame..."*

<meta>This page continues the speech of Venus as she searches for her runaway son, Cupid. In the previous page, she began to warn the reader (and Julius Alexander Capella) about the boy's nature. ### BOOK II.

His eyes shine with a sharp brow, but his **mind is wicked**, and his **little words** are **sweet**; for he does not feel the same thing he speaks. His **voice is honeyed** also, but when he is kindled with anger, his **mind is wild**, **deceitful**, and a **cheater**; and the **lying boy** plays sharp games. He adorns his head with **unshorn hair**, and has a **wild mouth** and a **bold face**. He has **tiny hands**, and yet he shoots from **afar**, even as far as pale **Acheron** <note>original: "Acheronta." One of the rivers of the Underworld, here representing the furthest reaches of the realm of the dead.</note> fiercely; nor does he care to spare the **Stygian King** <note>original: "Stygio Regi." Referring to Pluto (Hades), the ruler of the dead, suggesting that even the most cold-hearted deities are subject to love.</note> himself.

He is indeed **naked in his limbs**, but his **mind is veiled**. Flying like a **bird** now, he sits in the **heart of a man**, and now in the **lap of a girl**, so that, all other things being despised, they are carried away with a **frivolous mind** and **worry**. He also has **weapons**, and he strikes with a **tiny bow** and a **small arrow**. A small arrow indeed, but it often seeks the **high heavens**, and often even **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iouem." The king of the gods, whom Cupid frequently humiliated by making him fall in love with mortals.</note> himself, who by his own divine power turns the **highest pole** and the **stars**. A **small golden quiver** hangs from his shoulders, and inside are **bitter reeds** <note>original: "Calami." Literally "reeds," the material used for arrow shafts; "bitter" refers to the pain love causes.</note>, with which he often wounds even **me, his mother**. All things are **cruel**, everything. And he is much **crueler** to himself.

### POEMS

His torch is small, yet it surpasses that
**Phoebus** <note>original: "Phœbum." A common name for Apollo as the sun god. The poet suggests that the fire of love is more powerful than the light of the sun itself.</note> through whom nearly all things shine.
Therefore, if you catch him, come, strike him,
and do not pity him; even if you should perhaps
see him weeping, take care
lest he deceive you if he smiles.
Bind him more tightly if he should wish to offer
kisses to you; flee, for they are harmful
kisses, and poisons are spread upon those very lips.
If he should finally say to you:
“Take these, for I freely bestow all these
my weapons upon you,” do not
touch anything; do not hope for these
deceptive gifts, for everything belonging to **Love** <note>original: "Amoris." Referring to Cupid, the god of desire.</note>
is tainted with burning venom.
Thus the goddess, although she was his own mother,
long lamented much while searching,
until she found him hiding and greatly
scorched by his own torch,
embracing a certain **Psyche** <note>original: "Psychen." The personification of the human soul. This refers to the famous myth of Cupid and Psyche, where the god falls in love with a mortal woman against his mother’s orders.</note>,
whom—forgetful of his mother’s commands,
namely that he should never wish
to join himself to anyone in the bond of marriage—
he had desired as a wife for himself; and now,
from that union, the father had acknowledged a daughter,
who, although she always brings
not a little distress to mortals, is nevertheless
called **Pleasure** <note>original: "uoluptas." In classical mythology, the daughter of Cupid and Psyche is named Voluptas (the Latin word for pleasure), signifying the joy that results when the soul and love are united.</note> by a resounding voice.

For how does this place help, and why do you tire your 
mind with these unworthy matters? 
You, than whom no other could climb deeper 
->into the high ether,<- <note>original: "Aethéra." In ancient science, the "ether" was the pure, fiery element of the upper heavens where the stars resided.</note>

->your great heart.<-

->you might use wisely.<-

anxious cares, verges toward 
->late life.<- <note>original: "ſerum vergit in Aeuum." The poet warns that as Schola approaches old age, he should spend his remaining time on noble pursuits rather than the "unworthy matters" of his current business.</note>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

*He, outrunning the wealthy with the riches of the powerful,*
*will alone be able to live*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*a blessed life.*

*Whether he considers himself stable for a long time, or fragile;*
*for what will a wise person ever think*
*is more precious than the moments of the trembling hour*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*at any time?*

*As soon as he decides to use more sparingly*
*the downward course of time—because among*
*the many things which are foreign to us, this alone*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*stands as our own.* <note>This reflects a famous sentiment by the Stoic philosopher Seneca: "All things, Lucilius, belong to others; only time is our own."</note>

*No capacity exists to fully repay*
*any loss of this immense thing—*
*not with wealth, nor with those Honors tossed about*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*by the Popular Breeze.* <note>original: "popularis Aura." A classical metaphor for the fickle and fleeting nature of public approval or political fame.</note>

*Therefore, do not cease to wisely*
*hasten this work plainly with me,*
*so that no point of your age escapes you*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*in any sluggish way;*

Is it perhaps for my 
Bembo <note>Pietro Bembo (1470–1547) was a towering figure of the Italian Renaissance—a cardinal, poet, and scholar who shaped the Italian language.</note> that you are preparing these 
Green garlands, sweet Pieris <note>original: "Pieri." A name for a Muse, referring to Pieria, the region near Mount Olympus where they were born.</note>,
And do you strive to adorn the 
Poet even at his very 
Cradle?
Oh, what natural talent 
He displayed from the start,
When he recently dared to touch 
His excellent father’s troubled mind 
With a song,
And moved the solid Trumpet 
Of Heroic sound to his lips. <note>This likely refers to Bembo's early mastery of epic or "heroic" verse styles in Latin, showing his precocious intellectual maturity.</note>
Or when he grieved over 
The cruel wounds of Cupid,
Long fixed within his breast,
Which would become, through his Elegies, 
An eternal pledge to a harsh Mistress; <note>A "harsh mistress" (original: "Duræ Amicæ") is a classic theme in Roman elegy, where the poet-lover laments the coldness of his beloved through verse.</note>

<header>POEMS</header>

*As soon as they have perceived so many evils*
*mixed in with meager goods,*
*and that nothing is entirely pure,*
*you would soon see everyone perhaps seeking their own*
*homes contentedly, and leaving behind whatever*
*heavy burdens are born of their own innate sickness.* <note>original: "ingenita peste" — This refers to the "internal plague" of human ambition or the restless desire for status that prevents contentment.</note>
*Happy is he who, mastering a solid rest*
*as soon as possible, has neither laid his head*
*in anxious affairs, nor, in times of peace,*
*lofted himself up more than is right,*

## EXHORTATION TO HIMSELF

### ODE IX

*Therefore, why do you delay? This path lies*
*clearly open to all immediately, as we go*
*willingly, and hasten to station ourselves*
*upon the level Plain.* <note>original: "æquo Campo" — A metaphor for a state of mental equanimity or a life of steady virtue, often contrasted with the "peaks" of high office or the "valleys" of despair.</note>
*And if any great mass should first stand in the way,*
*preventing you from appearing to enter*
*fitly just then, do not nevertheless refrain*
*from going more promptly.*
*For once you have overcome this, you will have*
*arrived there, where no other place lies*
*more level for us, nor any path*
*wider than this one.*

<summary>The text concludes a reflection on how all people, if given the choice, would prefer their own quiet lives over the burdens of power, and then introduces a personal exhortation to pursue the path of virtue and peace.</summary>

**I** was [going] by chance <note>original: "B am forte" — Likely "Ibam forte" (I was going by chance), a traditional opening for a poem of this style. The envoy of the Father, having examined

### TO MARSILIO FICINO

## ODE 12.

I was walking by chance where the Aponus <note>Aponus: The thermal springs of Abano near Padua, famous since antiquity for their healing and "boiling" properties.</note>
<margin>1</margin> rages with healthful waters, wondering anxiously
    what great fires the Nymph <note>original: "Nais" — A Naiad or water nymph, used here as a personification of the spring.</note>
conceives within her liquid breast, O Marsilio,
eternal glory of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiæ" — Referring to the Greek concept of Sophia or divine wisdom, fitting for a philosopher like Ficino.</note>,
whether she swells with living sulfur, or with niter <term>Nitro: A naturally occurring mineral form of sodium carbonate, often associated with cleansing or chemical heat in ancient science.</term>,
or if, infused with black bitumen <term>Bitumen: A viscous, tar-like form of petroleum; the poet is speculating on the geological source of the spring's heat.</term>,
    she seethes with heavy vapors,
or if from here a path carries

### BOOK II OF ODES.
