Accessible to dreadful springs,
  Alas, a grim beast <note>original: "belua" — This creature is a personification of Envy (Invidia). In Renaissance poetry, Envy is often depicted as a hybrid monster that feeds on its own vitals.</note> in a remote,
Shuddering cave suddenly
  seemed to lift itself swiftly; its hands,
Forehead, head, and feet
  were human, but upon the rest of its body
A scaly serpent was grafted,
  its back lightly covered with wandering feathers.
In its right hand were twin torches,
  one of which, as soon as it touched me,
It drove the other more savagely
  into its own hostile breast, making the flame grow thick.
"And with these eternal fires," it said,
  "I burn here; should I ever see
Those who have prospered by their merits,
  I am never joyful except at the misfortunes of the noble."
While I stood thus astonished
  and scorched, the race of men then slipped from my mind—
Those who are utterly devoted to this monster
  and look down upon all things with livid <term>Livid: From the Latin "lividis," meaning a leaden, bluish-pale color. In the ancient world, this color was physically associated with the "bile" of envy and spite.</term> eyes,
So that now I desire nothing more
  than to flee the wretched Envious ones with you;

<header>ODES</header>

Laurence, do you often tell how he meditates
with a balanced mind upon the heavy
cares of men and the Gods?

And how, amid the restless tides of
high honors, he sometimes leads away his Muse—
as she lingers on the Euganean hills <note>original: "Collibus Euganeis" — A range of hills in Northern Italy, near Padua, often associated with poetic retreat.</note>—
with nimble jests.

There, happy, he once scaled the Aonian mountains <note>original: "Aonas Montis" — Mount Helicon in Greece, the mythological home of the Muses.</note>;
and there he boldly visited the virgin choirs
and, with an open mouth, drank
the Castalian nectar <note>A reference to the Castalian Spring on Mount Parnassus, a source of poetic inspiration.</note>.

So, either with a right hand bearing greatness,
he manages the gifts of powerful Princes,
and, as one who is blessed, pursues the sweet fruits
of an honest harvest of wealth.

Or, scattering the dark clouds from his brow,
he bathes his mind in the deep fire of
song and the lyre; then, having received the skill,
he strikes the Pindaric plectrum <term>Pindaric plectrum: Referring to Pindar, the ancient Greek lyric poet known for his high, complex style. A plectrum is a small tool used to pluck a lyre.</term>.

Then, indeed, just as when a rain-storm
gathered in the high clouds is halted by the South Wind <note>original: "notho" — Notus, the god of the south wind, often associated with storms.</note>,
and soon it assails the earth and falls
thicker than countless grains of sand,

So he, stirred by the great whirlwind
of a lively breeze, is carried into the hidden
praises of Kings and Gods; and most abundantly,
he spreads them through the mouths of men,

and [protects them] from the heavy decay...

<margin>q</margin>Learn from this truth-telling Song how Virtue <note>original: "Virtus" — In this context, it refers to both moral character and the "excellence" or "valor" required for academic mastery.</note> provides nothing too difficult:

You desire that by clinging more closely to the heart of things, 
<margin>❦</margin> he who would be wise should learn

*Whatever you see here, wherever you*

*In the tight knot of the Fates.* <note>original: "Parcarum" — The Parcae were the three Fates of Roman mythology (Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos) who spun, measured, and cut the thread of human life and destiny.</note>

*Admire with the praise of all.* <note>Theodore Gaza (c. 1398–1475) was a renowned Greek scholar and humanist who fled the fall of Constantinople. ->*Of the divine mind within...*<-

<margin>M</margin> He used to say that a spirit was infused, stirring itself
throughout the massive structure from all sides; <note>This refers to the *Anima Mundi* or "World Soul," a philosophical concept that a divine spirit animates the entire physical universe.</note>
from this come the delicate lives of living things—
those beneath the deep
<margin>V</margin> marble <note>original: "marmor" — A poetic term for the smooth, glittering surface of the sea.</note>, those of the air,
and those of us who feed upon the various gifts of the earth.
Moreover, he said that from there a pure particle
of the gods themselves is given to our intellect,
by which we have wisdom;
this particle, once released from earthly limbs,
would one day fly out into the company of the stars
and reside in its own part of heaven,
simple, no longer subject to darkness
<margin>I</margin> or the prison of the body. <note>original: "corporeo... carceri" — A classic Platonic metaphor describing the physical body as a cage or prison that limits the divine soul.</note>
Thus to us, O Passeri, with a clear river
of words, he used to speak—
he who was already a citizen of heaven even before
<margin>H</margin> he cast off the human sluggishness
wrongly added to mortals; yet he continued
to dwell upon the earth itself,
a starry glory;

# THE ADDITIONAL LITTLE BOOK 
# OF IAMBICS BY 
# GIOVANNI AURELIO 
# AUGURELLO.

## TO THE MUSES: ATTRIBUTING TO THEM ALONE THE FACT THAT HE IS ABOUT TO PLAY AT SOMETHING RATHER GRAND. <note>Augurello (c. 1441–1524) was a Renaissance humanist and alchemist. Here, "playing at something grand" refers to his shift from lighter verse to more ambitious poetic or philosophical projects.</note>

### POEM I.

*Is this the Pimplaean hill?* <note>original: *Pimplæus... collis*. Pimpla was a village and fountain in Pieria, ancient Greece, sacred to the Muses. Referring to a "Pimplaean hill" signifies a place of supreme poetic inspiration.</note> *Do the sweet* 
*Muses dwell here* <note>original: *Camœnæ*. These were ancient Roman water-deities eventually identified with the Greek Muses.</note>
*Everywhere? Does Phoebus seek this place* <note>Phoebus is an epithet for Apollo, the god of music, poetry, and the sun.</note>
*Willingly?* 
*When he sets down his bow, and takes the Lyre* 
*In his hand?* 

*Who then led me here? By what path* 
*Have I, so blessed, set my foot here?* 

*Has anything more fortunate ever happened to anyone?* 
*Than to be carried beneath this evergreen Laurel, beneath this* 
*Pine, or within this myrtle grove?* <note>The laurel, pine, and myrtle were all plants sacred to various deities (Apollo, Pan, and Venus respectively) and often represented different genres or qualities of poetry.</note>
*Or to sit, a little weary, at the source* 
*Of this purely gushing water?* 

*Oh, who will quickly bring me the tuneful strings?* 
*What spirit has seized me here of its own accord?* 
*My mind, struck by a hidden frenzy, longs—* <note>original: *furore*. This refers to the "divine frenzy" or "poetic madness" (furor poeticus) which Renaissance thinkers believed was necessary for true artistic creation.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the frontispiece and opening poem for Augurello's collection of iambic verse. The poet describes finding himself in a sacred landscape dedicated to Apollo and the Muses, signaling a moment of sudden divine inspiration.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello, Muses, Apollo, Pimpla, poetic inspiration, divine frenzy, iambic poetry, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

Now refreshed; it is right to return to you alone 
All these things that I have so frequently received;

## POEM II.

O Julius the Second, most holy pontiff, 
Father long desired by all nations, 
Chosen now by the command of the highest of the heavenly beings, 
And by the favoring prayers of all the Fathers, <note>The "Fathers" (Patrum) refers to the College of Cardinals who elected him.</note>
<margin>5</margin>Now each man for himself offers congratulations 
To you, which may serve as a sign of a devoted 
Mind. The powerful leaders of willing cities, 
And the firm hearts of republics, <note>original: *Rerumque firma publicarum pectora*. This refers to the statesmen and leaders of the various Italian Republics.</note>
By sending frequent embassies, 
Pledge and joyfully dedicate their loyalty: 
As if they come forward to give nothing greater. 
But those who perhaps regulate their living with smaller means, <note>The poet is contrasting the grand diplomatic gifts of city-states with the humble "gift" of his own poetry.</note>

Everyone promises abundant effort,
of the kind that diligent virtue can provide:

## POEM III. COMPOSED OF PURE IAMBICS.

Why do so many looming stars threaten?

<meta>This page continues the poem addressed to Pope Julius II. The previous page ended with the poet noting that God did not despise the humble and asking why the stars seem so threatening. Here, the poet argues that the Pope’s divinely appointed rule will counteract any bad astrological omens and usher in a new Golden Age.</meta>

<header>IAMBIC VERSE.</header>

Is it Famine, or War, or a diseased atmosphere?
Or do we believe that all things are so malignant?
That absolutely no kindness
Of Jove <note>original: *Iouis*. Jupiter, often associated in astrology with benevolence and kingship.</note> could help, or of the Cyprian <note>original: *Cypridis*. An epithet for Venus, referring to her birth on the island of Cyprus.</note> could favor?
And will the assembly of the furthest circle <note>Refers to the *primum mobile* or the highest sphere of the heavens, thought to influence the lower spheres.</note> add nothing
Of favor? And will that supreme power of the favoring stars
Leave us in envy?
Alas, what can we little men <note>original: *homunculi*. A diminutive, humble term for humanity common in Renaissance humanism.</note> think to guard against,
When the fates have destined all these things to loom?
But the Heavens <note>original: *Polus*; literally the "Pole," but used here to mean the celestial heights or God.</note> themselves have given a pious Prince: <note>Refers to Pope Julius II.</note>
And perhaps the threatened fate will be held differently.
Under his command, things will be guided more rightly
If anything, conquered by the injustice of the times,
Had begun to slip from its former station.
Under his command, all that will be ruled more justly,
Over which he—now powerful—holds
The place of the One who moves all things by His own power. <note>A reference to the Pope as the "Vicar of Christ," representing the Prime Mover (God) on Earth.</note>
Therefore, that age will return, which previously
They tell in stories was once the Golden Age. <note>The *Aureum seculum*, a mythical era of peace and plenty under the rule of Saturn.</note>
Every good thing will flourish immediately, as soon as
Justice <note>original: *æquitas*. Equity or divine fairness.</note> has powerfully put wickedness to flight:
Nor will any uplifted malignancy of the stars <note>Inauspicious astrological alignments or "bad luck" written in the heavens.</note>
Press upon us, the pious, with so many unholy evils;

<summary>The poet argues that the arrival of Pope Julius II as a "pious Prince" will counteract the negative omens of the stars, such as war and famine. He predicts that the Pope's just rule will restore a mythical Golden Age of peace and virtue.</summary>

## SONG IV.

What is it not fitting for all you good people to hope for now
<margin>¶</margin> under the gracious Prince?
Hope that all things suited to your prayers
Are already, even now, about to come to pass.
Your virtue will clearly become more abundant.
Rewards will increase in turn.
Depravity, prostrated everywhere, will be trampled.
Punishments will follow crimes.
So that Rome, restored to her ancient customs,
May fittingly lift up her head—
The head of the world, always to be revered and supreme,
The Head of the most holy Faith.
Other Princes in every part of the world
Will follow this example.
And their citizens will immediately become
Such as the princes themselves are.
Rejoice, you who are strong in the honorable arts,
And do not spare your labor now.
An abundant reward is prepared for everyone
Under this spreading oak. <note>original: *robore*. The Latin word *robur* means both a specific type of hard oak and "strength" or "firmness."</note>
This oak once nourished mankind under another... <note>Likely an allusion to the mythological Age of Saturn, where the oak provided acorns as food, or a reference to the previous Della Rovere Pope, Sixtus IV.</note>

<summary>This poem celebrates the restoration of Rome’s moral and political supremacy under Pope Julius II, promising that his "oak" (the Della Rovere reign) will reward virtue and punish vice. The poet envisions a world where other world leaders follow the Pope's example, leading to a universal moral renewal.</summary>

May you be kind: and may Charity <note>original: *Caritas*. The highest of the theological virtues, representing selfless love.</note> come forth,

Chains, and be driven before the chariot, and forever
Be weighed down by the most wretched slavery;

<meta>This page continues the opening of Song VII, which celebrates the election of Pope Julius II. The previous page established the Venetian Senate's joy and the Pope’s role as a "Father" to the people. This page expands on the Pope's qualities as a peacemaker and a leader who transcends fate through divine devotion.</meta>

# BOOK.

Such as every best man wished to be chosen:
Who first gifts his people with shining peace,
Just as He once, whose place he fills <note>Referring to Christ, whose "vicar" or representative the Pope is traditionally considered to be.</note>,
Left peace to the lands when He ascended to the heavens.
And may he quench the heavy rages of Princes,
Trampling the wild tumult of cruel Mars <term>Mars: The Roman god of war, here used to personify war and military conflict</term>,
And cause grim Wickedness to shamefully turn its back;
While at the same time, let joyful Virtue lift her head.
And though (as they say) all things seethe with the threats
Of the Stars, at which the World now shudders and fears:
Let the fates nonetheless yield at once to this man,
If the power of a wise man is indeed stronger than fate.
For the worshipper of God prefers divine things
To all others; he adores and reveres them above all,
And afterward takes care for the affairs of mortals.
And skillful, he weighs matters with an equal scale:
He who presides over the Christian commonwealth <term>Christian commonwealth: *Christianæ publicæ rei*, referring to the collective body of Christian nations, often termed "Christendom"</term>,
With all good men now thus in agreement.
And he who, renewing the memory of the earlier Fathers,
Has recently been added as a supreme father,
Fittingly following that Great Julius <note>original: *Iulio Magno*. This refers to Pope Julius I (reigned 337–352), but also serves as a flattering comparison to Julius Caesar, emphasizing the Pope's power and the continuity of the name.</note>.
He embraces good men with just honors,
And cares for all with equal charity <note>original: *caritate*. In this theological context, it refers to *agape*, the highest form of Christian love or selfless concern for others.</note>.
He is neither weary by labor, nor does he cease
Whenever an opportunity is given to help his people:
Thinking the name of Father more fitting for himself
Than the name of Prince <term>Prince: *Princeps*, used here in the classical sense of a "first citizen" or supreme ruler</term>, though he sits as the Prince of Princes.
Nor does he think to rule for his own sake, before himself...

<sig>q ii</sig>

<vocab>Virtue, World, Prince, Julius the Great, Supreme Father, Christian, charity</vocab>

<summary>This page continues a poetic tribute to Pope Julius II, characterizing him as a divinely appointed peacemaker who quenches the rages of war and prefers the title of "Father" over "Prince." The text emphasizes his wisdom and devotion to the Christian commonwealth, suggesting his virtue is powerful enough to overcome the "threats of the stars" or fate.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the praise of Pope Julius II, moving from his role as a bringer of peace to his personal virtues and his skillful navigation of the Church—symbolized as a ship—through political storms. The previous page emphasized his election as a divine appointment to end war.</meta>

# IAMBIC VERSE.

He bears his office for all, such that he never
Speaks of himself, not even slightly, but only of his chosen flock,
To whom he was given as Pastor <term>Pastor: Latin for "shepherd," a traditional title for a bishop or pope, emphasizing their duty to care for their congregation like a flock of sheep</term> by a favoring heaven.
From this, he brings reverence—not fear—to his people,
As one who is uniquely cherished; nor is any other leader
More desired to preside, safe and for a long time.
Simple in speech, a royal ornament, truth is always
With him, and the charm of his mouth is pure.
But deceit is absent, and cunning is absent; in their
Place, Prudence <term>Prudence: One of the four cardinal virtues, involving the ability to govern oneself by the use of reason</term> alone compels these to depart:
Pushing the one away as servile, and the other as evil.
This wondrous learning cultivates all things:
A sure sense of reality and a great understanding of all matters:
When his innate power warms up, and the teaching
Of the sacred mind meanwhile stirs his strengths.
For this is indeed the gift of this lineage:
A dense nursery <note>original: *seminarium*. While it means a nursery for plants, it refers here to a "seminary" or breeding ground for great leaders.</note> from which always sprouts
A line of bishops and presiding men.
From here—lest I mention so many others at once—
He who now directs the tiller of the blessed Bark <note>original: *Ratis*. The "Bark of St. Peter" is a traditional metaphor for the Catholic Church, with the Pope acting as its navigator.</note>,
The Wise One, signaled by hereditary right
That this office was once owed to you, O Supreme Pontiff,
Whom you would take up to be ruled; and you would rescue
With a powerful right hand the boat <note>original: *cymbam*. A small boat or skiff, further emphasizing the vulnerability of the Church in times of crisis.</note> already almost stuck
Upon the rocks of a turbulent sea: and finally
Bringing it back, you would settle it for a long time in a safe harbor.
From here, one can see all people of every rank
Congratulating you everywhere, just as...

<meta>catchword: In the age</meta>

<meta>This page continues the praise of Pope Julius II, following the transition from his familial lineage to his individual fitness for the Papal throne. # BOOK.

In our age there has been no joy such as this.
But no one, I believe, is yet so glad
As the powerful Senate of the Adriatic <note>original: *Adria*. A reference to the Republic of Venice, which held significant power in the Adriatic Sea.</note> rejoices.
Who so desires a Prince <term>Prince: Used here in the Renaissance sense to mean a sovereign ruler; in this case, the Pope as the head of the Papal States.</term> for the Supreme See
To be present—just, far-sighted, serious, and supreme—
Such as your many-sided Virtue has now given you:
Since the Senate itself has always been a constant,
Brave, and sharp supporter of the Church, and will be a present
Protector evermore, out of its own devotion to God;

## IN THE SAME APPLAUSE: RESISTANCE

-> That fate can be resisted by the Prudence of the Prince, <-
-> if the stars, as the Prophets <note>original: *Vates*. This can mean poets or diviners; here it refers to those who read the stars.</note> <-
-> tell us, threaten any danger. <-

## POEM VIII.

Depart, you innermost plagues of mortals,
<margin>u</margin> Deceit and crime, the ultimate wounds of life.
Depart, all of you, to the greatest misery <note>original: *maximam in mala cruce*. Literally "to the great cross," a common Latin idiom meaning "go to the gallows" or "go to hell."</note>.
Return, Virtue and Honor, with a prosperous
Step to our hundred-fold blessed fellowship.
O, let the Prophets threaten evil no longer:
Let the malignant stars now cease their raging.
What have we committed so wickedly, O heavenly ones,
That so many evils should follow upon continuous evils?
Does your mercy <term>Clementia: The virtue of leniency or mercy, often personified as a goddess in Roman tradition and adopted as a Christian virtue.</term> no longer look upon us?
Which alone might outweigh so heavy a stain?

<meta>This page continues the celebration of the election of Pope Julius II. # IAMBIC VERSE.

And yet you have given the best hope of favor.
By this election of the supreme father <note>original: *patris... summi*. Referring to the Pope as the Holy Father.</note> being granted:
Who, while he governs, may keep far from us
Whatever things unkind fate might have been able to bring:
Just as a just shepherd cares for and protects the flock
Left to him, when the previous Leader departs.
Therefore let everyone rejoice, especially the good.
Let every cultivator of the good arts hope,
And let the cultivation of all things be increased.
For now the Prudence of such a great Prince <term>Prince: Here refers to the Pope as the temporal and spiritual sovereign.</term>
Resisting fate, will grant all things prosperously;

-> TO THE MUSES: THAT HE HAS COME WITH A SMALL <-
-> Urn to draw from their fountain <-

-> *the lowest parts of the growing* <-

### POEM IX.

<margin>1</margin> O you maidens <note>original: *puellæ*. Referring to the Muses, the goddesses of inspiration.</note>, who keep everything profane away
From your clear fountains:
And who frequently admit only pious Prophets <note>original: *Vates*. Meaning poets who are divinely inspired like oracles.</note>
To such great draughts of water,
<margin>5</margin> Worshipping, I seek the sacred Waters, if it is permitted,
Modestly with a narrow little Urn:
So that I may water with a light dew the lowest parts
Of the Oak <note>original: *Roboris*. Literally "hardwood" or "oak," specifically referencing the Pope's coat of arms.</note> growing on high.

<sig>P</sig>

<vocab>Iambic, Poem, Muses, Prudence, Waters, Oak, Little Urn</vocab>

They will wash the heights with a greater spray, 
Those who drain the very rivers themselves.

## TO THE POPE.

<margin>h</margin> For a long time now, O **Nourishing** <term>Nourishing: The Latin *Almus* implies someone who provides life-giving support or "fostering," a common title for deities or high-ranking protectors.</term> **Pontiff**, my Muse, with strings tuned in reverence, has longed to play some kind of song she has meditated upon for you. 

Although this song may perhaps be very small and hardly equal to your serious ears, yet it would not be out of place for those ears to take relief for the mind in lighthearted matters. 

Indeed, this may serve as a distraction during the changing turn of your frequent and extraordinary cares, while you yourself labor under such a great weight on behalf of our holy religion <note>original: *Relligione*. Referring to the heavy administrative and spiritual responsibilities of the Papacy.</note>.

<summary>The poet concludes his metaphorical journey to the "Oak" of the della Rovere family and begins a formal address to Pope Julius II, offering his poetry as a lighthearted relief from the heavy burdens of the Papacy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poet’s address to Pope Julius II. Following the previous page’s imagery of the "Oak" (the della Rovere family), the poet now transitions into a series of humble metaphors—the swallow, the ant, and the myth of Pandora—to describe his modest poetic offering to the grand Pontiff.</meta>

Just as a wealthy, gilded ceiling <note>original: *lacunar*. A paneled or coffered ceiling, typical of grand Roman or Renaissance palaces.</note> in the grandest houses is known never to refuse the nests which, fashioned from a bit of earth,
->**The Swallow fits beneath them,**<- <note>original: *Hirundo*. In classical poetry, the swallow is often a symbol of a humble, seasonal guest seeking the protection of a great house.</note>

So may your vast store of praises not despise the slender means of a tiny **Poet** <note>original: *Vatis*. A term for a prophet-poet, suggesting divine inspiration despite his "slender means."</note>, but rather admit these hardworking
->**Gifts of a ready voice,**<-

Which, just like the once-diligent care of an ant wandering over great heaps of grain, soon stores up a 
->**Gathered Pile**<-
for itself within a narrow, hollow bed.

So, joyful through your wide-open fields, tasting your good deeds everywhere, this voice—as best as it is able—skillfully
->**Brings this work together from every side.**<-

For this trusting voice does not direct its hope elsewhere—that hope which alone abundantly nourishes wretched mortals, and shall nourish them
->**For all time,**<-

Once she alone of all things remained clinging to the very bottom of the **Box** <note>original: *Pyxidis*. A reference to the "Jar" or "Box" of Pandora. According to the myth, when all the evils were released into the world, only Hope remained inside.</note>, while all the poison of crimes and hardships
->**Was scattered through the world.**<-

O Prometheus, always far too watchful, you who are wise enough to keep yourself far from the gift of Jove and the messenger himself, even though she be a charming girl,
->**You keep yourself at a distance.**<- <note>In Greek mythology, Prometheus (whose name means "Forethought") warned against accepting the gifts of the gods, specifically Pandora. Here, the poet compares the Pope's discernment and wisdom to that of Prometheus.</note>

<summary>The poet uses humble metaphors of a swallow and an ant to present his work to the Pope, concluding with a classical allusion to Pandora's Box and the wisdom of Prometheus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mythological allegory of Pandora’s jar. The previous page ended as Mercury (the messenger of Jove) arrived with Pandora; here, the poet describes the consequences of Epimetheus’s folly and the enduring power of Hope (Spes) to comfort humanity, even those favored by Fortune.</meta>

Lest the sequence of evils, compressed within that tiny vessel <note>original: *minimo... Vase*. Though commonly called "Pandora's Box" today, the original Greek *pithos* and the Latin *vas* refer to a large storage jar.</note>, should disturb us without end—unless that foolish brother <note>original: *imprudens... frater*. This refers to Epimetheus ("hindsight"), the brother of Prometheus ("foresight").</note> should open it with his
->**Sickly mind.**<-

O, how much the wise man has always excelled over the light and the foolish! For what would be left for us now, unless a single, soothing **Hope** <note>original: *blanda nunc spes*. In the myth, Hope was the only thing that did not escape the jar when it was opened.</note>
->**Now remains?**<-

Whether the Gods willed it so, or whether she stayed behind by chance, yet she, once set free, could have left the mortal company through
->**An open path.**<-

But now, since she in her pity delayed her journey—whether someone desires something joyful, or is held by some grim fear, or 
->**Sharp pain**<-

Is endured by anyone, she heals them all with her varied care; provided that we soon believe all things will be better for us in our
->**Present course.**<-

Indeed, even to those whose heights of kindly Fortune the gods above guard more closely, she makes her promise, although she herself can
->**Add nothing greater.**<- <note>The poet suggests that even the Pope (Julius II), who has reached the pinnacle of success, still relies on the spiritual promise of Hope.</note>

But in the end, she consoles empty labors with nothing more than her own skill, having been taught to lead the hidden end with
->**Sweet enticements.**<-

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of Pandora's jar, emphasizing that Hope remained behind to comfort humanity in the face of the world's evils. The poet uses this myth to illustrate that even the most powerful figures, like the Pope, rely on the promise of Hope to endure the labors of life.</summary>

**You carry** my little books <note>original: *libellos*. A term often used by poets to refer to their collected poems or shorter works.</note> with you, studious Agolante:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I do not

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page concludes the poet’s address to Bartholomew Agolante. While the previous page focused on the physical responsibility of transporting the "little books" to the printers in Venice, this final section shifts toward a visionary promise of poetic immortality. The poet predicts that Agolante's diligence as a guardian of the text will eventually lead him to the same sacred heights of Parnassus where the Muses dwell.</meta>

I believe you will remain a diligent guardian over these [books],
And you will take abundant care so that they do not falter in any way:
Ensuring they admit nothing that should be erased, nor leave behind anything
Which refinement, long years of study, or grace might provide.
But when you have followed me yourself in this duty,
Future generations will one day return grateful rewards to you.
For when you arrive at the place where the path now leads you,
Where the road stretches toward the peaks of the laurel-bearing <note>original: *Laurigeri*. Laurel was the plant used to crown poets and victors.</note> hill,
Here it will be permitted for you to gaze upon the secret
Caves of the Muses <term>Aonidum</term> <note>original: *Aonidum*. A name for the Muses, derived from Aonia, a region in Boeotia where they were worshipped near Mount Helicon.</note> which are first surrounded from afar by green moss <term>Musco</term>.
Then, amidst the laurel berries and thick clusters of ivy <term>Corymbos</term> <note>original: *Corymbos*. Clusters of fruit or flowers, specifically ivy, which was sacred to Bacchus and often associated with poetic inspiration.</note>
You will enter these same places while the assembly of Apollo <term>Phæbi</term> <note>original: *Phæbi*. "Phoebus," a common title for Apollo as the god of light and poetry.</note> watches.
Indeed, while you stand in wonder, one of the Sisters <note>The *Sorores* or Nine Muses.</note> will sing
Songs to you, which you once played yourself upon the reed pipes <term>Calamis</term> <note>original: *Calamis*. Simple flutes made from reeds, symbolic of pastoral or early poetry.</note>;
The honor you once gave to me, time will soon provide to you.

->F I N I S<-

<summary>The poet concludes his work by promising his assistant, Agolante, that his faithful service in publishing these poems will grant him entry into the divine company of Apollo and the Muses. It serves as both a literal instruction for the printer and a figurative "passing of the torch" to a younger poet.</summary>

It follows the poet’s visionary address to Bartholomew Agolante from the previous page, shifting from the creative and spiritual world of poetry to the practical, technical details of the book’s manufacture in Venice.</meta>

<note>original: *Omnes Quaterniones*. This is a printer’s register, a list of the lettered "signatures" used to organize the printed sheets. A "quaternion" is a technical term for a gathering of four folded sheets of paper, which creates eight leaves or sixteen pages.</note>
